    Chapter 1

    "I'm alive," Agatha snarls back. "Because there's not a damn thing in this world or the next that makes you feel the way I do."

    -- or, the many meetings between Death and the witch Agatha Harkness over the centuries.

    SALEM, 1694 Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes SALEM, MASSACHUSETTS Agatha knows the best way to avoid the stake is offering someone else to the pyre.

    “I saw her!” she declares with a dramatic flourish she enjoys a little too much. “Cavorting with the Devil himself amidst the wood-” she pauses for a moment before adding: “Naked!”

    The townsfolk cross themselves hastily, their beloved reverend pale with terror. “Thou swear’st to this, Mistress Harkness?”

    “Truly, father.” She furrows her brow, expression painted with false contrition. “I would have told thee sooner but Mistress Lane threatened me with her dark magicks. Oh father, I have feared so!” she howls, burying her face in her hands with shame.

    It conceals her smirk as the uppity little bitch screeches her innocence to a crowd that has long since determined her guilt. They remember the last bout of trials too well to ignore Agatha’s accusations.

    She glances about the town square, silently assessing her performance with a perverted glee. They stare at their former golden girl, the oh so perfect Mary Elizabeth Lane and all they can see is the picture is the scene Agatha has painted: her nubile body bare and dancing amidst the forest trees, limbs flailing free and wild. For all their preaching and prayers, they are spellbound by the notion; it titillates them, captivates them. Agatha has always trusted in the hypocrisy of her fellow man and today is no different. Except for one face, hidden amidst the edges of the crowd who is now baying for Mary Elizabeth’s blood.

    Agatha holds the woman’s gaze for a moment, her curiosity piqued. She’s adept at looking with more than her eyes but her magic fails to sense anything about the stranger. It’s as if she’s not even there. Curiosity begins to sour into suspicion but there’s little to be done now: poor scrawny Mary Elizabeth is being carted off to the town cells in preparation for a test of her purity.

    Agatha allows herself just the hint of a smirk when the little twit passes her. Mary Elizabeth obliges her beautifully, thrashing in their grasp in her attempt to claw Agatha’s eyes out. “You witch! You hateful, conniving-”

    “Listen to how she maligns me!” Agatha wails back, forcing out a few crocodile tears for good measure. “Oh, spare me thy curses, agent of darkness!”

    Mary Elizabeth makes a noise like a strangled feline and tries to throw herself at her again.

    Agatha enjoys the chaos a moment longer. Too long, perhaps: when she looks back for the stranger, there is nothing to be seen.

    When they meet again, Agatha is taking inventory of her newly gained fineries in the private bedchamber of her fine new house.

    “Quite a performance.”

    She goes still for just a moment, her magic flaring to life within her. She’s still riding high off the power gained from the demise of her old coven. It’s rare that someone catches her off guard these days.

    She eyes the stranger for a long moment, contemplating whether she ought to blast her out the damn window but her magic prickles in her chest, still unable to detect the presence of the woman. That alone tells her there is more than meets the eye here and, well, Agatha has always been quite a slave to her own perverse curiosities.

    “My finest so far,” she agrees finally, dropping the new gown to the bed between them. “It was wasted on these cretins, of course but what else can thou do?”

    “Play to worthier audiences, perhaps?” the stranger suggests. Her eyes are liquid dark and eerily unblinking. Not of this world, Agatha’s instincts insist frantically. This one is something else. Something more.

    Agatha keeps her composure with a mocking scoff. “Such things are naught to be found in these parts, I fear. Salem breeds only weak women and even weaker men.”

    The stranger’s lips twitch and then, slowly, she smiles. It is undoubtedly the most terrifying thing Agatha has ever seen and she has no clue why.

    She forces herself to keep talking, even as her voice cracks slightly: “I do hope they submit the Mistress Lane to some awful fate. Perhaps a toss in the lake or being crushed by a stone or-”

    “She was hung.” The stranger interrupts smoothly. There is no judgement in her tone, nor compassion. It is a simply stated fact, as true as the sunrise. “Perhaps a half hour hence.”

    Agatha shrugs lightly. “Pity.”

    “What did such a creature do to deserve your ire, I wonder?” the stranger prowls forward, still not quite blinking. Her features are delicately wrought, fae-like and beautiful. Though she wears the garb of a common woman, there is no hint of wear. She does not make any sound when she walks, not even the creak of the floorboards.

    Not of this world, Agatha’s magic begins to howl. Not of this world.

    She considers lying but decides against it: “Her father is the wealthiest widower for sixty miles.”

    The grieving Lane family patriarch had taken her to wife only a few days after the unfortunate ruin of his only daughter. And to think it had only taken a few sweet words and some drunken groping under her petticoats to close the deal.

    The stranger’s lips twist in another, terrible smile. She glances around the new bedchamber, gaze lingering on the bed itself. “So, this is your prize? Some silks and a marriage bed?”

    “Hardly.” Agatha snaps, stung by the implication that this is all her ambitions amount to. “But it shall do for the winter.” The master of the house is about to suffer a bout of dysfunction that will make her marriage bed a peaceful one at least, until she can muster a plan to leave the area altogether. There is no question of staying. She might have passed off the demise of her coven as tragic deaths from illness but the rumours will continue to gather every minute she spends here.

    The stranger goes quiet, stroking her fingertips along the bedlinens; Agatha watches the movement, inexplicably captivated. The stranger catches her eye, as if she can hear the thought right out of Agatha’s head.

    She hates being caught out, hates surrendering the upper hand. She goes on the offensive.

    “And what of thee?” Agatha points out coolly.

    “Me?” The stranger looks up at her from beneath long, black lashes.

    “Does thou have a name?” Agatha has the distinct sense that she’s being toyed with, and she hates it. “Or should I name you trespasser?”

    This seems to amuse the stranger greatly: her laugh is more cackle than giggle, sharp like broken glass. Agatha likes it despite her suspicions.

    “Trespass? There is no corner of the earth barred to me.” The stranger finally says cryptically. “But perhaps you could call me Mary, after your latest victim.”

    Agatha stiffens slightly because the way she says ‘latest’ is laced with ominous knowing. The faces of her old coven flash through her mind for less than a second.

    “What business has thou here then, Mary?” she nearly snarls, hands beginning to curl.

    ‘Mary’ is unfazed. “I was looking for you, of course.”

    “And you have found me.” Agatha summons her audacity, steps forward until her body is only inches from this stranger who wears mortal garb like a costume, like an afterthought. “So what do you want with me?”

    Mary says nothing at first but there is something faintly hungry in those liquid dark eyes. Agatha refuses to blink first but the closer she peers into the stranger’s gaze, the less control she has over her shaking hands, her unsteady breaths.

    “Clarity.” She finally replies, tilting her head. “Perhaps I’ll find it next we meet, Agatha Harkness.”

    Her magic reacts before the words have slipped free of the stranger’s mouth: the violet lightning crackles in pulsing wreaths around her, striking out viciously but there is no target for the blows.

    Between one heartbeat and the next, Agatha is left alone.

    Chapter End Notes History check: The Salem Witch Trials took place between 1692 and 1693. Over two hundred people were accused and nineteen were executed by hanging like poor Mary Elizabeth Lane. Other tests for the detection of witches popular in this time included throwing them in lakes to see if they would float or sink and crushing them beneath stones which was how accused witch Giles Corey died in the 1692 trials.

    NEW LONDON, 1698 Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes NEW LONDON, CONNETICUT New London is nothing like its namesake.

    The timber and thatch buildings of the seaport town are cramped close together like orphans huddled for warmth, the main thoroughfare little more than a dirt road packed with the filth of its inhabitants. To the south of the settlement, the waterfront docks teem with ships bound for trade across the Atlantic, the air rich with the smell of fish and tar. Amidst the warehouses and supply sheds are a loose sprinkling of old taverns where coin might be made or spent in quick succession over games of card or drinking tankards of ale that tastes like piss.

    The inhabitants here live in each other’s pockets but resentfully so; there is always some new feud or grievance being aired in the street or settled with fists by the docks.

    Needless to say, Agatha has greatly enjoyed her time here.

    Chaos is easily sewn amongst such spiteful soil and her months here have proven lucrative indeed as she takes full advantage of the petty affairs of mortals. She’s come to like New London. So much so that when it burns, she feels the faintest twinge of regret.

    It’s not as if she was trying to reduce the whole town to ash. All she wanted was a little distraction and the home of the local warden seemed perfect. The pathetically self-righteous man has been trying to clean up the squalor and spite Agatha has been thriving on. But thatched rooves catch like tinder and before she knows it, the citizens of New London are struggling to pass buckets of water up from the docks amidst the wailing of the newly homeless and poor.

    Even with the city ablaze, her plan works flawlessly. Agatha waits until the attention is turned wholeheartedly to efforts to break the fire’s path by demolishing the schoolhouse and then makes a beeline for the home of Lucinda Smith-Fell, a wealthy local spinster who has successfully outlived all her male relatives’ attempts to marry her off.

    Agatha respects the other witch’s efforts. Not enough to stop her from rifling through her closets but still.

    The truth is, Agatha was drawn here by the rumour that Old Lady Fell is in possession of a powerful banishing charm. Now all she has to do is find the damn thing before the witch comes back from her attempts to save the town from the blaze. How the stupid fool plans to help with the power of divination, Agatha couldn’t say. Maybe she’ll throw some knuckle bones into the flames and ramble some nonsense about an upcoming journey.

    She hunts with more than her hands: her magic fills the room, searching for hidden chests or crevices and finding nothing but a few dusty grimoires and a cauldron caked in some long forgotten tonic. There is nothing to be found it seems, though perhaps she should’ve expected a banishing charm to be well concealed.

    Agatha grits her teeth, taking stock of the ransacked house. Her hand slips to the brooch pinned to the inside of her skirt pocket, her thumb rubbing over the cameo carving as she thinks.

    She’s about to start flipping the rickety wooden furniture over in frustration when a sudden and deeply unsettling voice speaks from behind her.

    “Planning for another wedding, Agatha?”

    “Mary Elizabeth.” Agatha replies flatly. “What a pleasure.”

    The years have taken nothing from either of them. The stranger’s beauty is still fresh and sharp, her dark hair spilling over her shoulder like raven wings. Agatha wouldn’t be surprised if she had not blinked since they’d last met, four years past.

    “Is it?” ‘Mary’ wonders, watching Agatha return to her searching. There’s only so long to look and leaving without the charm is out of the question. “Not particularly.” Agatha snarls, her desperation making her terse and fearless.

    The stranger is unfazed by her tension, simply takes up a position lounging on Old Lady Fell’s sagging straw mattress so she can watch Agatha pace the room like a captured beast. Agatha has the distinct sense that the stranger is enjoying herself which only frustrates her further.

    She snarls wordlessly and a tremble goes through the house, rising from the very foundations.

    “Outbursts like that will only attract their notice, you know.”

    Agatha glowers at the stranger. “What?”

    ‘Mary’s nose wrinkles with distaste. “That hive of festering witchmeat chasing your scent. That’s what the charm is for, isn’t it?”

    She doesn’t like that ‘Mary’ knows about the seven Salemite spawns who have taken to hunting her across New England. She doesn’t like that ‘Mary’ has reappeared like this, not when Agatha has spent years cultivating her theories on her identity.

    Not of this world, her magic murmurs warily.

    “What does thou know of them?” Agatha demands suspiciously. “Art thou here on their behalf?”

    ‘Mary’ hisses and it takes Agatha a moment to recognise it for a laugh. A snicker, of sorts. “I am no errand girl, Agatha. I come and go as I please.” There’s a certain confidence behind the words, a kind of power that Agatha finds terrible attractive even under these tense circumstances.

    It gives her an idea. Maybe a few ideas.

    Agatha smiles at the stranger coyly, abandoning her hasty searching. “It must be a fine existence,” she purrs as she kneels beside the bed, watching the stranger watch her with hawk like focus. “Living as the mistress of thy own destiny. I have only ever wanted the same for myself.”

    “Oh?” ‘Mary’ is very, very still but that hunger is back in her midnight eyes. Agatha has remembered it perfectly, even after so many years. It is still electrifying.

    “Should I find this charm,” Agatha continues, demurely dropping her eyes to her hands as she traces runes into the bedlinens between them. “Perhaps I might finally succeed. Oh, Mary-”

    “Vida.” The stranger intercuts simply. “Call me Vida.”

    The name suits her better but Agatha has no doubt it is as changeable as her garb, as the laced stays that bind her narrow waist into a perfectly cinched hourglass.

    Agatha leans a little closer, allowing that electricity to crackle between them. “Vida,” she murmurs sweetly. “Wouldst thou help me find it?”

    Vida says nothing, moves nothing, not even her breath. In fact, Agatha realises, she has not breathed at all. Another piece of the puzzle falls into place around her, bringing her tantalisingly closer to the truth-

    “Thieves!”

    The door to the bedchamber swings open, filled with the plump and haggard figure of Old Lady Fell, her hands swarming with weak blue light. Agatha throws herself backwards just in time to avoid the blast but Vida is not so fortunate; her body is thrown from the bed, smashing into the wardrobe on the opposite side of the room with such force that the wood splinters.

    Agatha admits, she didn’t think the Fell witch would have quite so much power left.

    “How dare you-!” the old woman howls, raising her hands again but this time Agatha is more than ready.

    She accepts the strike head on, the initial blow more painful than it ought to be, coming from an ancient hag of a witch like Fell. And at first, it’s agony to bear the flow of power as it burrows through her, intent on tearing her to pieces. But Agatha sense when the tide turns, when the flow is reluctantly reversed. She opens that hidden cavity within herself, the one that made her mother recoil at the sight of her, the one from which her own violet power explodes forth.

    Fell’s pale blue magic is no match for it and before she can realise what a mistake she has made, Agatha is swallowing it all, chewing through her magic with a rabid, vicious hunger. She is never satiated, her body is never satisfied with what it contains. She wants more and more and more and it never ends-

    Fell collapses to the floorboards, a dried husk of a woman as Agatha savours her newly gained power.

    “Oh,” she finds herself sighing with delight. “Thou art delirium embodied, Mistress Fell, truly!”

    Honestly, she has almost entirely forgotten Vida. When the stranger rises from the splintered remains of the wardrobe, Agatha struggles to recapture her composure, still heady with the flow of newly stolen magic.

    “Vida-”

    “There she is.” The stranger says cryptically but her expression is lively for the first time, her petite frame almost vibrating with some unknown fervour. “The coven slayer.”

    The words land like physical blows. Agatha tenses and leans backward as Vida prowls close. “I know not of what you speak-”

    Vida silences her, pressing a thumb against her bottom lip with undue pressure, as if she wants to bruise the imprint of it into the flesh. This is the first they’ve touched, Agatha thinks distantly but there is something so terribly familiar about Vida’s touch against her flesh, something that makes her ache and tremble.

    “I knew you were in there somewhere.” Vida confides with those sparkling midnight eyes of hers. “And look, you’ve left me a gift too.”

    “A gift.” Agatha echoes the word and her tongue brushes ever so slightly against Vida’s skin. She’s still high on the thrill of Fell’s stolen power but she’s still so hungry.

    “You know, a girl could get used to this, Agatha.” Vida hums with mocking. She retracts her hand, inspecting the corpse instead. Agatha tries not to take it personally but surely she’s a lot more interesting than an old dead biddy? Vida pokes at Fell’s withered cheek thoughtfully and then rises, studying Agatha for a long moment. And then, with a flourish, she presents a tiny wooden trinket on her open palm.

    “Surprise,” she grins around the words, the smile almost playful if it hadn’t also been so terrifying. “My lady.”

    The banishing charm. Agatha swallows her desperation, keeps her handy steady as she accepts it.

    Vida strikes as quick as a rattlesnake; she tugs Agatha sharply forward by the wrist and wastes no time pressing their mouths together.

    Lightning races through Agatha’s veins with every swipe of Vida’s tongue, with her nip of her teeth against Agatha’s tender lip. This feels less like a kiss and more like the way Agatha imagines her victims must feel when she reverse the flow of power between them: like Vida is stealing from her and Agatha can only cling to her, helpless to do other than savour this terrible, electrifying moment-

    Vida isn’t even breathing hard when she breaks off the embrace, hissing words into Agatha’s ear: “Til next time, my sweet.”

    Then she’s gone. Agatha can scarcely catch her own breath, her hand is clenched so tight around the banishing charm that she might be bruising the flesh, she’s almost certain she’s just been seduced by some kind of reaper and Vida is-

    Gone. Again.

    Agatha pauses, looks down at the charm and sighs. Well, at least she took the corpse of Old Lady Fell with her.

    Chapter End Notes History check: New London did suffer a serious fire in 1698 which resulted in serious property damage owing to the proximity of thatched rooves in the city's centre. With no dedicated firefighting force, citizens were forced to carry pails of water from the harbour in an attempt to extinguish the flames.

    BOSTON, 1721 Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes BOSTON, MASSACHUSETTS “Oh, God bless you, Mistress Harris!” The washerwoman positively wails her thanks, reaching for Agatha with rough hands.

    “Yes, yes,” she says impatiently, extricating herself with a forced smile. “Just remember, you have to finish the whole bottle before he’s healed, don't come back to me before then, understand?”

    “I shall, I shall remember, I swear it!” she insists, holding the glass bottle of pale liquid to her chest as if it contains all the riches of the world.

    Agatha keeps up the smile until the woman has scuttled beyond the apothecary doors, racing home to that sick husband of hers. Or was it a father? Not that it matters. The potion will do the health of a father as much good as a husband, which is to say naught at all. Smallpox is a dastardly illness at the best of times and Agatha isn’t the kind of witch prone to brewing cure-all potions for mortal peasants.

    But it costs her little to mix half a bottle of weak rum with some pain relief herbs that will give the impression of improvement for a brief time at least. While what she bestowed on the washerwoman won’t cure her beloved, Agatha is a firm believer that it is better to die addled than aching and oozing from pus-filled sores.

    She checks behind the counter and counts out at least two dozen more such bottles of rum, the abandoned cargo of an Atlantic trader who succumbed early to the epidemic as it swept through Boston. There’s plenty to last until the end of winter when it will become abundantly clear to her customers that Agatha Harris’ Miracle Cure is nothing but a bottle of delicious deception.

    Boston has been good to her this past year certainly, just big enough to conceal her activities and presence from the Salem Seven who have searched fruitlessly for her since the demise of their wretched mothers. But she can sense the power in the Fell banishing charm beginning to fade after so many years which makes it the perfect time to move on. Perhaps she’ll head further south this time. There is talk of a burgeoning city down there which has captured her imagination; she’s made a tidy sum from her Miracle Cures that could allow for a comfortable life in a big, anonymous city.

    Agatha is so distracted with tallying her ill-gotten gains that she almost doesn’t hear the knock.

    At least, not with her ears.

    Her magic, on the other hand, can sense the precise moment Vida’s daintily gloved hand makes contact with the door frame.

    “Hello again.”

    Agatha slows, allows herself a moment to drink in the sight of the stranger in the doorway: raven dark hair carefully pinned back to expose her features in all her delicate, fae beauty, her pointed chin tipped up slightly as though challenging Agatha to inspect her further while those liquid dark eyes study her right back. Her gown is cut to perfection, the rich green brocade accentuating every curve and hollow. She wears this façade well, a woman of means and finery.

    But even now, Agatha cannot detect a single breath from her; her smooth décolletage does not move an inch as Vida steps inside the shop.

    Agatha has had decades to think of this moment, she has planned for their next meeting carefully. But she had perhaps underestimated how the heady memory of Vida’s lips on her own would fill the room between them, as palpable as the dusty wooden shelves of medicinal tonics and supplies, as the creaking floorboards that make no noise beneath her dainty feet.

    “What?” Vida finally teases. “No welcome home kiss?”

    Something in her tone steadies Agatha’s stomach: she is not the only one handling memories, it seems.

    “It has been a while.” She says instead, leaning against the counter while Vida studies her surrounds with open curiosity. “I fear my humble abode is unready for such prestigious company.”

    That catches her attention. “Oh? And who might be this guest of honour, Agatha?”

    Agatha doesn’t back down from the challenge in her voice. “It is hardly every day that Death comes to call.”

    Vida’s smile is slow and toxic as a nightshade elixir. “You figured it out. Clever girl.”

    Agatha stifles the little part of her that preens under the naked flattery and presses on: “I would wonder what draws you here, my lady? To the dwelling of a simple witch such as myself-”

    “There’s nothing simple about you, Agatha.” Vida suddenly croons, close enough now to toy with the front laces on Agatha’s gown. “Let’s not pretend otherwise.”

    “You flatter me.” Agatha keeps her voice steady, eyes drawn to the stranger’s cool, nimble fingers. Because Vida or whatever she calls herself these days remains a stranger to her, a portentous creature of another world who has taken interest without explanation.

    Another languid smile. “I try.”

    “You have not answered my question.”

    “I’m not in the habit of answering to anyone.” Vida tugs on the laces with one hooked finger, traces the sharp edge of her fingernail along the sensitive skin at Agatha’s neckline.

    She struggles to keep her focus. “Won’t you tell me why you’ve come?”

    Vida hums thoughtfully. “The season here suits me. I think I’ll stay a while, collect my dues.”

    “Your bodies, you mean.” Agatha’s voice is strangled despite her efforts, but it is admittedly impossible to focus when Vida has her pinned under her gaze like a butterfly specimen.

    “Those too.” She sweeps her free hand around Agatha’s waist, tugging them even closer together until they are pressed to one another, until Agatha is certain Vida can feel her racing heartbeat between them-

    She can’t be sure who moves quicker but it is the work of moments to bring her mouth to Vida’s. And just like that, her plans to capture and interrogate this strange creature, her resolve to maintain her clarity and her advantage: all of it is abandoned.

    Agatha can’t say she particularly cares. Vida tastes like the first mists disintegrating at sunrise, like the ashes of a forest fire, like the sweet surrender of hemlock. It is intoxicating, it is unworldly, it is terrifying. Agatha takes her fill, greedy in the same way she is in most things.

    Her hands dance circuits up and down Vida’s narrow waist, fingers pressing hard against the strict boning of her gown with lustful intent. She wants to tear this pretty rich garb to pieces, wants to see if she can elicit some kind of shuddering breath from Vida-

    “God’s wounds, what is the meaning of this profanity?! Sinful whores-”

    “Oh, fuck off.” Vida snarls, breaking away from Agatha to cast a dark glower over her shoulder at the customer who has interrupted them with wide eyed scorn.

    His unshaven face purples with rage but Agatha has never had less desire to hear a man speak than right now and her magic evidently agrees. With a single flick of her wrist, her hex is cast: the man’s eyes roll back into his head and he drops first to his knees, then face-first to the unrelenting floor. Agatha listens to the cartilage in his nose crunching with wicked delight, but it is the cackle unleashed by the stranger in her arms that sends thrills down her spine.

    “Another gift, my sweet?” Vida croons with a crooked smirk. “You shouldn’t have.”

    “If it pleases you, my lady.” Agatha demurs in a way she rarely offers with any sincerity. But there is something about Vida, something that makes her ache to please her, to win her praise.

    Vida extricates herself to inspect the body, then glances up at Agatha through her lashes coyly. “There is no rest for the wicked after all.”

    Agatha’s brow furrows immediately. “Now? Must you really go?”

    “It seems I have work to do.”

    Agatha tries to keep the plaintive note from her tone. “Won’t you tell me why you’ve come at all?”

    “Send me another gift, Agatha Harkness.” The stranger known as Vida murmurs. “Perhaps I’ll answer you.”

    The May showers of spring soon give way to a humid, festering heat that transforms the smallpox outbreak into a full blown epidemic. Boston overflows with the sick and dying: bodies begin to pile into hastily dug graces, the mortals desperately nail shut the doors of those forsaken households in a vain attempt to contain the spread, the city’s preachers prattle on from their pulpit about the divine punishment being inflicted on their population.

    All this to say, business is booming: Agatha sells through an entire crate of her Miracle Cure in a single week, much to her delight.

    But the greatest joy comes from those nights she tumbles into her bedchamber above the apothecary to find Vida already waiting between the sheets.

    They spend hours together amidst tallow candles and moonlight, exploring each other in perfect, clandestine detail. Over those sweltering summer months, Agatha begins to recognise the lines and curves of Vida’s body even more thoroughly than her own; Vida learns precisely how to drive Agatha into a shuddering, gasping mess with just a crook of her fingers.

    Ever the dedicated student, Agatha memorises the choir of moans, sighs and snarls she elicits from Vida’s body with unabashed glee.

    She has taken Death as her lover and it is wickedly, terrifically, thrilling. It emboldens her, stokes her audacity. When Vida begins to show herself in the daylight hours some time at the end of July, Agatha leaps at the opportunity.

    She asks about greencraft, about the next world, about spirits and ghouls and the veil between life and death. Vida indulges her, seemingly enjoying the attention Agatha showers her with. Her curiosity is as greedy as the rest of her; her pocket grimoire is soon ink-drenched with her scribbles, every answer Vida has ever let slip to her committed to its pages.

    All but the answer she craves most especially.

    “Will you tell me now?” Agatha purrs one night as she kneels before Vida. Like a supplicant, like a devotee, clad in nothing but moonlight from the open window.

    Vida snarls at her teasing. “Put your tongue back to work, Agatha. Now.”

    “Won’t you confide in me?” Agatha continues, her fingers nimble and slick. “What brings you here, my love?”

    The only moments Vida seems to breathe are when she’s summoning her patience to deal with Agatha’s dramatics. “What does it matter now?”

    Agatha pouts. “I want to know. Won’t you tell me?”

    Vida seethes, her tone at odds with the gentle hand she runs through Agatha’s hair. “It’s good to want things, Agatha. But it’s better to follow your mistress’ commands.”

    Her mistress, her lady, her love. Agatha has taken many things that do not belong to her over the years but this might be the most precious, the most dangerous.

    “As you wish…” Agatha pauses, then smiles with wicked intent. “Vida.”

    There is something about this name that drives the stranger (her stranger) to distraction. Her body positively shakes at the sound of it, even before Agatha returns to her ministrations.

    Every week that passes, Agatha becomes more infatuated, more desirous.

    There is no end to her desire for Vida's thoughts, her voice, her body, her secrets. And even more thrilling is the way Vida seems to reciprocate that need. Death herself wants to know everything: she wants Agatha to spill her inner thoughts with utter abandon, she wants to crack her open and inspect the labrynthian patterns of her soul.

    It is more than lust, Agatha realises. It is hunger, it is obsession. It is everything Agatha has been taught to despise about herself, reflected in Vida's liquid gaze. Her dark mirror, her twin flame.

    Agatha spends long midnight hourse holding the sleeping, breathless Vida to her chest, contemplating whether she has ever felt so understood.

    It all comes crashing down one autumnal evening.

    Agatha has just returned from the physician’s ward, having successfully sold Miracle Cure to half a dozen boil-covered pox victims. The weight of newly swindled coin in her pocket gives her a jaunty step as she plans all the many ways she’ll apply her good mood to Vida, but it is not her fae-

    faced lover waiting for her at the door to the apothecary.

    “Mistress Harris,” the woman moans weakly, her face creased with exhaustion as she leans limply against the door. “Mistress Harris, please, you must help him.”

    Agatha frowns at her a moment before the recognition hits her: the washerwoman from all those months ago. How tiresome. All the same, Agatha forces a benevolent smile. “Oh, my dear, I did tell you, didn’t I? You’ll need to consume the whole bottle-”

    The washerwoman says nothing but only opens her cloak slightly: a trio of familiar glass bottles topple to the ground with empty clinks!

    “Ah.” Agatha pauses, brows raised. “Well, that doesn’t bode well.”

    “Please, Mistress Harris,” the washerwoman’s eyes are wide and jaundiced, she lumbers forward with the balance of a drunken sailor. “Please, ma’am, help him, save him.”

    Agatha is about to launch into her regular spiel about the Cure’s potency over time (how it can only heal when administered correctly and, well, it is hardly Agatha’s fault if the daft woman has been over-drinking her fill of herb-laced rum, is it?) but the washerwoman has evidently used the last of her strength to journey here.

    She staggers once and then sinks to her knees with less sound than the bottles had made, gaze dimming right before Agatha’s eyes.

    And from her arms tumbles not a load of linens as Agatha had thought but the frail, swaddled body of a little boy.

    She hardly thinks, only reaches for him before he can be smothered by his mother’s corpse. Agatha cannot believe how light he is, like a baby bird with hollow bones. Perhaps four or five years of age, he breathes but with a terrible wheeze that makes every inhale a trial, his dark eyes glossy and feverish. Every inch of his tiny body is sallow and jaundiced, covered in angry red and yellow pustules that must itch terribly, but the child is evidently unable to even muster the energy to scratch at them. He only gazes up at Agatha without comprehension, without emotion. A near-

    hollowed shell with only the faintest hint of soul left to suffer within. She has never been one to coddle children or coo over babes. Sparing the spawn of her treacherous Salemite coven had been a grave error after all. And yet.

    She takes him inside, upstairs where she can clean him in the basin of fresh water, where she can sit by the window and cool his brow in the sea breeze as it rises to meet them. She holds him against her chest, wills her magic to ease his stifled lungs even though it was never intended for such tenderness; hers is the power to obliterate or control, not comfort, not heal.

    Agatha tells herself this is hardly her own fault. His stupid mother has evidently fed him three bottles of her ridiculous Miracle Cure; three bottles of rum and a dosage of herbs never intended for a child so young. How was she to know? This is a clear case of customer misunderstanding.

    The thoughts are no comfort to her. Not while she cradles his limp, agonised body in her lap. His skin is clammy, his eyes yellowed. His liver has been poisoned beyond healing. She does not even know his name to soothe him.

    When Vida comes, there is none of her usual delight. She appears in the space between heartbeats, gently reaching her hand for Agatha’s.

    “You can’t save him, Agatha.”

    “You can.” Agatha begs and to her humiliation, she can feel the sting of tears in her eyes. “You can save him, can’t you?”

    Vida doesn’t answer, but brushes the hair from the boy’s face. “He suffers. It is time for him to stop.”

    “No, no,” Agatha insists. “No, please, my love. I- I did this. I didn’t…I didn’t mean to. He’s only a babe, please.”

    Her stranger doesn’t meet her eyes right away. “This is why I came, Agatha. It’s my job.”

    “This?” Agatha echoes, bewildered. “This is- but why? What makes him…

    why…?”

    “I told you,” Vida still won’t look at her. “I take special care to collect those gifts you send to me.”

    Agatha’s stomach lurches. “Those I…You mean those I kill?”

    Finally – finally – she catches her eye but her gaze is no midnight sky but a void, an emptiness wrought from obliteration. “He is your gift to me, Agatha. Let me take him.”

    Agatha’s grip on the boy tightens unconsciously. “I did not send him to you. Not like this.”

    “You did not mean to.” Vida hums in agreement. “But I must take him all the same.”

    Agatha has not paid much mind to those fools who have accepted her Miracle Cure in Boston, nor those who have crossed her path in all the years previous. They have been a calculated cost, souls who would have perished either way, with or without her interference. She has profited and survived as a witch must, at any cost.

    But watching Vida lift this shell of a child into her arms, Agatha cannot think it a cost worth paying.

    “I didn’t mean to kill him.” Agatha can only whisper. The words are a familiar song she has sung before. I didn’t mean to. I don’t know how to control it. Please mother, please help me.

    “I will take him gently, Agatha.” There is a hint of something like regret in Vida’s eyes as the babe draws his last, haggard breaths. “But he will not be the last, my love.”

    And that is the last time Agatha sees Death for nearly thirty years.

    Chapter End Notes History check: The Boston Smallpox Epidemic in 1721 was one of the most devastating disease outbreaks in early American history. Highly contagious, the disease rapidly in Boston's dense, tightly packed settlement, causing widespread fear and death, with an estimated 6,000 of Boston’s 11,000 residents infected and 844 people ultimately dying from the disease.

    DOVER, 1754 DOVER, NEW HAMPSHIRE Nicholas Scratch is the best thing Agatha has ever done in all her life.

    She watches him toddle around their campsite collecting river stones and leaves and discarded feathers. He brings each of them back to her for her consideration. Agatha is careful to inspect each new offering. Nicholas solemnly awaits her approval before continuing on his hunt.

    Her son is four years of age and while he is not a particularly talkative child, he doesn’t need to be. The pair of them are a coven of two, a family so closely synchronised that Agatha could sense his needs even as a babe. He is gentle in ways Agatha is constantly confounded by and curious in ways that make her unbelievably proud.

    He pauses for a moment near the creek to inspect a cluster of frogspawn, captivated by the sight of the first tadpoles beginning to emerge.

    Agatha is engrossed with the sight of him, with the sheer miracle of him. But not so distracted she does not notice immediately when Death takes a seat beside her.

    “No.”

    “Agatha-”

    “No.” Agatha glowers at Vida, her heartbeat suddenly thudding loud in her ears. “You told me I had time, you said-”

    Vida only tilts her head, pityingly. “You do. I am not here for him, my love.”

    Agatha has no reason not to trust her word but her tension does not ease. She does not think she could trust another soul, living or dead, with her son’s safety.

    “Why are you here then?”

    “Why else?” Vida replies cryptically, studying Nicholas from afar. She has abandoned the mortal garb she once used to conceal herself in Boston, wrapped only in a long, embroidered cloak fashioned from dark green silk. If Agatha looks closely, she can detect patterns in the stitching, icons and symbols that make little sense to her: cups and brooms, faces contorted in screams, a dandelion seed.

    “He’s growing quickly.”

    “Too quickly.” Agatha agrees warily. “But he’s strong. Stronger than you.” Vida sighs. “I told you, Agatha. I am not here for him. Not yet.”

    That word, yet, makes the violet of her magic curdle and crackle inside her. “You do not need him at all.” Agatha pleads. “Have I not sent you others? Better, worthier gifts than a simple child?”

    Vida’s lips twitch. “Indeed, you’ve sent me so many witch souls I can hardly count them.”

    Agatha throws caution to the wind. “Because I love you, Vida.” She insists, taking Death’s hand in her own. “Because I miss you. Because I cannot live like this, not knowing when you will come for us.”

    “All mortals live that way. It is simply the natural order.”

    Agatha pulls Vida’s hand against her cheek, presses her lips into Death’s palm. Her heart bleeds with the memories of their time together. “Please, my love. I’ll give them all to you, I swear it. Only spare him. For me.”

    Vida gives a low sigh, brushing her lips to Agatha’s forehead as if in blessing. “It is not his time yet, Agatha. That is all I can give you.”

    “He’s mine, Vida.” Agatha keens, her dignity abandoned, her pride dashed. “He’s the only thing that has ever been mine.”

    “Not the only thing.” Vida refuses in a soft voice.

    “Mama?”

    Agatha startles, her beautiful boy peering curiously at the newcomer as he tenderly cups the frogspawn in his pudgy little hands. “Nicholas, oh, what do you-”

    “Hello, Nicholas.” Death interjects sweetly. “My name is Vida.”

    His eyes are the same as Agatha’s, grey-blue like an ocean tide but they lack the gleam of his mother’s cunning. She has never hoped he would inherit her wickedness except in this moment. If he were more deceitful, perhaps he could outwit his own Death as she sits before him.

    “Hello, Vida.”

    Death raises her brows. “What have you there?”

    As it is, Nicholas only holds up his cupped hands for her perusal. “Frogs.” “I see.” Vida hums. “You took them from the river?”

    “Yes.” He pauses, thoughtfully. “Is that bad?”

    Vida doesn’t answer directly. “Were you gentle?”

    Nicholas considers this and then nods. “Think so.”

    “That’s good.” Vida says. “You’re a kind boy, Nicholas.”

    The child’s smile is a slow, warm thing at the praise. “Should I put them back?”

    “Go on, dear heart.” Agatha interrupts in a warm voice, shooing him back to the creek. “I’ll watch.”

    Walk, walk, walk the road. Nicholas hums to himself as he goes, a familiar little made-up ditty that seems to have caught his ear.

    Vida and Agatha sit in silence together, watching him.

    “I never thought I would be a mother.” Agatha admits. “I thought…well, it isn’t as though I have a particularly good example to follow.”

    She can sense Vida’s attentive gaze, the way she hangs on the words that follow. Agatha has kept very few secrets from her lover of the years but the scars left by Evanora are one of them. But she would gladly offer it up on behalf of her boy, would split every old wound open once more if it could save him from his fate.

    “She saw the wickedness in me from the beginning, you see. I was only a little older than Nicholas when she first caught me siphoning magic. It was innocent, I swear. I had no clue what I was doing. But she would not listen.” Agatha’s breath hitches. “She made me stand on the coals of the hearth and refused to let the other heal me for three days.”

    Vida says nothing but her hand tentatively seeks Agatha’s.

    “I tried to follow her rules, I did everything I could to ignore what lived inside me but the further I buried it, the less control I had. That was the real agony, feeling as if I might fly apart at any moment. It did not seem to matter how she punished me. Lashes to the spine, boiling water poured on my hands. When I could bear it no longer, I went searching for my own answers, even though it led me in to magicks that were forbidden in my coven.”

    “Dark magic,” Vida concludes, half to herself. “I always wondered what led you to it.”

    Agatha twitches her fingers, summoning an arch of pristine violet light. “I didn’t understand it was Dark at first. All I knew is that this space inside of me, this absence, finally made sense. All my hunger had a purpose. I swore to myself I would use it for good, that I would devise a way to be the good daughter my mother wanted. But my mother saw my talents only as a weapon, my search only as a betrayal.”

    Please, Mother, I can be good! All these years later, Agatha can still hear her mother’s reply: No, you cannot.

    “She saw nothing but the worst in me every day of my life, right up to the end. I worried for a time that I would do the same to my own blood.”

    Agatha continues. “That I would look on them and feel only disgust. But with Nicky… there is only light in him. There is no hint of malice nor spite. He was born laughing, Vida. I could do naught but love him with all my heart.”

    “I can see that, my love.” Vida says quietly. “As does he.”

    “Then you have to understand,” Agatha pleads. “Why I need him. Why I must keep him.”

    Vida only looks back, utterly still in her remorse. “All roads lead to the same end, Agatha. Even for him.”

    “I meant what I said.” She insists, her voice trembling. “If you take him, I will hate you forever.”

    Vida sighs. “Forever is a long time, my sweet.”

    “Please.” Agatha clings to her cloak, fingers made talons in her desperation. “Please, Vida, please-”

    Vida presses another kiss into Agatha’s forehead, accepting every plea without a hint of joy or satisfaction. She can feel Agatha trembling against her and there is nothing she wants more in this moment than to assure her, comfort her, to kiss these tears away and embrace this witch who holds the dead withered heart of her in her hands.

    “Time is all I can offer you.” She whispers the words against her flesh.

    “And a gentle hand is all I can offer him.”

    Dover, New Hampshire When Death comes for Nicholas Scratch, she wears a face that is ever so slightly familiar to him.

    It is a face that has lingered at the edges of his memories. He has glimpsed it among crowded taverns and spotted her amongst the woods that have been his home all his years. It is tangled in his earliest recollections, of his mother once weeping into the arms of a stranger who praised his kindness. So Nicholas is not afraid when she beckons from the woods, her torch lit in brilliant emerald flames. He rises from his mother’s embrace. The stranger points to her sleeping form, Nicholas obligingly brushes a kiss to her cheek. When he comes close, Death greets him with a small smile. “Hello, Nicholas.”

    And he knows her name, he knows it like he knows the lullaby he invented to pass time on their journeys, like he knows the smell of rain coming and the sound of his mother’s laugh.

    “Hello, Vida.”

    “Are you ready?”

    He nods. “I think so.” But he makes no move to follow her.

    “It’s alright if you aren’t.” she reassures him softly. “Many are not.”

    Nicholas isn’t sure how but he knows (he just knows) she wouldn’t take him somewhere bad. That’s not what stops him. He looks back at his mother and then up at Vida, chewing his lip.

    “Only…would you look after her for me?” he asks quietly, hopefully.

    And Death suddenly understands what Agatha means when she says there is only light in this boy, why the thought of his absence is so deeply abhorrent. Vida extends her hand to him; a promise, a vow, an oath she intends to keep until the last corners of the universe fold in on themselves.

    “I swear I will, Nicholas.”

    “Alright then.” His smile is guileless and warm and knowing. He knows he meets his death, and he takes her hand willingly, readily.

    Rarely has Vida encountered a soul she regrets taking but as they depart side by side, she knows one thing for certain: that the world will be darker without Nicholas Scratch.

    VALLEY FORGE, 1779 Chapter Notes Trigger Warning: Agatha is going through some pretty gnarly grief and there's some mild suicideal ideation involved. Think, begging Rio to take her to see Nicky, kind of thing.

    See the end of the chapter for more notes VALLEY FORGE, PENNSYLVANIA “Down, down, down the road, down the Witch’s Road…”

    It has been a truly miserable winter.

    Washington’s troops have been stuck here for months, beaten back by the British soldiers after they claimed Philadelphia for their own. The officers insist the move has been strategic, taking advantage of the Valley’s natural defenses but Agatha can smell their bullshit a mile away.

    If only that was all she could smell. The encampment is pieced together from log huts and threadbare tents that do little to chase out the bitter cold or the vermin alike. Every shelter stinks of old smoke from the sputtering interior fires and festering sweat from the dozen odd camp followers crammed in to each one together.

    And what a pathetic batch they are. The troops themselves are a ragtag bunch, riddled with diseases and exhaustion and, even more dangerously, an increasing apathy toward their struggle for freedom. This is to say nothing of their women and children who have been dragged along in their wake, few of whom offer the kind of prey Agatha has been looking for.

    “Circle sewn with fate, unlock thy hidden gate…”

    For weeks, she has sung the Ballad over and over in the makeshift square at the heart of the settlement while she stokes the fires or stirs the large communal soup pots. She has studied the faces of wives and daughters and relatives for any hint of recognition, any whisper that the legend of the Witch’s Road has taken root in their minds. It has been slow and fruitless and frustrating work and as the weeks drag on, Agatha has felt her power draining daily.

    When the girlish waif of a witch approaches her, it is all she can do not to leap at her.

    “I don’t mean to bother you, sister.” The girl’s manners are made stilted by the hunger in her eyes. “But I know that melody of yours. Have you a moment to speak with me?”

    Agatha pretends at reluctance, claiming she has chores and errands and tasks to complete but eventually, she capitulates. She listens and frowns sympathetically as the waif recounts her terrible tale of her slaughtered brothers and her passionate desire for the power to end this war. Her intentions are selfless; she seeks not glory for herself but the freedom of her fellow countrymen at the end of the Witch’s Road.

    How precious, Agatha seethes silently.

    She hems and haws, feigning hesitancy. She remarks on the dangers of the Road, of the terrible fate of other witches who have sought its prizes. Every word only hooks the waif further.

    “Well, if you insist,” Agatha sighs as if aggrieved. “You will need a coven.” “A coven.” the witch echoes to herself. The shortages of food have left all the camp inhabitants with the same frail, sallow look but this one was petite to begin with. There is almost nothing left on her bones.

    Agatha shrugs delicately. “As the ballad says, you must gather witches of fire, earth and-”

    “Air.” She finishes, looking pained. “Yes. Though I…I am not certain there are many of us here, sister.”

    “Find them.” Agatha snaps. “The Road is certain death without a coven.”

    She’d taken a risk following along with the revolutionaries because the combination of aspiration and desperation created perfect conditions for the kind of witches most likely to seek out the legend of the Road. But then Washington’s forces were forcible ejected from Philadelphia and while the Law of Covenstead insists there are least three witches amidst this camp, they are doing an infuriatingly good job of keeping it to themselves.

    “Perhaps we could go together?” the waif suggests hopefully, reaching out for Agatha’s hand. “Better two than one, after all.”

    Burn and brew with coven two and glory shall be thine…a young voice hums absently in the deepest recesses of her mind.

    Agatha forces a smile, pats the girl’s hand reassuringly. Her touch makes Agatha’s skin crawl, but she hasn’t had a boost of power for a long time, too long. She’s hungrier than she has been in years.

    “It is the only way, I fear. Seek them out and I will gladly show you the Road.”

    She sends the waif on her own quest. If she plays her cards right, Agatha will feast well enough to leave the encampment on her own, brimming with power once more.

    And in the meantime, she has other webs to spin.

    The wonderful thing about war is the opportunities it offers to the unscrupulous. Agatha has no real interest in whether the British retake the colonies. Neither does she believe in the virtues of the revolutionary struggle. In fact, Agatha makes a point of caring for nothing than her own survival these days. She buried those soft parts of herself decades ago with her son.

    Donning her cloak, Agatha abandons the square, confident that no one will be paying attention to her. The local farmers have come to fleece their meagre wares at outrageous prices to the troops; the prospect of sustenance is far more important than one lone woman vanishing into the snow-ridden woods.

    Her contact from the British army awaits her some four miles south of the encampment. He has at least shed that ridiculous red uniform, as she insisted last they met; talk about sticking out like a sore thumb.

    He straightens as she approaches. “Mistress Hayfield.”

    “My, don’t you cut a fine figure, Lieutenant Crawley.” Agatha croons, enjoying the way his lip curls in disgust.

    This one doesn’t like her, she knows. She offends his sense of decorum, his sense of decency. Crawley doesn’t like her willingness to deceive her compatriots, to steal and lie under the noses of her people, even when his command benefits from the documents she smuggles to him.

    “You have brought the maps?” he says instead, refusing to take the bait she dangles.

    Agatha raises a brow. “Have you brought my fee?”

    “I have.”

    With a flourish, she produces the parchment papers detailing Washington’s newest defensive plans from her sleeve. “Then we have a deal.”

    “Traitor!”

    The sound of the musket firing is a deafening echo amidst the snowy woods.

    Agatha throws herself to the side and the bullet marked for her tears right through the lieutenant. He dons his customary red after all: crimson splutters from the gaping chest wound as he collapses to the mud.

    Agatha fixes her attention to the young, grubby revolutionary who has dropped his now-fired weapon and is advancing swiftly.

    “You!” he howls at her, contorted with his outrage and fury. “You traitorous bitch-!”

    Frantically, Agatha summons her reserves, but her hex is weak; the spluttering purple light does little to deflect the youth’s fist as he collides with her cheek, shattering the delicate bones there.

    It has been a long, long time since a man came close enough to Agatha to strike her. She’d forgotten the shock of it. It’s the only explanation for how he manages to land his next blow so quickly into the hunger-struck frailty of her stomach.

    The pain is hot and thick and suffocating. She coughs, hunched forward as he throws her from him, as if disgusted by the sight of her.

    “I’ve half a mind to snap your neck right here for this wickedness. But you’ll pay for this deception before the whole regiment. I’ll see you hung for this!” he snarls and kicks her hard enough with his ragged boots that she flips onto her back, wheezing.

    The dark, leafless canopies overhead spin violently against a pale wintery sky but Agatha can barely think straight, let alone resist as he binds her hands together with his own rough-hewn belt. The leather bites into her wrists hard enough to bleed with every step he takes, dragging her limp body in the snow behind him.

    If she could breathe around what is surely broken ribs, Agatha would scoff at herself. Laid low by a fucking boy barely cut loose from his mother’s apron strings. She’s fought harder than this when she had little to no control over her own abilities. She fought her own mother harder than this. It’s pathetic. She’s pathetic. She’s pitiable and weak and despicable and wicked. What good would it even do, Agatha thinks dizzily to herself as the youth yanks her rough through the mud and snow behind him, to resist now? Why bother? So she can return to her soup cauldron and wait for the waif to return, to bring her a new coven to slay?

    What power is there to be gained from other, half-starved witches anyway? What does survival matter when the purpose of your existence has been taken-?

    Above her, the revolutionary’s footsteps halt suddenly.

    “Who goes there? Another spy?” he barks suspiciously, holding his now empty musket at the ready.

    Agatha would do anything not to hear a familiar voice answer: “That’s a strange fish you’ve caught there.”

    “This one is a traitor to the fucking English,” he snaps back. “Fit to be hung for the entertainment of the regiment, I say. Now move on. This is no business of yours.”

    “A very strange fish,” Vida murmurs as though he hasn’t spoken. She steps closer, barely paying him any mind and kneels beside Agatha’s face, now muddied with snow and blood. “But a beauty, nevertheless.”

    “Fuck. You.” Agatha hisses back woozily. There’s two of Vida in her vision, the images blurring and swaying with nauseating speed but once she gets her hands on the real one…

    But the revolutionary holds firm to her bindings, even as she rocks forward, trying to get her bearings.

    “Enough!” he demands, assuming her to be pleading for assistance. “And you, move on. As I said-”

    “You’ve done enough talking.” Vida says coolly, gripping his tongue between her talons in a move so swift Agatha had not even seen her rise.

    “Be gone.”

    The youth’s eyes are wide, rolling in their sockets with pure panic as he realises precisely how far out of his depth he is but within the span of a moment, it is done: his body goes limp and he sags into the snow, cold as the grave he’ll never be given.

    Agatha wrestles with the belt binding her hands, rising to her knees. She chews at the old leather with her teeth like an animal but her body is weak and dizzy and starving: every blink of her eyes makes her head spins sickeningly.

    She slurs the words she wants to scream: “You monster-”

    Vida’s claws fix to the belt before Agatha can pry it loose. She tugs sharply and Agatha’s balance is so shaken that she falls near flat on her face at Vida’s feet with a groan.

    “Look at you.” Death murmurs. “Weak as a kitten, aren’t you, my love?”

    “Don’t!” the witch howls the word, half-demand, half-plea. She is at Vida’s feet, staring up at the face her heart once ached for. And those words are daggers to her soul.

    “I told you! I told you I would hate you and you still took him-!”

    “Agatha-”

    “I told you!” she screeches hysterically, the way she wanted to the morning she found her boy cold in her arms.

    Vida unexpectedly twists the belt and yanks her to her feet, bringing their faces into close proximity. Agatha wants to scream again, wants to howl like a wild, untamed beast in the throes of an unnameable anguish. But there is some power in those midnight eyes that commands her to be still. “It was long past his time, Agatha.” Vida whispers after a long moment.

    “But it is not your time to join him. Even if I took you, Nicky would hardly recognise his mother.”

    Agatha can only heave a sob at the sound of Nicholas’ name, at the gentle sorrow in Vida’s voice.

    Goddess three, it has been so long since there has been another who remembers the face of her little boy, who can recall the pitch of his joyous laughter. Her grief hungers for another who knows of her loss. Misery loves company after all.

    “Take me to him,” Agatha begs. She doesn’t care that she’s pleading like a beggar, she doesn’t care that Vida’s grip on the belt is so tight that her wrists are bleeding anew. “Please, my love. Please, if you ever…if you ever cared for me, for us, take me to my boy, please.”

    Something in the depths of those midnight eyes tightens. “It is not your time, Agatha Harkness.”

    Death runs a gentle thumb over her cheek. Agatha can feel the bone twitch and wriggle under the skin, not just in her face but throughout her body. Such power in those delicate fingers.

    “Vida, please,” Agatha sobs. “Make it so, I beg you-!”

    “You don’t beg, you take.” Vida snarls, even as her cool fingers comb gently through Agatha’s muddied, tangled hair. “This weakness, it sours you, Agatha. This is not the witch I know. This is not you-”

    Agatha’s sorrow has been a constant companion these many years. It has shaped her as much as it has eaten from her, leaving her hollow and frail in the places where her joy once lived. But she has known her fury since the cradle. And it quickens inside her now, not unlike the babe she once carried. She rears forward and her teeth sink into Vida’s throat without warning, incisors digging deep into the supple flesh. Blood gushes into her mouth but there is no pulsing metallic taste, only a cold earthiness like a handful of grave dirt.

    Agatha doesn’t care, doesn’t stop; she bites down hard and rips her back, taking some chunk of flesh with her.

    “I still have some bite left in me, you proselytising asshole.” she goads with a half-delirious cackle around her bloodied teeth.

    Her heart thunders wildly and her whole body seems to have abandoned its pain; instead it chases the thrill of adrenaline, filling her violent, flexing purple power until it wreaths around her like a thunderstorm, like her anguish has been made spectacularly visible.

    And Vida is smiling. No, not smiling. Grinning.

    “There’s my girl.” Death croons, heedless of the gaping wound in her neck. Already the flesh is knitting back together, right before her eyes.

    “Fuck you!” Agatha snarls. The violet storm swells with rage, blowing the midnight-eyed creature right off her feet and into the trunk of a nearby tree. “Don’t you try to placate me.”

    Vida sighs with relief- No, not Vida. Vida was the name of her lover, Vida was the dark twin flame of Agatha’s twisted soul, Vida held her in her arms and screamed Agatha’s name in ecstasy.

    Death rises to her feet in a single, smooth move and grins with all the menace of skeletal remains. “Finally. I was getting worried, my love.”

    “Don’t call me that.” Agatha repeats, but her pain is no longer dizzied and frenzies but scabbed over like her swift-healed wounds. She cannot succumb to her grief. Not when she can feed it to her rage instead, to her survival instincts, to everything that keeps her alive, keeps her a witch. But her powers are burning through the very last of her reserves, through the pools of energy she had not even known she had, and Agatha is no fool. She cannot face her enemy like this.

    But neither can she resist the last word: “You are nothing to me now. Just the thing that took my son.”

    Death’s smile does not twitch, her face demonstrates no hint that the words have landed any injury. But Agatha can detect the shadows within those black eyes now.

    And when she spins on her heel, intent on returning to her waifish prey and recovering the glory of her power, she knows this is not a meeting either of them will soon forget.

    Chapter End Notes This chapter was alternatively titled: "How Agatha Got Her Groove Back."

    History check: In 1779, Washington’s forces endured a brutal winter encampment at Valley Forge. Facing severe shortages of food, clothing, and shelter, the suffering was extreme, with thousands of soldiers dying or deserting.

    Tensions were also running high as information on both sides began to leak, foiling battle plans and trust among the forces: American general Benedict Arnold famously defected to the British and secretly plotted against his compatriots, though this would not become public knowledge until a year later in 1780.

    MANHATTAN, 1849 Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes MANHATTAN, NEW YORK Agatha has discovered she’s a city girl at heart.

    There’s a hedonism to the air down here that makes her blood sing, a silent agreement amongst the inhabitants that you can buy anything (violence, flesh, drugs, souls) if you wave enough money under the right noses. The anonymity is delicious: Agatha can wind her way through high class card dens and smoke-filled gentlemen’s parlours, slip amongst seedy brothels and overcrowded boarding houses alike, dabble in a few backroom deals at the occasional evening gala festivity and poof! Her reputation is mist behind her, evaporating in the daylight. Those who do follow its path, who try to seek her out, are her favourite kind of prey: greedy, blind with ambition or desperate.

    If only her witch’s scent were so easily dispelled. With the Fell banishing charm drained and long abandoned, there are a batch of pesky Salemite spawns still determined to hunt her down. New York is a muddling, chaotic pit of scents and trails but Agatha knows it’s only a matter of time. There are few things that could repel them effectively. She should know, she’s spent the past seventy years investigating every possible cure to her little hive-mind roadkill problem.

    Which is why she’s waiting on this morning’s client even though the cretin is an hour late.

    Agatha catches a well-groomed waiter with a flirtatious smile. “I’ll take another gin. Put in on the Forrest tab.”

    “My, it sounds as if you’ve had quite the head start, Miss Hardison.”

    Her eyes flash back with warning. “Well, a girl’s gotta keep herself occupied when her escort makes himself scarce.”

    Forty something years old and growing more handsome by the year, Edwin Forrest has a thick head of hair and matching whiskers that pair well with his roguish smile. Agatha hasn’t seen him perform yet, but she supposes he has the look of an actor. Whether he merits the accolades heaped on him by the American press as the ‘finest homegrown actor ever to set foot on stage’ is debatable.

    Edwin inclines his head with a regretful sigh. “My apologies, I was detained by my director. His obsession with the Scottish play is endless, it seems.”

    “Double, double toil and trouble, rather?” she taunts idly.

    The other man twitches slightly, unable to conceal his nerves a moment longer and for a second, she can spy a much less self-assured figure beneath his veneer. “Indeed. Now, shall we discuss the matter at hand?”

    “I like a man who gets to the point. What can I do for you, Mr Forrest?”

    Her gin arrives in the nick of time as Edwin launches into a long-winded explication of his recent scandals: an acting tour four years ago across England’s finest playhouses that ended sourly, his brief friendship turned bitter rivalry with their homegrown Shakespearean, William Macready. As he describes his most recent act of vengeance (hissing from his theatre box during Macready’s performance. How uninspired), Agatha begins to gather her own suspicions.

    She waits until Edwin’s drink turns up at their table and then smiles like the Cheshire Cat: “So how long were you two fucking?”

    He splutters his sherry across the pristine white tablecloth, drawing the concern of the nearby tables to Agatha’s delight. “How dare you- I would- that is a vile accusation-!”

    “Spare me,” she waves a bored hand. “I know a lovers’ spat when I hear one. So? How long were you two playing at St George?”

    “Miss Hardison,” Edwin grinds out, heatedly. “That is enough-”

    “Giving him his green gown? Blowing the groundsels?”

    “Six months!” he finally growls under his breath, looking as if he’d like her to throttle her if they weren’t in public. “We were involved for almost half a year. There, are you satisfied?”

    Agatha purses her lips. “Almost.”

    “Well?” he sounds harried.

    “Who ended things?” she wonders, more to see him squirm than from curiosity. Men these days could be so awfully prudish about a casual round of sword-polishing under the covers.

    Edwin grimaces. “He did. I accused him of setting the English audiences against me, of seeding the jealousies of other actors toward me.”

    “And did he?” Agatha probes the wound with twisted satisfaction.

    Edwin finishes what remains of his sherry in a single gulp. “He never denied it. Said it was beneath his standing to refute such allegations.”

    She takes a deep breath in and sighs it out with exaggeration. “There, don’t you feel better, Eddie? Can I call you Eddie?”

    “I-”

    “Good. So.” Her smile lengthens into a sneer. “Let’s talk revenge.”

    It takes disturbingly little effort to cajole the New York masses into action. All Agatha has to do is point out to a few keen eyed, patriotically inclined journalists that the upstart Englishman Macready is set to perform his own Macbeth on the same night as their beloved Forrest.

    “And at the Astor Opera House no less!” she adds insult to injury, watching as her words incite outcries of fairness, equality and other loud, populist rhetoric.

    Constructed to purposefully exclude those who could not afford its performances, The Astor is a testament to the lengths wealthy New York theatre patrons will go to avoid mingling with the unwashed working classes and, oh the horror, immigrants. Amidst the Bowery area overpacked with the newly arrived and overworked, the Astor is a stinging reminder of how little the city’s elite think of them.

    Agatha would be lying if she said she didn’t enjoy the idea of ruining the evening of a few rich assholes.

    Then it’s a simple matter of magically stirring the pot, so to speak: when the paper handbills calling for a vocal (albeit peaceful) demonstration of support in favour of the working man are plastered all over the city, Agatha is there with her sly purple craft. She sends around a few hundred of the angriest off to the box office to buy up as many seats in the top circle for Macready’s performance and spends the next few days whipping them into an absolute frenzy.

    Then, clad in her finest purple silk gown and petticoats, Agatha arrives at the Astor early on Monday evening with her own ticket and a smile.

    “Excited for the performance, madam?” the usher politely enquires as he escorts her inside the theatre. He cuts a dashing figure in his grey waist coat with its polished buttons.

    “Oh, yes.” She winks at the young man, toys with his starched white collar playfully. “I just love a woman on a murder spree, don’t you?”

    He smiles uneasily for just a second before his eyes roll back in his head. She catches him in a hex and lets his limp body fold carefully into her seat. "Nostrae figurae commutantur..."

    When she withdraws, she has swapped their forms: with any luck, he will sleep under the illusion through the performance, Macready’s public embarrassment and any potential fallout.

    Agatha, meanwhile, plans to enjoy the freedom of trousers.

    She returns to the doors of the theatre and gleefully begins to let in the three hundred or so angry Forrest supporters who eye her sceptically as though surprised they’ve managed to get this far. But in they come in droves, smuggling all kinds of interesting shapes under their coats and skirts: she detects the smell of rotten eggs and apples, potatoes, even bottles of their own urine saved for special purpose.

    Agatha doesn’t think much of mortals, but she’ll admit they have a certain creative penchant for violence she finds endearing.

    The curtain rises, the performance begins. Shedding the usher’s face, Agatha wonders if she is the only one who can detect the seething hatred bubbling away above the crowds of New York’s elites, clad in silks and jewels.

    Idly, she pops the buttons of her trim waistcoat and scans their faces from her vantage point in one of the inexplicably empty private boxes. It’s a sea of politely captivated expressions as Macready prances across the stage, none the wiser-

    Except one.

    The moment after she locks eyes with Death’s black gaze, Agatha can hear her whisper in her ear: “So foul and fair a day I have not seen.”

    “Quaint,” Agatha sneers, unwilling to rise to the bait offered her. “What do you want?”

    “What I always want.” Death murmurs cryptically, materialising in the seat beside Agatha.

    She’s immaculate as ever. Her hair is pinned up to accentuate the arch of her neck and the delicate curlicues of her collar bones are displayed to perfection in her off-shoulder gown. These ridiculous, voluminous skirts would have swallowed a lesser creature but not her; they swirl about her soundlessly like deep, verdant shadows.

    The two of them are pressed close in the darkness, surrounded by bodies and yet totally unseen. The shiver her voice sends down Agatha’s spine was thrilling once but her body is not yet attuned to the anger in her belly. It still craves her once-lover’s touch.

    Agatha forces her heart to calm. She has a job to do, a task to finish and a goal to mind. She has no time for these games. “I didn’t take you for a theatre-lover.”

    Death’s laughter is slippery as oil. “You didn’t take me at all, Agatha. That was usually your role, wasn’t it?”

    “Take care with your words,” Agatha’s temper flares despite her attempts to cool it. “Or I’ll be inclined to take another bite of that throat of yours.” “Do you promise?” Death whispers with mild interest.

    “One way to find out.”

    She marvels, “You know, no one speaks to me the way you do.”

    “Oh? And how’s that?”

    “With such irreverence.” Death hisses the last syllable mockingly.

    Goddess three, Agatha has to shut her up because every moment she keeps talking, Agatha’s body spends remembering all the ways that Vida (she is Death, she is Death, not her Vida, not anymore) used to make her sigh.

    So Agatha turns with eyes like daggers and stubbornly snaps, “You want reverence? Prove yourself worthy of worship first.”

    There are many ways Death could prove such a thing to Agatha. Bringing her son back to her, for one. Keeping the Salem Seven off her back, for another.

    Slipping to her knees in front of Agatha’s seat is not one of them.

    “What do you think you’re doing?” she snarls near silently, as Death dares to run her cool, firm hands along her thighs.

    “Proving something.” Death murmurs, never once taking her eyes off Agatha’s.

    Agatha’s hand shakes as she raises it to Death’s sweet face, her fingers sparking with a lilac so pale with heat it’s nearly white.

    She knows better than to think she could vanquish Death herself. But she thinks…she has done her research. Agatha could hurt her. Rich with the energy of three freshly slain covens, her power brims and spills over the edges, ready to wreak havoc where she wills it. It has nearly been a century since she last saw Death and Agatha has been planning, studying, researching for this day-

    Her fingers are ungentle as they spear through Death’s perfectly pinned locks, rumpling their immaculate styling. But Agatha makes no move to remove herself. And she says nothing as Death deftly unbuttons the fly front of her stolen uniform, heedless of the crowds below them.

    It has been a century and a half since she first laid eyes on this creature. But Agatha has never seen her from this viewpoint before: between her thighs, gripping her tight, black eyes flashing upwards with a nameless fervour as she spills all the secrets of the universe into the point of contact between her tongue and Agatha’s flesh.

    Agatha’s fist is curled tight around locks of black hair, savouring the illusion of control over this cosmic creature whose power conquers all, whose mouth is slick and grinning, who has not blinked as if missing the sight of Agatha’s bitten-back cries is too unbearable to even consider-

    She refuses to scream. But the sound she makes as Death drives her to the very height of pleasure is no softly spoken whimper.

    Which is about when the screaming starts.

    “Shame!” howl the dissenters from within the Astor’s top circle. “Shame!

    Shame!”

    The noise is truly deafening, and it only gets worse as Macready attempts to speak up over their jeers.

    Agatha smirks, her body thrumming with residual energy. “That’s my cue.”

    Death tilts her head. “What a shame.”

    “Shame on the leeches!”

    “Down with the codfish aristocracy!”

    Agatha stands from her seat, fixes her trousers and raises a brow at the creature who makes no move to rise from her knees, only sumptuously licks her lips clean. “Don’t come looking for me again.”

    Death leans back against the balustrade of the box and smiles like a half feral feline. “How can I stay away when you keep sending me so many pretty gifts?”

    Agatha’s lip curls in disgust. “They’re not for you. Not anymore.”

    She serves herself now. She slays for herself, survives for herself.

    Death doesn’t seem wholly convinced. Truth be told, neither is Agatha.

    “I think I’ll stick around a few more days.” She says instead casually. “See the sights. Catch a few more shows.”

    Displeased, Agatha scowls but the sound of shattering glass below cuts her off, raising the timbre of the crowd’s panic as the top circle launches all sorts of trash toward the stage. The audience begins to retreat in fear of the projectiles: shoes, rotten vegetables, bottles of piss, pieces of their own seats.

    It gives her a moment to remind herself that she does not care what this creature does anymore.

    “Do what you will.” Agatha says dismissively, busily fixing the buttons on her waistcoat and reapplying her usher-faced illusion. “I have work to do.” “That’s what you call this?” Death scoffs, dark eyes rolling in their sockets. “This kind of meddling is beneath your talents, Agatha.”

    “And you, sadly, are not.” Agatha replies to the innuendo bluntly. “So do us both a favour and stop looking for me.”

    Death is quiet a moment as Agatha makes her exit, paused only when she speaks in a strangely earnest tone: “This confidence suits you. Better than what I saw last time.”

    One foot out the private box door, Agatha cuts her a filthy look over her shoulder: “You can’t know how little that means to me.”

    “He’s scheduled another performance!” Edwin paces his hotel suite with a newspaper in hand, waving the crumpled pages like a lunatic.

    Lounging nearby with a drink courtesy of their hotel, Agatha rolls her eyes when he isn’t looking. “So what? You got what you wanted.”

    “Are you even listening?” he snarls, throwing the article to the floor at her feet. “He’s not beaten, not if he’s planning to perform tomorrow night as if nothing happened! You promised me ruin, Miss Hardison!”

    Agatha raises her brows, sceptically. “His audience threw their own piss, Eddie. They bottled and brought their own urine specifically to douse your ex-lover on his own stage. I’m not sure what more you could ask of me.”

    Edwin’s scowl is thunderous. “I’m asking for what you assured me.

    Scandal, embarrassment, shame. Everything I suffered at his hands, I want returned on him tenfold!”

    She pauses, taking in the impassioned outcry for a moment and claps her hands. “Huh, now that was convincing. Maybe you have the talent after all.”

    But Edwin is in no mood for her mockery it seems. “If I am not satisfied,” he grinds out ominously. “I will not be fulfilling your request.”

    That catches her attention. “For a Shakespearean, you seem awfully quick to cross a witch, Eddie. It never ends well.” She warns in a deceptively soft voice.

    “Then get me what I want.” He snarls, loom forward with both hands on her arm rests. “Macready ought to be finished in this town when you’re done with him.”

    Agatha tilts her head, eying him with open hostility as a snake might consider a particularly mouthy rat. And speaking of rodents…
