    There is a crow at the window to the suite, pecking idly against the glass pane. They’re on the fourteenth floor, higher than scavenger birds prefer to traipse for their meals. And it is staring directly at Agatha as if trying to discern whether it recognises her.

    She makes her decision swiftly. “Then I want my payment now.”

    Edwin scoffs, retreating to the array of whiskeys set up near the window to pour himself a stiff drink. “Impossible. I have no assurance you’re even capable of completing your promises-”

    He turns back to her, but Agatha is quicker, suddenly standing only inches from him with a crackle of purple light tussling amongst the locks of her hair.

    Edwin startles, nearly drops his glass but she snatches it for herself, finishes it in a single swallow.

    “Thrice to thine and thrice to mine and thrice again, to make up nine.” She quotes idly. “You should read your playbook more carefully. That’s the law of Threefold, you know. What you release unto the world will return upon you three times over. Are you willing to accept your consequences, Edwin Forrest?”

    For a scarce moment, there is real hesitance in his eyes, real fear. Agatha savours it. Then he nods. “I am only returning what was done to me.”

    Her smile lengthens. “Then I will take my payment now. And you will have Macready’s ruin.”

    The riot kicks off beautifully.

    By the time the curtain rises on Macready’s recovery performance of Macbeth, there are thousands amassed in the street outside the theatre. The city’s police chief has called the spineless new mayor who has summoned the militia in a panic to protect the interests and safety of their ‘upperten’ citizens. The political thugs of the New York underworld have brought their own club-wielding brutes to play amidst the crowds, their passions stirred to frenzy with Agatha’s assistance.

    The violence intensifies to a heady fever pitch, the streets awash with blood, sweat and fire. The police and militia fire on civilians but there are six working men to every officer; many of them vanish in a tangle of bruised fists and thrashing limbs.

    Agatha watches it all unfold from a safe distance, high on the rooftops of Grace Church just a few blocks away. Churches have never been particularly generous to witches but Agatha finds its smooth stone arches accommodating for her purpose. She watches the fray with her witch’s senses more than her eyes, waiting until-

    Ah, yes. There, amidst the havoc of the riot: the Seven move amongst the heaving bodies, graceless and jerking as if they haven’t practised their human forms in a while. Invisible to the mortals around them, the Seven are relentless as they hunt for Agatha’s scent toward the theatre. Those who unknowingly cross their path meet a savage, swift end: necks are snapped, eyes are plucked out, bones are chewed in a matter of minutes.

    Agatha finds her lip curling with disgust, if only at the grotesque state of their magic. There is little left of the children they once were, the pretty little girls who looked on Agatha as a sister and a friend. They have grown to maturity in form but their power was never honed, never channelled. It has festered inside each of them and calcified the bonds between them into something toxic and deranged.

    Eventually they come to the end of her trail, her scent mired by the violence ongoing in the street; the realisation that their prey has eluded them stings. The Coyote howls her displeasure when they find the Astor Opera House empty, carving her overgrown talons into floorboards like the animal she has become. The others join her, a screeching cacophony of rage at having lost their prey.

    If Agatha listens closely, she imagines those cries might almost sound like her name.

    She does not listen closely.

    Instead, she palms the scroll given to her by a reluctant Edwin Forrest.

    “I found it amidst a bazaar of oddities and ancient mysteries in London while I visited. The owner suggested it had been damaged in the Great Fire of 1666,” he’d explained, carefully unwrapping it from its cloth bindings. “But could tell me little else of its language or origins.”

    Agatha unfurls the blackened parchment with gentle hands, eyes hungrily absorbing the ink-struck shapes and forms.

    “He had only managed to translate this line here,” Edwin had added as he handed it to her. “It reads, A Moste Ancient and Wicked Spelle to the Unlocking of the Darke and All Powerfull-”

    “Darkhold.” Agatha runs her fingers over the word and the thrill it sends through her is simply delicious.

    Chapter End Notes History check: The Astor Place Riot was a real event in which over 20 people were killed during riots that took place outside the Astor Theatre, initially instigated by a bitter rivalry between American Shakespearean actor Edwin Forrest and British Shakespearean actor Charles Macready.

    CHICAGO, 1871 Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes CHICAGO, ILLINOIS Agatha has often dreamt of being set aflame.

    Usually it follows the memory of the coven’s betrayal. Agatha is dragged forward, and bound forcibly to the pyre built deep in the woods she knows so well. The rough twigs and logs tear at the soles of her feet, her wrists bound tight enough to bruise.

    The women around her gather and disrobe and there is no sympathy to be found, no face to comfort her. Her own mother stands before her and in this dream, she holds high a torch wreathed in black flames.

    “Mother,” Agatha wails, still desperate for some mercy, for some kind of kindness from these witches she has loved as her own for so long. “Mother, please.”

    “It must be so, Agatha.”

    “I can be good,” she promises. “I can be good, I can!”

    Evanora Harkness does not blink, only raises those black flames higher.

    “No. You cannot.”

    But instead of unleashing their own magic upon her, Agatha watches her set the torch to the logs, watches the flames as they lick at the hem of her gown until her entire lower half is engulfed with fire that eats greedily through her clothes and skin and muscle, all the way down to the bone-

    Typically, this is when Agatha awakens.

    But tonight, the flames come with her. The sky outside glows orange with fire and the wind sweeps thick plumes of acrid smoke across the city as the blaze feasts on building after building after building. The streets below teem with frantic hordes escaping the danger with little more than what they can carry.

    The door to her rented apartment is shaking in its frame as the landlord pounds frantically: “-Miss Harman! Miss Harman, if you’re in there, we have to leave! Miss Harman?”

    His voice is reedy with terror, but Agatha has no patience for him, only sends a powerful compulsion hex through the wooden door to send him on to the next flat.

    Instead of evacuating with the rest, she turns back to the witch’s circle she has painstakingly carved into the floorboards, at the offerings positioned at each point of the pentacle: water of the womb, an eternal flame, dirt from a virgin’s grave, a crone’s last breath and – most precious of all – her brooch containing the last remains of her boy. She’s spent two decades collecting each one under the specific circumstances detailed in the Forrest Scroll, all in preparation for this moment.

    But for some goddess forsaken reason, the Vault won't even show itself!

    Agatha takes a deep breath, ignores the screams of doomsday terror echoing from the streets below and resumes her position at the centre of the star. Her magic flexes and crackles around her, curious about the noise but she focuses it quickly, channelling every ounce of light into a single violet orb between her hands.

    Under her breath, she recites the instructions she has decoded and translated, the words etched into the sides of her skull: “Within this cyrcle, a Witch may find the Vaulte of Darke and Powerfull trickery. Be ware of its tayste for Feare or be swallowd by it.”

    She’s ready for the Vault. She’s ready for what lies beyond it. "Aperi mihi cryptam..."

    Agatha inhales deeply and her magic flows along the contours of the circle, responding to the runes etched along each line until-

    “Please Mother! Please, I can be good!”

    “No, you cannot.”

    With a roar of frustration, Agatha staggers from the circle, launched from the dream again.

    “What do you want from me?” she howls, glowering down at the spellwork as if expecting it to answer compliant.

    The only sound comes from outside as the building across the street suddenly, explosively, breaks into flames.

    Agatha presses her palms into her eyes and sinks against the windowsill with a half-sob of frustration and exhaustion. She’s been trying to access the Vault for hours now and all she’s managed to do is set most of Chicago on fire. Every time she makes an attempt, the circle absorbs and then rejects her power, sending up another building in smoke.

    “A taste for fear,” she breathes, casting her mind back through the pages and pages of research she’s spent decades collecting about this spell and its powers. “A taste of fear that swallows…”

    The vision is the same on each attempt: dragged, bound, sentenced, charge. Evanora holds the torch, Agatha burns.

    It’s not how it happened, of course. Her own mother indeed led the charge in her attempted murder but the flames are a gruesome addition to the familiar scene: every time, Agatha wakes with the smell of her own burning flesh in her nostrils.

    Outside, the city is a true nightmare, one of her own making. Agatha grits her teeth and turns away from the overcrowded, frenzies streets of evacuees. She just has to focus, she has to figure out what the Vault wants from her, she has to focus on the surroundings, not the fire-

    The black flames, Agatha suddenly realises. Her mother’s magic was blue, not black.

    Swiftly, she strides back to the circle, sits cross-legged at the centre. She summons her violet lightning and allows it to intensify…to build and focus…at the very core of herself…"Aperi mihi cryptam...Aperi mihi-"

    This time, she focuses on the sharp, stinging pain of the sticks piled under her feet as Evanora raises the torch of black flames.

    “You will always be a monster, Agatha. None can change that.”

    “Shadowfire,” Agatha snarls out the words around the panic clawing its way up her throat as the flames begin to chew through the pyre, swifter than ever. “That is shadowfire and I refuse to fear it!”

    Shadowfire preys on fear, amplifies it. It’s a nasty trick used to guard against nosy mortals who might want to take a peak inside a witch’s cauldron but the spellwork here is more powerful than anything Agatha has ever seen. No wonder the city is crumbling so quickly; every fearful citizen fleeing in the streets is feeding the blaze’s power.

    The flames crawl higher. She can feel her skin blistering and peeling, she can feel every agonising moment. But she hasn’t been ejected from the vision yet so Agatha grits her teeth against the pain.

    She has not come this far to fail now. She doesn’t care if her fucking face melts off, she will get what she came for.

    There she is, a voice from the depths of her memory whispers admiringly.

    The coven slayer.

    “I refuse to fear it,” she repeats instead, glaring at her mother’s form from between the flames. “It’s a trick and I am Agatha Harkness.”

    Use your purple, Mama. His voice is so sweet, so faint amidst the crackling inferno. Agatha allows her memories of her boy to sweep through her like a flood, allows all her weariness and sadness to drown out the fire’s burn… “I refuse to fear it, do you hear me-?!”

    The fire is at her throat now. It laps at her locks of hair, singeing the ends and the smell is putrid.

    Won’t you tell me why you’ve come?

    Send me another gift, Agatha Harkness. Perhaps I’ll answer you.

    “I AM AGATHA HARKNESS.” Agatha bellows and with her final ounce of strength, she thinks of Vida: of her liquid dark eyes, of her gentle hands, of her shrieking cackling laughter and the look in her eyes when she had given Agatha’s son the first breaths he never should have had.

    Why are you here?

    Why else?

    “I REFUSE TO FEAR THIS FATE.”

    The air shimmers, mirage-like in the heat around her and streaked with smoke. But her mother’s form is gone now like a bad memory.

    And replacing it is something she never could have imagined.

    “Nicky.” Agatha breathes.

    The vision is clouded and pale, its edges ragged as if Agatha has torn open a hole in the universe itself but she knows the edge of that smile like she knows her own breath. Her beautiful boy stands amidst a large open field. The scene is strangely sapped of colour, the sky emptied of its vibrant blue, the birdsong muted.

    Quietly, he holds his hands outstretched as the wind stirs up the stalks of pale golden grass.

    “Nicholas?”

    But it’s not Agatha calling for him this time.

There is no part of her safe from the fire now: not her lungs or her tongue or her lips. How can she let go of this image, of this glimpse of a boy she has spent centuries mourning? The shadowfire can have her, so long as she can have him, she bargains delirious with pain. She is burning, she is burning, she is burning- “-fucking idiota.”

    The room is on fire.

    “Wha-?”

    “Get the fuck up.” Death snarls as she yanks Agatha from the floor, heedless of her carefully prepared circle. Not that it’s going to survive the inferno currently engulfing the tiny apartment anyway.

    “Goddess three, what-?” she mumbles, staring up at it stupidly.

    “Are you deaf?” Death demands. “I said get up! And get out!”

    Agatha graciously accepts the wisdom in those instructions and staggers forward. She’s astonished to find her limbs at all, certain they had been charred to stubs at least but her skin is only sweaty and angrily flushed from the proximity to the fire.

    Death takes most of her weight, eerily strong for a creature of such petite stature but Agatha does her best to keep moving: out the door and down the narrow hall, dodging the ceiling beams as they groan and creak and topple from above in a cascade of sparks.

    “Keep going!” Death insists, almost pushing her into the stairwell.

    Agatha is exhausted and bewildered and riddled with phantom pains from burning alive in her vision. She blames all of these things for the way her heart leaps frantically when Death pulls away. “Wait, where are you going?!”

    Death pauses and glances over her shoulder with a smile that flickers with skeletal menace. “You’ve given me work to do, carina. As always.”

    It’s not until Agatha has thrown herself down the stairs and out into the soot-coated back alley behind the building that she realises she’s holding something.

    The book is slim but weighty, its leather cover splattered with unmentionable substances and etched with twisted runes of power. Her hands shake as she runs them over the pages; only the first third of the pages flick open, the rest glued shut tightly in the back of the book.

    The Darkhold. She’s holding the Darkhold. She summoned the Vault and took a copy of the Darkhold and burned down a city to do it. Death herself came to her rescue. She saw her son.

    Agatha stares up into the obliterating flames above her and she knows she’s being foolish when she prays to the Three Faced Goddess to bring that lurking, shadowed creature of hers back to her safely.

    But she prays nevertheless.

    Chapter End Notes History check: In 1871, a three-day fire devastated the city of Chicago, killing over 300 people and leaving thousands homeless. The blaze tore through 17,000 buildings and is thought to have been accidentally started by a woman knocking over a lantern in her barn while attempting to milk her cow.

    Agatha finds this rumour equal parts hilarious and insufferable.

    Also, other instances in which witches burned cities while accessing the Darkhold: - The Library of Alexandria, 48 BCE - The Sack of Ancient Rome, 64 CE - The First Grewt Fire of Lübeck, Germany, 1157 - The Great Fire of London, 1666 - The Moscow Fire of 1812 FALL RIVER, 1892 Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes FALL RIVER, MASSACHUSETTS With a noise of smug satisfaction, Agatha rolls on to her back as her lover’s body continues to tremble with the aftershocks of pleasure.

    “You will be the end of me, Aggie.” She manages to gasp out, pressing her lips against Agatha’s throat in gratitude. “But what an end to meet.”

    Agatha lets out a tiny chuckle. “I wouldn’t dream of it, sweet girl.”

    “I wish you wouldn’t call me that.” She grumbles, twirling a lock of Agatha’s hair around her finger. “I’m an old maid, you know. Everyone says so.”

    “You’re no maid,” Agatha murmurs back, voice thick with innuendo. “Not after this morning.”

    Lisbeth laughs freely, unencumbered by the guilt which had originally plagued her affections. “I suppose you have a point. Oh, I wish to never leave this bed.”

    Agatha allows herself to enjoy the way Lisbeth draws close to her side, warm naked flesh pressed together. It’s been a while since she enjoyed these particular comforts with a lover of any sort, least of all one as pretty and enthusiastic as Miss Borden, the town’s ever so prim and proper Sunday school teacher.

    But in the end, Agatha has her own plans to keep on track.

    “Is that horrid woman waiting on you?” she wonders in a carefully idle tone.

    Lisbeth groans, as if on cue. “Don’t speak of Mrs Borden here, of all places.”

    “How strange you call her that,” Agatha muses, ignoring the complaint.

    “Rather than Mother.”

    Lisbeth inhales sharply, bristling. “That woman could never be my mother.” Agatha presses a kiss to the top of her head in apology. “I know. I’m sorry, sweet girl. Tell me, is she very different?”

    Her body slowly relaxes as Lisbeth speaks, describing the mother she’d never really known but whose memory was the closest she came to worship. Dead just two years after Lisbeth’s birth, Sarah Borden nee Morse had been canonised in her daughters’ minds: a saintly woman capable of no wrongs or unkindness, a woman who would have managed their domineering father easily if only she had lived.

    Agatha listens intently. Not for the sentimental fantasies of a starry-eyed daughter but for any scrap of information that might lead her to the prize she seeks.

    “He loves her still, you know.” Lisbeth suddenly says in a wistful tone.

    “She makes him hide them away, but he kept all of her papers and books.

    I’ve seen them in his desk.”

    “Hid them, you say?” Agatha repeats, her voice carefully sympathetic.

    “How awful for you, sweet Lizzie.”

    “She really is the worst creature alive,” Lisbeth grumbles and then sighs, rolling away from Agatha. “And she will be home soon. You should go.”

    Agatha smiles affectionately, privately contemplating how she’ll gain access to Mr Borden’s private study. “If I must.”

    As Lisbeth leaves to freshen herself up in the bathroom, Agatha reaches for her crumpled day dress.

    She really is a sweet creature, Agatha thinks to herself. Uncertain but delightfully excited to be acting on the passions she’s long shamed herself over. Even if she is deluding herself where that deceased witch of a mother is concerned.

    Agatha fixes each button on her dress carefully, mentally reviewing every scrap of information she has gathered about the Morse coven who seem to have risen to power after a suspicious fire tore through the English town of Marlborough in 1653.

    Agatha knows how to recognise the Darkhold in the pages of history these days. The Morse coven positively drips with it. For a time at least.

    But most importantly, it seems that the secrets to whatever fortune discovered in its arcane depths has clearly been passed down: Lisbeth and her sister have been raised with silver spoons in their mouths and Agatha doubts its entirely due to Mr Borden’s frugal investments.

    She wonders if their beloved mother would have revealed the source of their luck in time. She’s read the Book of the Damned herself and knows the cost Sarah Morse must have exacted to ensure her children’s security. Human sacrifice, blood rituals. Frankly, she sounds like Agatha’s kind of witch. She’ll have to settle for rifling through her grimoire for answers as to how to open the next chapter of the Darkhold instead.

    Lisbeth smiles shyly at her from the doorway. “You really never take them off, do you?”

    Agatha reflexively clenches her hands, the delicate fabric of her gloves tightening for a moment. “I told you, sweet girl.” She says in a forcibly pleasant tone. “The skin is horribly rough. I’d hate to touch you so ungently.”

    It’s not a perfect excuse but the Darkhold has left her fingertips permanently mottled, coloured grey like fireplace soot. Agatha has never been particularly vain in her looks, but the sight is morbid. Besides, they attract more attention than suits her purpose these days.

    Lisbeth takes her gloved hand and brushes a gentle kiss to the knuckle, looking up from below her lashes. “I don’t mind ungentle.”

    For a moment – just a moment – Agatha imagines that she stands here in the bedroom of her lover without an agenda, that she has come for the sole purpose of showering this affectionate creature with her desires.

    It’s a pretty picture but an incomplete one. Lisbeth does not understand the heart of her, not really. She’s never met the real Agatha Harkness.

    “Oh Aggie,” her pretty little mortal sighs, embracing her waist tightly in her arms. “I think I’ll die if I don’t see you every day.”

    Agatha’s eyes strain from the urge to roll. “I’ll take you away from here soon, my Lizzie. We’ll be together, just the two of us.”

    Whatever platitudes Lisbeth is about to spill turn to ash on her tongue at the sound of the front door of the house opening and closing below them.

    “She’s back early.” Lisbeth hisses, eyes blown wide with panic. “Damn it all. How will we get you out of here now?!”

    Agatha seizes her opportunity. “Hide me in your father’s study,” she suggests quickly. “He’ll be gone a while yet. Then go distract her and I’ll sneak out when you two are up here.”

    “Yes,” Lisbeth mutters distantly. “Yes, that will work. Alright, come quickly then.”

    They tiptoe down the stairs in hasty fashion, listening keenly for the sound of chatter in the front of the house. Lisbeth’s stepmother must have brought home a guest because Agatha can detect the low timbre of another voice.

    “Miss Elizabeth?”

    “Bridget!” Elizabeth suddenly squeaks, unprepared for the maidservant who crosses their path. “Oh, I-I thought you were outside. Didn’t Mrs Borden tell you to clean the windows this morning?”

    The curly haired maid nods suspiciously, eying Agatha. “I didn’t think you had a guest, miss. I would have prepared tea for you both had I known.”

    Lisbeth’s smile stretches thinly, lips trembling with tension. “There was no need. It was a quick visit. Miss Harlow was just returning a few prayer books of mine.”

    “Very instructive,” Agatha pipes up innocently. “I learned so much, Miss Borden, my thanks again.”

    “It was my pleasure, Miss Harlow.” Lisbeth demurs.

    But Bridget’s pale blue eyes drift to Lisbeth’s slightly mussed hair, to the missing button on Agatha’s blouse that must’ve popped off.

    “I’m not sure Mrs Borden would approve of you bringing company upstairs, miss. I haven’t tidied it proper yet.” She says slowly.

    “I found it immaculate.” Agatha’s tone is bland. “Practically virginal.”

    Bridget scowls. “Still-”

    “Well, perhaps you could tidy it today, Bridget.” Lisbeth suggests, tugging Agatha away from the conversation. “Miss Harlow really must be off!”

    They turn a corner and Lisbeth immediately drops her pleasantries. “What was that? Are you trying to give us away?!”

    Agatha blinks at her innocently. “Never.”

    “In here,” Lisbeth demands, pushing her through a nearby doorway and insisting she wait for her knock. “I’ll take her upstairs and tell her Bridget has been lazing on the guest room. That’ll discredit whatever the girl says about you later.”

    “As you say, sweet Lizzie.” Agatha coos and with one last shy kiss, Lisbeth vanishes.

    Leaving Agatha is free to hunt for the Morse grimoire.

    Her powers have grown considerably since she unlocked the first chapter of the Darkhold. Her siphoning magic seems uniquely suited to its practises and she has absorbed its knowledge with gleeful abandon. Tugging her gloves off with her teeth, Agatha hunts with her newly honed senses, allowing her magic to fill the room with its presence and power.

    As if on cue, the third drawer of Mr Borden’s writing desk begins to tremble in its socket.

    The book is disguised as a simple volume on domestic practises, something Sarah Morse knew her husband would evidently never give a second glance. But Agatha peels back the illusion on the grimoire’s battered leather cover, revealing pages etched in dark dried blood.

    “Oh Sarah,” she sighs to herself. “I think I could’ve really liked you.”

    A sudden, almost silent knock breaks her focus; Lisbeth’s signal to leave the house.

    Agatha quickly restores the papers to cover her tracks and prepares to exit the Borden house and Lizzie Borden’s life forever – Only to find the one creature still able to steal her breath with a glance. “I should have known.” Death raises a faintly mocking brow. “What is it now, Agatha?”

    Agatha frowns petulantly at her tone. “What do you mean ‘what is it now’? What are you doing here?”

    “I was drawn here, though I didn’t know why until this moment.” Death drawls, taking in the grimoire in her hands. “Your prize, I take it?”

    Agatha resists the urge to hide the book behind her back like a caught child. “You know you have some nerve talking to me here. Now, after all these years.”

    Death’s eyes widen, just a fraction. “You’re unbelievable.”

    “Miss Rosario, have you seen Mrs Borden-? Oh. You’re still here.” Bridget the maid sounds dreadfully displeased by that fact.

    Rosario? Agatha mouths to Death with a deadpan expression.

    Death ignores her and smiles thinly at Bridget. “I believe her stepdaughter had something to show her upstairs.”

    The maid is barely listening, having suddenly realised where Agatha is standing. “What were you doing in Mr Borden’s study?”

    “A study? Oh, is it really?” she marvels foolishly. “I was looking for your toilette and must’ve lost my way.”

    “And found yourself some new reading material, I see.” Death suddenly chimes in pleasantly, looking pointedly at the grimoire.

    Bridget’s brow furrows into a thunderous scowl. “Did you steal that?”

    “Steal?” Agatha squawks, outraged. “How dare you! I am a guest of your mistress!”

    “You’re a thief if I ever saw,” Bridget snaps stubbornly. “Give back what you took and see yourself out-”

    “Oh.” ‘Miss Rosario’ suddenly exclaimed, her eyes drifting toward the stairs with something like realisation. “Oh, I see. Well, this should be interesting.”

    “I’m going to speak with Mr Borden about this!” Bridget fumes, headed for the front door. “I saw him coming back from his walk any minute and he’ll hear all about your thievery!”

    Agatha does roll her eyes this time and then scowls at Death. “Must you thwart me at every turn?”

    “I seem to remember pulling you from a burning building last we met, Agatha.” Death corrects her stiffly.

    And then you vanished, Agatha doesn’t say. Doesn’t mention how she waited hours that night for a glimpse of Death’s lovely, ash covered face. Doesn’t mention how she walked the wards of injured and dying for almost a month afterward, hoping she might appear.

    “And what did you mean, ‘this should be interesting’?” Agatha demands with narrowed eyes.

    Whatever explanation Miss Rosario owed her was interrupted by the sight of Lisbeth standing at the top of the staircase.

    Covered in blood.

    Agatha takes in each detail with a mix of intrigue and faint surprise. “… Lizzie?”

    Lisbeth smiles at her, angelic as ever. It’s hard to reconcile the sight with the blood coating her hands.

    “I did it, Aggie.” She enthuses in a voice just a touch too bright. “I did it for us.”

    “Oh?” Agatha wonders aloud, slowly climbing the staircase. When she gets about halfway, she can see directly inside the guest bedroom where she had spent the morning defiling the youngest Borden daughter. Only now it seems Lizzie has done some defiling of her own.

    The body of her stepmother is hunched forward on the ground, her back covered in bloodied gouges and her head…well, the little that is still identifiable as her skull is butchered almost beyond recognition.

    “See?” Death murmurs in Agatha’s ear, having followed her up the stairs.

    “Interesting.”

    Lisbeth narrows her eyes at the stranger’s proximity. “Who are you? Who is this, Aggie?”

    “Miss Rosario, she’s a friend of your mother’s,” Agatha answers distractedly. She really would not have pegged Lisbeth as a cold blooded murderer but, well, there doesn’t seem to be a better explanation. “Or, was.” “But I’m starting to like you much better,” Death teases, slipping passed the two of them to inspect the body. “Awful lot of rage you’ve been carrying around there.”

    “She deserved it.” Lisbeth snaps. “She found this.” Her hand unclenches around a tiny silver dress button. “And I just…I told her. I told her everything, how we were going to run away together, how we would leave everyone behind.”

    Agatha can feel Death’s eyes on her, and it takes every ounce of her control not to show her discomfort as Lisbeth rambles on about their fictitious plans. “All she could ask was what the town would say? How would it look, her husband’s daughter committing sin like this? Can you believe her, Aggie?”

    “Aggie.” Death playfully rolls the insipid nickname around her tongue.

    “Shut up.” Agatha hisses back.

    Lisbeth is staring at her earnestly. “I didn’t even think, I was just so angry. And before I knew it, there was this…this lightning at my fingertips and somehow I just knew it could take care of her. So I started swinging.”

    Death pokes at Mrs Borden’s pinkish brain matter. “We can see that.”

    Lisbeth frowns at her heatedly. “This doesn’t concern you, Miss…Rose or Rio or whoever you are!”

    Death’s cackle is quiet but deeply amused. “You’re not old enough to understand the irony of that sentence.”

    Lisbeth rounds back to Agatha. “I don’t know how it happened, but I did it for us, Aggie! We can be free now, don’t you see?”

    Agatha is spared having to come up with some empty platitude by the sound of Bridget storming up the staircase to join them: “Now, I’ve told Mr Borden all about your sticky fingers- Holy Mary, Mother of God!”

    Her screech at the sight of Mrs Borden’s corpse is loud enough to wake the dead or it would be if Agatha hadn’t swiftly bracketed the girl’s face in her hands.

    “Gentle now, girl.” Agatha murmurs softly, allowing her magic to infiltrate the maid’s mind, to saturate every layer of her consciousness like rain sinking into the dry earth. “Accipe hanc novam memoriam...There is naught amiss here. You will go downstairs and take care of your master as if all is well because it is. Do you understand, Bridget?”

    Lisbeth looks heartbroken at their embrace. “Aggie, what are you doing?”

    “She’s practising a new trick.” Death says admiringly. “Picked that one out of your new book, Agatha?”

    “Her name is Agnes!” Lisbeth suddenly howls, her temper stirred to blazing in seconds.

    The lightning that erupts from her fingertips is crimson, with more than enough to cleave a mortal’s head in two and it launches toward Death with brutal speed.

    Death moves not an inch, only lets loose a delighted cackle, grinning with all the macabre of a flayed man as, ready to accept the blow-

    Or would have, had Agatha’s own dusk-tinted magic not dispelled the magic before it could reach her.

    “Get yourself under control!” Agatha snarls and then fixes her former lover with a pointed stare. “And stop baiting her.”

    Meanwhile, Bridget shakes her head slightly in confusion, their connection broken. “I…I have to take care of Mr Borden,” she mumbles, heading down the stairs with lumbering steps as if half asleep. “I have to…”

    “Aggie, I don’t understand.” Lisbeth cries, eyes wet with tears. “Please, I don’t…you love me, don’t you? You said you loved me, I don’t understand…”

    “You and me both, kid.” Death mutters sourly.

    Agatha ignores the quip. She can feel Death’s eyes on her, can feel the tension simmering in the air in that moment. Jealousy, envy, disapproval, desire. Whatever it is, it rubs up against her like a needy feline.

    “Listen now, Lizzie,” she keeps her tone soft as she brushes the tears from Lisbeth’s face. “Listen closely. I am a stranger to you now-”

    “No!” Lizzie sobs.

    But there’s a dead society matron behind them, a hysterical witch covered in blood and an ensorcelled maid who is hopefully keeping her mouth shut downstairs. The time for plotting has passed; this calls for damage control if Agatha wants to get out of this clean.

    So Agatha steadies her hands at Lisbeth’s jaw. “Accipe hancnovam memoriam...I am a stranger to you now. You cannot recall what befell your stepmother, you will check on your father soon and know nothing of the matter. Listen Lisbeth…listen to my voice…”

    Lisbeth’s clear blue eyes go glassy and then blank as her mind adjusts to the reality Agatha has gifted her.

    And then, almost without meaning to, Agatha murmurs: “You will be vindicated, Lisbeth. Your freedom will be yours to be what you are, to love whom you choose.”

    She’s not certain why she says these things. Some distant, misplaced sense of guilt, perhaps. Or sympathy for a witch whose family ought to love her better.

    Whatever the case, Agatha allows these truths to be absorbed into Elisabeth Borden’s mind and then releases her.

    She forces a smirk as if everything is going precisely to her wicked, depraved plans. “Shall we?”

    Death follows her downstairs silently, glowering holes into the back of Agatha’s skull. She isn’t ready to meet her gaze, isn’t ready to answer to the questions she’ll find there-

    “Oh, for fuck’s sake!” Agatha nearly screams in frustration. “Can people in this damned house stop hacking at each other for ten minutes?!”

    Bridget stands beside the bloodied corpse of Mr Borden, a hatchet in one hand. “I must take care of the master of the house.” She says simply.

    Behind her, Death begins to laugh.

    It takes less than an hour to burn the blood-soaked clothes and the handle of the axe used to chop firewood, to lay new illusions into Bridget Sullivan’s mind that will hopefully exonerate the pair of them.

    By the time the news spreads about the slaughter at the Borden house, Agatha is safely ensconced in her room at the boarding house on the other side of town.

    Naturally Death appears the moment the door closes.

    “Aggie. Really?”

    “I liked it.” Agatha lies, to spite her.

    But the problem with baring your soul to the personification of Death is how difficult it makes deception.

    “No, you didn’t,” Death waves a hand dismissively. “But you liked her. I can tell.”

    “Perhaps.” Agatha shrugs off the thought. “It doesn’t matter much now.”

    “You were kind to her.” Death insists, following her with her eyes as she moves about the room, packing her trunks. She has the Morse grimoire now, there’s nothing keeping her in Fall River. “When you didn’t need to be.”

    “Let’s not overcomplicate things.” Agatha replies shortly. “It was the most efficient way to ensure her consciousness would accept the false memories.”

    “It was kindness.” Death says thoughtfully, stretching out across the bed. The sight fills Agatha with a sense of déjà vu, a reminder of their second meeting when everything was less…fraught. “But was it love?”

    “Love?” Agatha echoes in a scoff. “Hardly.”

    “You waited for me. After the fire.”

    Agatha goes still, halts every movement. “You saw that.”

    “Yes.”

    “And yet you didn’t show yourself.” Her lip curls, annoyed and injured by the implicit rejection. “Well, aren’t you just a peach?”

    “I wasn’t sure I should.” Death replies after a moment.

    Agatha wrestles with her impulses and eventually turns to face her, to study that ageless face for any clue as to her thoughts. She used to read this creature like a book. The thought that perhaps she no longer can is painful in ways Agatha doesn’t care to interrogate.

    “Why?” she demands, tossing a scarf to the ground. “If you saw me waiting for you, why not come to me?”

    “You saw things you weren’t meant to.” Death says cryptically, toying with one of Agatha’s hair ribbons. She rubs the silk against her cheek as she speaks. “Things I thought might upset you.”

    Things like Nicky. “Was it real?” Agatha whispers. There are cracks where her heart has been split with longing, cracks that she has patched up with spite and ruthlessness. They ache now with the words.

    Death pauses. “It was.”

    “Then why would it upset me?” Agatha pushes. “Seeing him again has been my dearest wish, you know that.”

    Death has tied the ribbon into a hangman’s noose around her fingers. “I wasn’t sure you would approve of my visiting him.”

    The words hang between them, crystalline and precious and fragile. One wrong move and they might shatter.

    “You visit him.” Agatha repeats the words, confirming what has long been her suspicion since that night in Chicago. “Often?”

    “Yes.”

    “He…he enjoys seeing you.” It is not a question, not really.

    “I think so, yes.” Death watches her steadily as she adds: “I promised him I would watch out for you. So sometimes I tell him what I see.”

    Oh, her heart. It feels as if someone has poured acid into those cracks, the muscle itself feels chewed through with shame. Agatha’s voice is weak with it as she asks, “You tell him what I’ve done?”

    “Not all,” Death assures her, swiftly. “Not everything. He likes to listen to your adventures mostly.”

    “What adventures?” Agatha whispers, still reeling from the powerful sting of her guilt. The thought of Nicky knowing, of his disappointment should he listen to her crimes, is more than she knew she could bear.

    “Sneaking between the camps of revolutionary armies. Tricking actors into making fools of themselves.” Death’s smirk is faint. “I embellish a little, perhaps.”

    There are not words to adequately describe how deeply thankful Agatha is in that moment. She can only stand there, inches from Death, caught in some spell that keeps the rest of the world at bay whenever they’re together. Finally, Death speaks: “I have something of yours.”

    In her palm rests a small, ivory cameo brooch. Her brooch.

    “You left it behind in Chicago.” Death murmurs, carefully affixing it to Agatha’s blouse. “I kept it in the hopes we would meet again one day.”

    Agatha thinks of all the days Death has held on to this trinket, waiting for an opportunity to restore it. It is kindness, she thinks to herself. But is it love?

    But Agatha knows her love isn’t kind. She knows it is vicious and cruel and impetuous. She wonders if it would be a kindness to leave Death now, to spare them whatever pain will inevitably comes next. It is love but is it kindness?

    She repeats the question over and over. She repeats it when she reaches for Death, when she presses their lips together. She repeats it with every finger stroke and every gentle brush of her tongue. It is love but is it kindness? “I refuse to call you Aggie,” Death growls as they topple back against her sagging mattress, limbs still locked together. “I won’t do it.”

    Agatha hears herself laugh. “Fine. And what do I call you? Rosario?”

    “If you like.” Death presses her skeletal smirk into Agatha’s thigh, eyes flashing. “Or Rose or Rio or whoever I am, wasn’t it?”

    “Come here then, Rio.” Agatha whispers, reaching for her.

    Death – Rio, now – rubs her face against her palm, needy and affectionate. “Always, Agatha.”

    Chapter End Notes me, with a megaphone and absolutely no evidence to support my beliefs: LIZZIE BORDEN WAS A LESBIAN.

    History check: Lizzie Borden was the youngest daughter of the upper middle class Borden family in Fall River, Massachusetts. Her mother (Sarah Morse)

    died when she was a child and her father remarried Abby Gray in 1866. Lizzie reportedly believed Abby had married her father for his considerable wealth and only referred to her as 'Mrs Borden'. The Bordens were murdered in 1892, after which Lizzie was tried and acquitted of the crime despite several unsettling and contradictory details in her statement regarding her alibi.

    The Morse family did originate from the English town of Marlborough which did suffer an intensive fire in 1653. To my knowledge, the Morse family did not perform any spells or blood rituals.

    However, Lizzie did go on to have close friendships with various women including popular actress Nance O'Neil. In fact, after an argument about Nance, Lizzie and her older sister Emma became estranged and never spoke again.

    PARIS, 1900 Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes PARIS, FRANCE The turn of the century is always a spectacular occasion. Agatha should know, she’s seen three of them now.

    Something about the cycle of time gets the mortals fired up. They think on the future, on their legacy, on what the world will look like when this wheel of fortune next turns, a hundred years from now. It makes the worst of them even duller, but it makes the best feisty and sharp-eyed.

    In Paris, all the keenest are gathered to celebrate L’Exposition Universelle de 1900, a two-hundred-day festival held to celebrate the newest innovations from every corner of the world. The city thrums with the energy of so many minds, heady with all the possibilities. The perfect place to finally discover the secret to unlocking the next chapter of the Darkhold. “You know, it hasn’t changed since the last time you read it. Or the hundred times before that.”

    Not to mention of course, that her stay in the City of Lights has been made all the more pleasant by the dark eyed wraith in her bed.

    “You don’t know that.” Agatha mutters petulantly. But studying the yellowed parchment page carefully at the desk set up in the corner of their Parisian pied-a-terre apartment reveals nothing new.

    “I had no idea you were such a voracious reader,” Rio stretches out her lithe limbs out against their feather mattress, taunting Agatha with the glimpses of smooth naked skin. “It must interfere terribly with all the, you know, killing.”

    Abandoning the Darkhold to whatever dimensional void it lingers within when she’s not using it, Agatha smirks at her lover. “You’re not subtle.” “Oh?” Rio blinks deliberately.

    Agatha prowls toward the bed and slips between the sheets, pressed alongside Rio’s outstretched form. Her skin unusually warm for once, from her lounging in a patch of morning sunlight.

    “You’re jealous I haven’t brought you any gifts lately,” Agatha surmises, the grey of her eyes lit with amusement. “Is someone feeling a little neglected?”

    Rio’s brow twitches, a sure sign that she’s sulking about something.

    “Perhaps. You have spent the past six months pouring over those dusty old books.”

    “When I could be pouring over your dusty old skeleton,” Agatha teases.

    “You’re right, how shameful of me.”

    Her dark eyes flash with heat and suddenly launches forward, climbing into Agatha’s lap without hesitation.

    “I don’t like sharing your attention, Agatha.” She grinds down in warning, their bodies separated only by a deliciously thin bed robe.

    Agatha can’t quite bite back her groan. “You knew I was coming here for a reason. You’re the one who chose to come with me.”

    Rio’s hips are making slow, infuriating circles against her. “Do you want me to leave?” she says in a low taunt.

    The witch snarls in frustration. “That’s not what I meant.”

    “Good.” Rio is heavier than her form suggests, intentionally so; she enjoys pinning Agatha to whatever horizontal surface available to them, but she especially loves to provoke Agatha into flexing her powers.

    And Agatha of course is only too happy to oblige.

    Her magic crackles with pulsing violet light; with the speed of a whip, she has flipped them over, her body now pressed atop Rio’s. “How’s this for a gift?” Agatha croons mockingly.

    Her reaper grins gleefully at the change in positions, hands eagerly climbing inside Agatha’s robe. “Well, it’s a start.”

    They’re hardly attached at the hip, or any other part of their anatomy for that matter. Agatha has her own agenda, as always.

    The Morse coven spellbook taken from Lizzie Borden had been something of a dead end, excusing the pun. Her ancestors did not seem to have cracked more than the first chapter of the Darkhold either. But there was a scribbled note on the very last page theorising pessimistically that the key to accessing the next chapter may have been lost to the fires of Alexandria Library.

    Officially, nothing survived the destruction of the Library. Unofficially, the largest collector of Library documents departed for Egypt permanently a year ago and left behind most of his collection in Paris.

    Standing on the sidewalk in front of the immaculate townhouse in the heart of Le Marais, Agatha grunts with exertion as she tries to force apart the wards protecting the Maspero home. These are an old, ancient magic scarcely practised anymore, the kind Agatha imagines Monsieur Maspero must have picked up on one of his jaunts through Egyptian tombs.

    A passing couple eye her sweaty brow in silent, snooty judgement. Fucking Parisians.

    “Va te faire voir!” she snaps at them. No matter what Rio says, it is the only French Agatha has really found much need for since they arrived.

    With a deep breath, she straightens her spine and recites the spells she has long memorised from the Book of Sins. "Aperi coram me...aperi coram me..." Her power prickles with every carefully pronounced syllable until- CRACK!

    The wards audibly snap like an overstretched rubber band, the wrought iron gate swinging open like an invitation.

    Agatha smirks and lets herself inside.

    Gaston Maspero has made quite a name for himself in the field of Egyptian antiquities but there’s much less said about his penchant for Ancient Egyptian blood rituals. Even Agatha is surprised by the size of his private collection of relics: papyrus scrolls, stone tablets, emerald amulets the size of a baby’s fist.

    She bets the Director of the Louvre would give his eyeteeth for an hour alone in this house.

    “Well, well, well,” Agatha murmurs to herself, inspecting what appears to be an original complete copy of the infamous Padiamenope Papers. She tucks them into her skirt pocket for her later. “Looks like someone’s been keeping all the good stuff to himself. Naughty boy.”

    Her magic struggles to detect her prize, muddled and confused amongst such ancient relics. Which leaves Agatha no other choice than to look herself, even though she has no clue what she’s looking for.

    The sun has begun its descent by the time she comes across a small orange clay tablet marked with a familiar symbol: four bound circles like keys inserted in a lock.

    Agatha’s smile widens as she caresses its rough surface, detecting the faintest hint of sulphur. “There you are.”

    She heads back for her apartment, feeling unaccountably smug in her victory. Like a returning conqueror, she plans to demand all the appropriate adoration from her lover who will undoubtedly think very little of yet another dusty text for her to read.

    Agatha doesn’t care. She approaches the pied-a-terre with a jaunty step, savouring the moment. Agatha Harkness, wielder of the Darkhold, is one step closer to her goal, thrumming with power and satisfaction in one of the most beautiful cities in the world with the most stunning creature to have ever existed-

    Who is not here.

    “Rio?” Agatha calls out, frowning. “My love?”

    She checks the bathroom, the balcony, the parlour, the bedroom. Twice. Rio is nowhere to be found.

    Agatha purses her lips, considers her options. This morning’s quip about Agatha’s neglect was not enough to prompt her to leave, was it? She’d more than made amends before she departed. And Rio does have work to do, as she keeps reminding her, though usually she does it while Agatha is busy herself.

    It is the first time Agatha has returned to an empty apartment since they arrived here months ago. She finds, swiftly, that she does not care for it. Before she’s even really decided to do it, Agatha is reaching between the bed linens for a stray strand of Rio’s raven dark hair. It’s not exactly polite to go foraging for scraps of your lover in order to hunt them down but, well, Agatha’s never been accused of minding her manners.

    “O partem,” she murmurs, knotting the strand with careful, practised movements. “Totem reperi…”

    The strand goes suddenly taunt in her hands, stiff as glass, and begins to spin against her palm like a compass needle. Part, find the whole. So does Agatha go to find Death.

    There is a pale stone embedded in the doorway above the entrance to the Parisian catacombs etched with the words: ARRETE. C’EST ICI L’EMPIRE DE LA MORT.

    Stop, the archway warns her as she passes beneath it. Here lies the realm of Death.

    An ominous warning to some. Agatha has learned Death’s name in every tongue imaginable and some no longer uttered but there is a reason the word is so often regarded as feminine.

    The passages are narrow and dimly lit, the stairs leading deep into the bowels of the city where the darkness is almost tangible. Every surface is the stuff of nightmare: the empty sockets of a million skulls peer at her curiously, wedged between pelvic bones, femurs, ribs, sternums.

    Someone has lit torches at parts of the tunnels. By their light, Agatha finds her way easily enough, guided by her makeshift compass spell. Eventually, the sound of voices drifts toward her, bouncing off the ossified walls.

    The accent is French but he speaks English well enough: “-truly an honour, monsieurs. I am sure you will find my work to be the marvel of modern medicine that it is.”

    Agatha is unprepared for the way the passage suddenly widens into an immense hall the size of a cathedral. The ceilings are painstakingly decorated with what looks to be rib bones laid in precise, macabre patterns, and at the centre of the room what looks to be a surgical theatre has been erected, a pale cadaver laid out on the operating table in a simple white cotton sheet.

    Surrounding the table stands an unlikely group: scholars, by the looks of them, and nurses, doctors. Perhaps three dozen men and women alike gather around the speaking Frenchman, a spindly fellow with a slightly manic look in his eye. And on the edges of the crowd, Rio.

    The peculiar gathering pay her little mind as she sneaks around their edges but Rio studies her approach with a faintly annoyed expression.

    Agatha ignores it. “Having a party without me, darling?”

    “You have your errands,” Rio deadpans. “I have mine. You couldn’t have waited?”

    “You couldn’t have invited me?” Agatha counters sharply. “What is all this?”

    “Apparently,” her lover drawls flatly. “I’ve been vanquished.”

    Before Agatha can interrogate that little concept further, the Frenchman begins whatever demonstration he’s been gearing toward. One of his assistants, wrapped in simple cotton scrubs, prepares the cadaver for surgery while another reveals a well-stocked surgical instrument tray.

    Agatha recognises most of the shiny steel tools but there is one unusual knife that catches her eye: the blade is wickedly curved and gleams with malice, its horn handle polished to a black shimmer. It's a shape she has seen in old spellbooks, a knife she has only ever known to be associated with one thing.

    “…is that-?”

    “Yes.”

    “That’s a reaping knife.”

    “Yes.”

    “But isn’t it-?”

    “Mine.” Rio snaps testily. “Yes. Now shut up.”

    “You lost your reaping knife?” Agatha cannot contain the mirth in her voice. “You lost your-”

    “Shh!” a narrow lipped woman in spectacles hushes them sharply.

    She rolls her eyes in response but concedes to lower her voice to continue interrogating Rio. Or she would if the Frenchman wasn’t ripping open the dead guy’s ribcage with a bone saw.

    He speaks cheerily as he works, as if they weren’t leagues underground and surrounded by literally millions of dead remains. “I have found les catacombes a uniquely suited setting, likely due to the combined environmental factors such as low humidity, temperature and such. You can expect a publication on the matter shortly.” He adds pompously.

    Two more assistants begin to dress him in a white cotton gown for the surgery, working quickly and efficiently as he continues to address his audience.

    “Of course, my first experiments were conducted at La Pitié-Salpêtrière but the hospital administration has demonstrated its contempt for this marvellous new field. A disappointment but c’est la vie, of course.”

    “What do you call this practise?” a German in a suit near the front calls out. “It is nothing less than the revival of life, monsieur,” the Frenchman pauses dramatically. “I have referred to it as l’art du reveilogie.”

    “Reawakening,” Rio scoffs with an unexpected degree of agitation. “What fucking mierda.”

    “Chut!” the bespectacled woman hisses again. “Un peu de respect, madame!”

    Agatha flicks her fingers, and the resultant hex yanks a chunk of her dishwater blonde hair right out of her skull in retribution. The woman outright yips at the unexpected pain but Agatha pays little attention.

    “What is he doing with that knife?” she probes Rio. “What is all this?”

    Rio wordlessly hands her a small playbill flyer, the kind handed out amongst the world fair crowds for lectures or demonstrations to attend. This one depicts the Frenchman, René Brissaud, holding a human skull high in the air as if it were a trophy. WITNESS THE DEMISE OF DEATH, it reads.

    INTRODUCING MONSIEUR BRISSAUD’S GROUNDBREAKING NEW TECHNIQUES FOR THE REANIMATION OF THE RECENTLY DECEASED. JOIN US TONIGHT AT LA PLACE D'ENFER.

    Agatha can feel her jaw dropping but she’s unable to contain her astonishment. “Is this…is he going to…?”

    “Seems like it.” Rio grumbles.

    “But that’s-”

    “Yep.”

    There are black magics and dark arts and forbidden powers. Agatha, naturally, is well versed in these. But then there is necromancy.

    Contrary to popular opinion, necromancy is not capable of resurrecting or even summoning the dead. If it was, there is no doubt in Agatha’s mind that she would have followed its practises to bring her boy back.

    No, necromancy is not witchcraft. It is nothing less than the practise of letting ghosts fuck you over.

    And not just ghosts you’re trying to reach either. No, necromancers open doors at random like a drunk party guest looking for a bathroom and whatever steps through those doors tends to be the meanest, strongest spirit available.

    Nothing offends Rio more, Agatha knows. There is no balance, no order to it. Witches might bend the world to suit their ambitions but necromancers gleefully tear gashes in the fabric of the universe with little regard for what they are unleashing.

    Brissaud has successfully exposed the dead man’s heart for the crowd who edge closer, titillated by the spectacle of blood and gore. “Now, mesdames et monsieurs, I must ask for complete silence as we begin.”

    Of course, the crowd breaks into whispers at this dramatic request.

    Agatha glances at Rio expectantly. “Well? Aren’t you going to do something?”

    She crosses her arms with a sneer. “Mortals have to learn some time.”

    Brissaud selects the curved reaping knife from the tray with a flourish and begins to insert the tip into the bloodied hunk in his hands. A second later, to the crowd’s astonishment, the heart begins to twitch.

    “Mon Dieu!”

    “Oh my!”

    “How is that-”

    “Unbelievable!”

    “Incroyable!”

    A chorus of incredulous cries fill the chamber. One woman staggers back, another man faints dead away to the ground.

    Clearly enjoying their reaction, Brissaud replaces the heart in the corpse, removes the reaping knife and replaces the removed sternum in quick succession.

    And, miraculously, the corpse takes a shuddering, horrible breath.

    “Well, that’s not good.” Agatha mutters warily as the revenant lurches upright, inhaling sharply as if unused to his lungs. Which he isn’t because if that is the spirit of the dead man, Agatha will eat her own head.

    Even Brissaud appears surprised by the energetic movements of the newly risen corpse. His half dozen assistants, on the other hand, remove their surgical masks in unison.

    The sight is not a pretty one. One face is covered in infected boils, another is bloated and swollen as if he has been drowned some time ago. One of them is missing half her jaw; the wound is ragged like the blast from a shotgun. Each of them watches Brissaud with eyes cloudy with death and riddled with menace.

    “Oh, great.” Agatha hisses, throwing her hands up in exasperation. “More revenants! Just terrific!”

    “I didn’t ask you to come.” Rio reminds her in a sing-song tone of mocking. “You knew I’d come find you.” Agatha snaps, quickly shedding her tidy purple silk day jacket. “This was brand new, you know.”

    “Was it? You hadn’t mentioned.” Rio replies sarcastically.

    Agatha glowers at her as she pulls her lace gloves off.

    “Monsieurs, please, do not worry, there is some adjustment in these things,” Brissaud calls out but even he doesn’t sound convinced.

    The mood in the chamber has shifted from elated and dazzled to ominous in seconds. The crowds look between the newly risen corpse and the mangled assistants and in their mortal hearts, they can sense something is deeply, deeply wrong.

    There’s no telling how old these spirits are, how long they have lingered here in the darkest depths of Paris. But they have been given new bodies now. And they intend to use them.

    “Merci, René.” The Revenant’s tone is gravelled and raw, more akin to a growl than a voice. “Mais maintenant-”

    His hands move quickly, with unnatural strength. In the blink of an eye, René’s head has been torn clean off his shoulders and tossed amongst the crowd.

    Then the screams begin in earnest.

    The six assistants move swiftly. However long they have bided their time with Brissaud, waiting for this particular spirit to rise, they’re clearly practised with their stolen corpse limbs; Boils launches himself at the German man from earlier, tearing into his flesh with his bare hands while Shotgun furiously bashes a woman’s brains into the earthen floor, more animal than human anymore. The crowds are desperately trying to escape, their screams bouncing off the bones with a horrid, ghastly echo but there is only one narrow door and seven vicious undead monsters.

    When Drowner catches sight of Rio, he lets out a bellow like an injured beast, recognising her instantly.

    Eyes flashing with anticipation, Rio lets him prowl closer than Agatha likes; when he is only inches from her, she unleashes a blast of vivid green lightning so powerful that he is thrown high above the crowd into the opposite wall, knocking loose a half dozen skeletal bones with the force. “Ghosts.” Rio snarls. “God, I hate ghosts.”

    Agatha grimaces as the other revenants begin to take notice of them, each recognising the face of Death, come to send them into the void once more. One of them lunges forth but Agatha is quicker. “Hey asshole!”

    She wrenches her hands in a complex, fluid pattern that literally tears the creature in half, filling the chamber with the smell of rotting flesh and old blood.

    One of the revenants scoffs, “Une simple femme? Comment?”

    “L’Americaine, rien que ça.” Another adds in a sneer.

    But their leader seems to know better. “Une satané sorcière!”

    Agatha winks at him with gleeful mocking. “Guilty on all counts, sweetheart.”

    “Enough flirting. Time to head back to the void, boys.” Rio adds flatly.

    “Play time is over.”

    Curses and hexes soar around the catacomb chamber, a storm of green and violet energy unleashed in full force. Agatha has rarely had the opportunity to test her power like this, to fully unfurl the knowledge she has gained from the Darkhold, all she had learned in these past decades. Even rarer is the opportunity to do so at Death’s side.

    It is a heady combination indeed.

    The revenants die screaming and cursing but they die. One by one, Rio banishes these errant souls to the world beyond this one until even their leader succumbs to the power of the original Green Witch herself.

    The last of the mortals has escaped while the undead were sufficiently distracted with Agatha, who scoffs when silence overtakes the chamber once more.

    “You’re welcome!” she snarks after them.

    “Chasing accolades, my love?” Rio teases as she wipes the blood from her pale green lace blouse.

    “A little recognition wouldn’t kill them.” she sniffs back.

    Rio’s smile is unusually soft. “Then may I say how stunning you are like this?”

    “Blood-soaked?” Agatha grumbles, stepping over the massacred bodies of those who did not run swift enough “Victorious.” Rio corrects, tilting Agatha’s chin upwards to meet her gaze. “Triumphant. Powerful.”

    Agatha preens under the compliment, flicking a piece of gore-drenched hair out of her face. “That’s more like it.”

    With a chuckle, Rio retreats for a moment, heading for the toppled instrument tray. Among the blood splattered tools lies her reaping knife. “So?” Agatha asks, watching as Rio inspects the blade carefully. “How’d he get his hands on that?”

    “I suspect he found it down here somewhere.” Rio murmurs back. “This is where I last had it.”

    Agatha’s brow furrows. “You remember losing it?”

    “I didn’t say I lost it.” she bites back, then sighs. “I left it with someone. A friend.”

    “Who?”

    “Her name was Genevieve.” Rio’s eyes are distant as she speaks. “She was a witch, even if they called her a saint. And she loved the dark. I used to tease her, blow her candle out while she prayed at night. So when they buried her up there, I brought her here instead. Someplace she could finally rest. I left it to protect her tomb.”

    Agatha chews her lip, struggling to maintain her composure but her ego is bruised, her heart stung with envy. She had thought herself unique among Rio’s affections. She’d imagined that no other being alive or dead could know this creature the way Agatha did.

    Oh so saintly Genevieve, a nasty little voice mutters. Precious candlelit Genevieve.

    It’s as if Rio can hear the voice too because her brows twitch with amusement. “Now, now. Don’t get all riled up.”

    “Over some Frankish peasant who performed a few measly miracles?

    Doubtful.” Agatha scoffs, tossing her hair back impetuously.

    Rio’s smile widens. “She wasn’t like you, Agatha.”

    “Bully for her.” Agatha mutters waspishly.

    “I mean, I didn’t feel for her as I do you,” Death corrects herself, tucking the blade away safely in her skirt pocket. “She loved me in vain.”

    “How pious of her.”

    “Agatha.” Rio looks like as if she’s holding back her eye roll. “There is only one witch who has ever held a claim to this black heart of mine. This I swear.”

    Their hands interlock, Rio’s flesh cool as ever and slick with blood splatter, but all Agatha can think of are the handfasting rituals of her youth. Of course, she’s been wed and divorced and widowed countless times over but they’ve hardly mattered, not to a witch who does not recognise the authority of any church, least of all one with a man in charge.

    But standing here in this cathedral of bone and blood in the realm of Death, surrounded by the carnage of their own making, Rio holds her hand and makes her a vow and Agatha-

    Agatha says: “I am not myself without you.”

    Agatha says: “I think we might be parts of the same whole.”

    Agatha says: “This I swear.”

    There is no ribbon to knot around their hands but it hardly matters. When they return to the surface, the moon is high above them amongst a cloak of stars. Even so, Agatha swears she can feel sunlight.

    Chapter End Notes History check: Gaston Maspero was a very well known French egyptologist who retired to Egypt in 1899 where he remained until his death. To my knowledge, he did not perform spells.

    The Patron Saint of Paris St Genevieve, however, blinded her mother when she refused to let Genevieve go to church on a feast day and miraculously healed her twenty months later when she forgave her.

    Extremely witch-coded.

    The Parisian World Fair of 1900 is absolutely a thing that happened.
