    SOUTHAMPTON, 1912 Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes SOUTHAMPTON, ENGLAND Agatha watches distractedly as the porters bring their luggage aboard, each trunk and suitcase carefully charmed shut. Amongst them, the remaining third-class board in droves along a plain, wooden gangplank that leads directly to their dormitories on the lower decks: a pack of men in simple work clothes who laugh uproariously together, a woman in a grey dress lugs a pair of suitcases, a young couple with their hands interlaced stare up at the enormous ship with awe.

    From the gargantuan ship’s uppermost deck, they look more like ants than people. She worries how many witches are amongst their number.

    “So? Is it what you imagined?” Rio teases in her ear, arms slipping around her waist without hesitation.

    Agatha smiles faintly. “How many opportunities come along to set sail on the largest ship in existence?”

    Rio sniffs. “I could’ve sent us back to New York in a blink, you know.”

    Her gloved hands tighten on the wooden railing. “And leave behind my hard won treasures? I don’t think so.”

    In their decade spent traipsing across the continent, Agatha has indeed collected a vast array of magical artefacts, scrolls and curio, not to mention her penchant for silks and lace fineries. But they both know the real prize Agatha refuses to give up.

    Rio presses a gentle kiss to Agatha’s neck, just above her pretty high collar. “How are you today?”

    But the softness of her tone grates. Agatha scoffs, shrugging free of her lover’s grasp. “Perfectly well, as I told you when we boarded and when we left the hotel and when we woke this morning. Enough, Rio.”

    Her dark eyes harden and narrow slightly. “You’re the one waking up screaming every night, Agatha.”

    The truth is hard to bear. Ever since she opened the second chapter of the Darkhold, Agatha has been plagued with nightmares not unlike the vision she underwent to accessing the Vault in the first place back in Chicago. But these visions have begun to follow her into her waking hours.

    The Maspero tablet had warned her as much when she finally decoded it. In addition to the spells needed to unlock the next chapter, the tablet also revealed the names of each of the Darkhold’s three chapters. The first she knew well: Fear & Anguish. Agatha had mastered her ability to draw power from her own terror as well as the anguish of others.

    The second, Madness & Control, was proving more temperamental. She hadn’t mentioned to Rio the full extent of the Darkhold’s consequences but Death was no fool.

    “I’m not saying give it up altogether,” Rio was saying, reaching for Agatha’s wrist. “I’m only suggesting you take your time-”

    But Agatha dodges her grasp, instead adjusts her hat and gloves. “I have it all in hand, I assure you. Now, I must insist that we inspect what I so dearly overpaid for.”

    “You forged the check.” Rio points out, lips twitching.

    Agatha winks at her playfully. “Then by all means, let’s inspect what the dear Duke of Westminster paid for.”

    The Grand Ballroom positively glitters.

    Crystal chandeliers hang like frozen halos above the crowded room, casting delicate light over the polished mahogany floors. The air is thick with the scent of fresh flowers, candle wax, and the faintest hint of salt from the sea. Music swirls from the orchestra at the far end of the room. A waltz, perhaps, though Agatha is a little too distracted preening before the sea of politely outraged faces to pay much attention.

    “Look at them all, clutching their pearls,” she sighs with a smirk, fluttering her fingers at one particularly elderly prude. “You’d think they had never seen tuxedos before.”

    Rio chuckles beside her, their arms linked. “To be fair, my sweet, I doubt they’ve ever seen a sight quite so dashing as you before.”

    Agatha leers right back at her, admiring the dark green cravat tied neatly at her throat. “I could say the same.”

    “Shall we give them something to really ogle at?” Rio sweeps them toward the dancefloor, her fingers coming to rest gently on Agatha’s waist beneath her perfectly tailored jacket.

    Agatha joked once that the last ten years have felt like a honeymoon, that the romance would undoubtedly wear off and they’d grow bored and fat like spouses tend to. But it does not seem to matter how long she spends in Rio’s presence; every time she touches Agatha, her stomach does a dizzy little flip with unrestrained delight.

    Rio moves with devil’s own grace, whirling them around the dance floor with practised ease. It’s the one circumstance in which Agatha submits, follows rather than leads, content to be swirled through each movement.

    Rio’s dark eyes flash as she turns Agatha into a spin, her suit tailored so sharply that it looked as though it could cut glass. Agatha remembers the afternoon they had it made in a tiny shop overlooking the Venetian canals, watching hungrily as the wizened Italian tailor zipped around Rio to take her every measurement. It is worth every cent.

    Agatha finds herself paying less and less attention to the scandalised whispers of the upper crust classes, her focus lost in the delicate curves of Rio’s face.

    As if sensing her captivation, Rio’s smile lengthens. “You’re staring again.” “You know,” Agatha murmurs back. “I do believe I never want to move from this spot.”

    Rio’s arms tighten. “Then don’t. We’ll stay in this moment forever.”

    “Forever is an awfully long time.”

    “Oh, believe me, I know.” Rio’s eyes have always absorbed the light around them but Agatha would swear they glow in this moment. “Agatha-”

    But something catches her eye, just beyond Rio’s shoulder: a face watching her, a blonde woman with flashing eyes filled with something more than the outrage she’d expected. Something like…hunger.

    Then Rio sends them into another twirl and when Agatha looks back, the face is gone. Her pulse quickens for a moment, a small stab of paranoia making her skin prickle, as though she had just witnessed something she shouldn’t have.

    “My love? What is it?” Rio wonders aloud, hand tightening on Agatha’s waist.

    Agatha forces a smile, grinds her teeth together as she feigns calm.

    “Nothing at all. Though I could do with some champagne. Should we find our seats?”

    Rio doesn’t appear remotely convinced, but she nods, leading them toward the tables. Her touch is steady against Agatha’s back, an anchor in the middle of a storm.

    Her gaze flickers again to the back of the ballroom, but the woman is gone. As though she had melted into the shadows of the room.

    The days pass in all their gilded glory. As per usual, Rio is gone most of the day on her own 'work errands' but Agatha doesn't mind much. She has grown used to their routine.

    Instead, she savours the extravagant meals taken in the dining saloon, makes full use of the body massages offered in the afternoons before a relaxing hour or two in the Turkish baths. When she gets bored of her own company, she wreaks a little mischief at the card tables, playing bridge with heiresses and debutantes and the wives of oil tycoons.

    So far, she’s jeopardised several business deals and no fewer than three upcoming engagements. At least one of the young debutantes doesn’t look particularly put out by the change in plans.

    But throughout the hours, that persistent dread remains. Agatha’s skin prickles constantly as if she’s being surveyed.

    It’s making her irrational. Just this morning, she’d nearly exploded at the doe-eyed maid who had come to change her bed linens when she knocked on the door.

    “Madness and control,” Agatha murmurs to herself as she indulges in an evening walk around the decks. “Keep your eye on the prize, Harkness.”

    The real prize, of course, being the third chapter of the Darkhold.

    She pulls her dark mauve wool coat tight against the sharp evening wind.

    The moonlight is bright enough to set the waves aglow, their gentle peaks glinting like polished silver. The ship’s engines rumble far below, a sound so constant it had become background noise, Titanic’s own heartbeat.

    Idly, she finds herself peeling off her gloves to inspect the darkening hue of her fingertips. More evidence of the Darkhold’s influence, of the cost she is bearing in order to ascend to greater knowledge. The spells it unlocked were unlike anything she’d ever wielded before—raw, transformative, terrifying. If the price was the gnawing sense of being watched, the whispers she couldn’t quite make out, the way the shadows seemed to bend toward her when she turned her back…she could pay it. She would pay it and happily.

    “Careful, my sweet,” Rio had teased her the night before they departed.

    “I’m the only one allowed to get inside that beautiful mind of yours.”

    Agatha had laughed it off then, but now, with the vast ocean stretching endlessly in every direction, she isn’t so sure.

    She glances over her shoulder, her pace slowing as she inspects the near- empty promenade: a gaggle of half-drunk old geezers sharing a flask of alcohol, a young couple with their heads close together exchanging saccharine whispers of love and fidelity, a crewman in a navy uniform with his hands tucked behind his back, nodding politely to her as he passes.

    Absolutely nothing out of the ordinary-

    Until her eyes catch on a figure further down the deck: a woman in a rich velvet shawl. She turns her head slightly, just enough for the moonlight to catch the glow of her pale blonde hair.

    Agatha narrows her eyes suspiciously. She looks like the face from the ballroom on the first night of the voyage. The hungry one who melted into the shadows.

    Mind made up, she moves swiftly, boots clicking against the wooden planks. “Excuse me.” Agatha calls, her voice firm but not loud enough to draw too much attention.

    The woman doesn’t move an inch as she draws near but when Agatha reaches out, ready to grab her by the arm—

    —she snatches at empty air.

    Not in a puff of smoke or a flicker of light. She is simply gone, as though she’d never been there at all. Agatha stumbles back, scanning the deck with increasing alarm.

    The men have gone inside, the crewman has vanished on his rounds. The young couple stare at her with raised eyebrows for a moment before returning their nauseating attention to one another.

    “You’re seeing things.” she mutters under her breath. “Madness and control, Agatha, madness and-”

    “Everything all right, miss?”

    Agatha finds one of the ship’s stewards nearby, clad in his neat white uniform and ready as ever to help. A young man, probably no older than twenty. His face is so earnest she wants to knock his teeth out.

    The sudden spark of violent rage catches her off guard, cools her temper instantly. Was that her? Or was that the Darkhold playing with her mind again?

    “Quite,” she says simply, her voice colder than she intends. “Just… a chill.” The steward nods politely. “Best not to stay out too long, then. The wind can cut something fierce this time of night.”

    Agatha waves him off and resumes walking, her steps brisk and purposeful. She can’t bring herself to stand still, to let herself think too long. She has the unbearable feeling that if she does, the shadows might creep in again. It isn’t until she reaches the far end of the promenade that she stops, leaning against the rail and letting the wind whip her hair loose from its pins. Below her, the ocean churns endlessly, its dark surface rippling under the moonlight.

    Her reflection peers back at her in the glass window of the darkened morning breakfast hall, closed for the night. She looks tired. No, worse than tired— hunted.

    A flicker of movement beyond the reflection catches her eye. She freezes, straining to see inside the gloom but the outlines of the figures blurs, as though the glass cannot quite hold their shape.

    Breathless, Agatha lunges toward the window pane, unable to contain her anger this time. Her palms thwap loud against the glass.

    “Leave me alone,” she hisses viciously, slapping the glass again. “Expelle hoc hostis-”

    But then, below the rumble of the engines, she can hear them. Soft at first, like wind threading through the rigging, but growing louder with each passing second: the sound of her her own voice whispering to her in ancient tongues, a thousand spells she has never heard let alone uttered.

    The Darkhold is toying with her.

    “Enough!” Agatha snarls, turning back toward the promenade, her jaw set, her stride purposeful.

    She doesn’t care if it is real or not. If someone is following her—witches, hallucinations, figments of her cursed imagination—they’d better hope they are faster than she is.

    The hum of the Titanic’s engines vibrates low and steady through Agatha’s cabin. It should be comforting, that sound—constant and unyielding—but tonight it scrapes against her nerves like sandpaper.

    This gilded, immaculate suite has become a prison of sorts, especially with Rio gone as she is tonight. If she sleeps, the visions will chase her through her dreams. If she remains awake, the walls themselves seem to whisper to her.

    There is perhaps one more night left in their journey and then she will be on solid land once more where she plans to do whatever she must to master this chapter of the Darkhold.

    Agatha worries she will lose her mind otherwise.

    She slips fitfully, waking often. When she awakens some time just before the witching hour, at least Rio is there, one hand combing through Agatha’s hair while she idly reads a book in the other.

    Agatha wants to weep in relief. “I missed you.”

    “Did you now?” Rio hums casually, flipping a page.

    “Of course,” Agatha presses her face further into Rio’s lap, aching for her comfort, her silent and steady support. “I think…perhaps you might be right…”

    “I always am.” Rio replies but her teasing is oddly flat. “You know, I can see why it captivates you so, Agatha.”

    She frowns up at her lover, eyes straining against the gloom of their bedroom. “What do you mean?”

    “The Darkhold,” Rio replies, showing her the familiar cover of her book. “I understand now.”

    Agatha’s breath catches but before she can reach for Rio or the spellbook, Death’s gentle fingers become talons, tangling tight in her hair.

    The pain is swift and shocking. “What are you-?”

    Rio stares down at her, unblinking, “I think I’ll take it for myself, my love. I’m better suited to this kind of work, you see.”

    Agatha’s eyes water against the pain of her hair being ripped from the scalp but her mind is addled with the terror, the shock, the betrayal-

    Agatha awakens with a harsh, stinging slap! to her face.

    “There you are,” an unfamiliar voice laughs coolly. “Time to wake up.”

    Agatha’s eyes roll in their sockets frantically as she seeks the source of the laughter; she reaches for her power instinctively but there is something smothering the connection, some concrete block placed between her and her magic.

    “Uh, uh,” another voice chides. “That won’t do. Get her on her feet.”

    They pull her from her sweat drenched sheets, throwing her hard to the wooden floor. Agatha can’t seem to inhale enough air, choking on her panic as it drowns her. A moment ago, Rio was here, Rio was stealing the Darkhold and now-

    Now, she kneels powerless before three witches with extremely familiar faces: the blonde woman from the promenade, the doe-eyed maid she’d yelled at, the wife of the oil tycoon she’d played bridge with.

    “Good evening, Miss Harbridge,” Mrs Tycoon says pleasantly. “You’ve brought quite the treasure on board, haven’t you?”

    Agatha trembles with a dizzying mix of fury and panic. “You have no idea who you’re messing with.”

    Mrs Tycoon raises her brows. “And you should’ve known better than to flaunt the Book of Sins on a ship in the middle of the Atlantic.”

    The maid scowls at her, innocent façade abandoned. “There’s no easy way out of here.”

    “No help is coming,” the blonde adds, stepping nearer. “So just tell us where it is, and you’ll spend the rest of the trip in a nice little nap.” Her anger bubbles up, fuelling her desperation. The fear starts to peel away, replaced by raw, stubborn defiance. She knows she’s outnumbered but if she’s going down, she’s going down swinging.

    “Suck my dick.” Agatha snarls back, reaching for her power again.

    But the maid simply laughs, nodding toward the runes scratched into the four walls of her bedchamber: “Nice try but we’ve done a little redecorating while you were sleeping. No magic but ours in here.”

    “I'm sick of this.” The blonde snatches a handful of Agatha’s hair in her grasp and yanks her head back, just like Rio had in her dream. “Where is the Darkhold?”

    Agatha snaps her teeth at her, itching to take a bite out of her throat. “I’m going to enjoy watching you die.”

    Mrs Tycoon wrinkles her nose. “Now, now, there’s no need for such unpleasantness. Miss Cascot, release her.”

    The blonde reluctantly concedes and Agatha takes the spare moment of distraction to study the runes scratched into the walls, the ones keeping her power blocked.

    “I say we just start flaying her.” The blonde says petulantly. “She’ll cough it up eventually.”

    “We didn’t find it in her trunks,” Mrs Tycoon points out. “Which means she’s hiding it someplace only she can access. Isn’t that right, Miss Harbridge?”

    “My name,” Agatha seethes. “Is Harkness. Agatha Harkness.”

    All three of the witches freeze.

    Her reputation proceeds her evidently.

    The blonde whirls on Mrs Tycoon suddenly. “You said she was just a common witch, not the damn coven slayer!”

    “You saw her hands just as I did,” Mrs Tycoon argues, trying to stay calm and failing miserably. “She has the Book, that’s all that matters.”

    The maid ignores them both, eying Agatha with curiosity. “They say you survived the Witches’ Road. Is it true?”

    Agatha’s smile bears all her teeth. “Let me go and I'll show you.”

    The maid rolls her eyes. “How stupid do you think I am?”

    “Well, that depends,” Agatha's smile shifts into a smirk. “How did this unholy little triad come to be, hmm? I mean these two, I get, but you?"

    "Shut up."

    "-well, you don't strike me as the lady of leisure type-"

    "I said, shut up."

    "-in fact, I bet this is the finest room you’ve ever set foot in without a feather duster-”

    The maid screeches, golden flames crackling in her hands; she blasts Agatha right across the room in a fit of rage.

    “Oh for heaven’s sakes!” The blonde sighs in frustration. “You said you could keep it together!”

    “Did you hear what she said?”

    “Well, was she wrong?”

    “Ask me again and I’ll show you.”

    “Honestly, you two-”

    A lone, low chuckle silences the bickering.

    Agatha rises to her feet slowly, one hand steady on the wall she had been thrown into. The wall bearing one of the four sacred runes.

    Or it had, until Agatha scratched out its shape with her bare hands. The splinters buried deep beneath her nails are well worth it. With one of the runes corrupted, there is nothing containing her power now and it floods back to her with the strength of an ocean wave.

    “I told you,” she savours the way their faces pale with terror. “You don’t know what you’re messing with.”

    Her magic sparks violently around her body—small, erratic explosions of violet light that scorch the carpet and rattle the furniture.

    “Holy Mother-”

    The force blasts out from her body The maid is standing the closest and recieves the bulk of the blast; Agatha watches gleefully as the little twit is thoroughly obliterated. Meanwhile, the blonde flies across the cabin like a ragdoll. She smacks her head so hard into the opposite wall of the cabin that she leaves behind a bloody smear of brain matter as her corpse flops to floor.

    But Mrs Tycoon isn’t ready to surrender. Her silver magic coils around her, shielding her from the surge. And as she murmurs her incantation, those pale chains begin to snake toward Agatha.

    “Pereo in tua potestate…” Agatha thrusts her hands out but the Darkhold’s whispers surge in her ears, feeding on her panic and anger. Her magic roars out of her like a forest fire, aimless and hungry and devastating.

    She cannot control its path. The chains shatter. The cabin erupts.

    The world explodes in violet light and deafening noise as her magic rips apart the bedchamber, the cabin, the entire deck of the ship. The walls buckle. The floor splinters. There’s a shuddering groan of metal as the Titanic itself seems to roar in protest.

    Agatha has never wielded such sheer, colossal force before and with so little ability to control it. She has scarcely time to understand her own power before it has knocked her off her feet, slammed her into something hard and unforgiving-

    Agatha wakes, coughing up icy seawater.

    She is soaked to the bone and freezing cold. The cabin around her is unrecognizable—furniture splintered, walls warped, the door half-hanging from its hinges and an enormous gaping hole has been torn through the floor. But what really strikes her first is the vanished hum of the Titanic’s engines. The grand ship’s heartbeat is empty and the silence it leaves behind is unnatural, oppressive.

    The ship has stopped, Agatha thinks dizzily. Then: the ship is sinking.

    Head throbbing, she drags herself upright, clutching at the shattered remnants of a wooden dresser for support. It’s hard to tell how long it has been; perhaps an hour or two? The water swirls around her knees, rising fast.

    Thinking quickly, Agatha snatches at a large fur coat as it bobs past listless, the garment sopping wet with water. “Asciugar calefacie.” she commands, savouring the way it suddenly warms and dries out, impermeable to the seawater.

    But no sooner has she used the simple spell than the shadows begin to writhe in her periphery vision. They reach for her with clawed hands, with voices too faint to make out but sharp enough to slice through her nerves. Agatha wades toward the door, breath hitching as the water rises to her thighs.

    “It’s not real,” she mutters under her breath. “It’s not real.”

    But it feels real. The shadows grab at her arms as she wades through the wreckage, their touch cold and clammy. Her panic spikes as she flings them off but with every spark of her magic, they only seem to grow stronger.

    The hallway is worse. The narrow passage is a crush of panicked passengers, their loud voices (calling for loved ones, shouting for help, screaming with fear) reverberating off the walls.

    Agatha fights her way forward, fur coat warm but heavy. Her limbs tremble with exhaustion.

    She tries to push through the crowd, but their faces warp and twist in her vision. Are they real? Are they witches?

    A child cries out, and for a moment Agatha freezes, her heart lurching because she knows that sound, doesn’t she? She knows that cry-

    When she turns to look, she sees nothing but chaos—people scrambling, shoving, clawing their way toward the staircases. She shakes her head and keeps moving. Focus. Get to the deck. Get out.

    The whispers follow as she ascends, mingling with the screams and shouts of the passengers. By the time she reaches the upper deck, she’s gasping for breath, for relief from the cacophony of terror.

    The cold air hits her like a slap, shocking her back into focus.

    Hundreds of passengers clamour forward for the lifeboats but there are too few and they move too slowly down into the water. First-class passengers shove their way forward, ignoring the protests of the crew.

    “Do you know who I am?!”

    “My wife and daughter are on board, please, I have to-”

    “Stand aside this instant!”

    Agatha spots an opening in one boat and makes a desperate lunge for it, but a portly man still wearing his dinner jacket slams his shoulder into her, his considerable weight knocking her back.

    “Out of my way!” he shouts, climbing into the boat gracelessly.

    Agatha hits the deck hard, her head spinning. Her magic flares instinctively, a ripple of violet light that makes the man flinch, but it’s too erratic to do any real damage. She can’t trust it; she can’t control it, she can’t use it, she can’t-

    Agatha bites back a scream of frustration, fingers curling into fists. She has the most absurd impulse to laugh at the stupidity of it all: she has accrued more power than most witches ever dream, she has doomed an entire ship with it, but she cannot control it long enough to save herself-!

    “Well, this is quite the welcome home gift, my love.”

    The voice is soft, calm, and achingly familiar. She looks up to see Rio standing over her, hands in her coat pockets, her expression one of quiet amusement.

    Amongst the terror and frenetic movements, she is utterly, inhumanly still. “Rio?” Agatha gasps, her voice trembling. “You’re…you’re real, aren’t you? Please tell me you’re real…please…”

    She will not bear another vision, not now.

    Rio crouches down, her hand brushing Agatha’s shoulder. “I’m real. I promise. Now, get up.”

    Agatha lets Rio pull her to her feet and it is all she can do not to throw herself into Rio’s arms. Take me away, take me from here, please, she wants to beg. Agatha has not felt so out of control since she was a girl in Salem, fighting to be the witch her mother wanted.

    The weasel-faced man is still in the boat, staring at the pair of them warily from amidst his terrified fellow evacuees.

    Rio strides over, movements unhurried, and grabs him by the collar.

    “Get lost.” she says, her voice low and dangerous. And then she tosses him out of the boat with a single, effortless motion.

    Rio turns back to Agatha, her smirk softening. “Your chariot awaits, my sweet.”

    Agatha hesitates, chest almost collapsing with relief and gratitude and love. “Come with me, please, please, I need you, I don’t want to be alone.”

    Rio presses an achingly sweet kiss to her forehead and gently tugs the collar of Agatha’s warm dry coat closed. “I have work here, sweetheart.” She replies. “But you’ll be safe. And when I’m done here, I’ll meet you in New York.”

    Before Agatha can argue, the boat suddenly jolts, its chains creaking as it is lowered down toward the icy sea. She clings to the edge, her eyes locked on Rio the entire way down.

    “She…she’s staying behind?” one of the passengers asks quietly, a boy only a handful of years older than her last memory of Nicky. “Isn’t she scared?” Agatha swallows her terror, buries her hands in her coat. “No,” she finds herself saying. “She’s too brave. And if she says we’ll be safe, we’ll be safe.”

    The cold wind bites at her skin, but she barely feels it. Her hallucinations quiet, the whispers fade. But as their lifeboat bobs away, Agatha listens to the sounds of screams from across the eerily still waters as the Ship of Dreams moans and creaks and succumbs to its doom.

    She cannot decide which is worse.

    Chapter End Notes History check: The RMS Titanic struck the iceberg at 11:40 PM on April 14 1912, rupturing six watertight compartments and water poured into lower decks first, flooding the mailroom and boiler rooms, trapping crew and Third Class passengers.

    The ship sank within 2 hours and 40 minutes, and at first, many passengers were unaware of the severity of the damage. Some joked or resisted putting on life belts. The corridors were narrow, with limited visibility due to dim electric lights. Passengers from lower decks struggled to reach lifeboats, many of which launched half-full. The North Atlantic was near freezing (around -2°C/28°F). Passengers in the water succumbed to hypothermia within minutes.

    Just after midnight, lifeboats began launching. There were only 20 lifeboats, enough for about half the passengers. Women and children were given priority, leading to arguments especially as first class ticketholders were given priority access. Between 1:40–2:20am, the stern lifted high as the ship broke apart.

    Of the Titanic's 2,000 passengers, 706 survived.

    BOSTON, 1921 Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes BOSTON, MASSACHUSETTS Some witches may have thought it tacky and demeaning but Agatha could not help but find the rising interest in mysticism amongst mortals to be dreadfully – delightfully – amusing.

    The parlour she rents for this particular con is a gaudy, smoky mess of velvet drapes, mismatched furniture, and candles dripping wax onto every available surface. Wrapped in a silken turban adorned with a peacock feather, Agatha presides at the head of a round table, her face illuminated by the flickering light of a dozen stubby candles.

    She’d painted a dark smudge beneath each eye, giving herself the haunted, hollow look, she assumes her clients expected from a proper medium. In truth, the whole ensemble is less “all-knowing mystic” and more “pantomime ghost,” but her latest clients aren’t exactly discerning critics. The five of them sit around the table, all clasping hands obediently. Their knuckles are white from the sheer effort of believing. A plump woman in her forties, sporting a hat the size of a tea tray, trembles beside a man with a moustache so robust it deserves its own séance. Across from them, two young women clutch at one another, eyes wide with awe and fear, while a stern-looking banker with a monocle and permanent sneer looks on with ill- concealed skepticism.

    Goddess three, she loves the sceptics.

    “Spirits of the beyond,” Agatha intones, her voice deep and resonant. Her eyes roll back into her skull as though in a trance, the white glistening in the candlelight just so. “We call to thee from the mortal plane. We seek answers, guidance… perhaps closure.”

    The plump woman gives a soft, strangled sob, dabbing at her eyes with a lace handkerchief.

    Agatha files it away for later; tears were often a precursor to bigger tips. “Please!” the woman cries. “I must speak to my late husband, Harold. I must know if he forgives me!”

    Agatha resists the urge to blink, surprised but impressed at how neatly the woman has handed her the information she needed. Harold, is it? Easy enough.

    She places a hand over her heart, her expression softening into one of practiced pity. “Dearest woman, Harold’s spirit is restless, yearning to speak. But tell me… what might he need to forgive you for?”

    The banker snorts. “Is this necessary? Surely the spirits know the answer already.”

    Agatha shoots him a vicious glower. “The spirits do not enjoy being second-guessed, sir. And neither do I.” She leans forward, her tone ominous. “If you wish to antagonize the dead, be my guest. But do not complain when the consequences come knocking at your door in the middle of the night.”

    The banker’s sneer falters. “I see no need for dramatics.”

    “Then keep quiet,” Agatha snaps, before turning back to the woman with the hat. “Now, where were we? Ah, yes. Harold.”

    The woman swallows hard, her fingers gripping her handkerchief like it might float away. “I… I sold his prize-winning stamp collection after he died. For the rent, you see. I didn’t know it would fetch so much!”

    Agatha presses her lips together, suppressing the urge to laugh. She resists the temptation to suggest Harold’s spirit might haunt the local post office. “Harold’s feelings about the stamps are immaterial,” Agatha declares, boldly. “It is his love for you that binds him to this realm.”

    The woman sobs again, muttering, “Oh, Harold!”

    Beside her, Mustache Man interjects in a quivering voice: “And what of my brother, George? He perished in the War, God rest his soul. Does he… does he regret enlisting?”

    Agatha raises a dramatic hand, silencing him with a flourish. “The spirits speak not in regret, but in wisdom gained from their journey beyond the veil.”

    “That doesn’t really answer the question,” Monocle Banker mutters.

    Agatha slams her palm on the table, sending a few of the smaller candles toppling over. “Do you think communing with the dead is easy? Perhaps you’d like to try it, Mr. Doubter. Shall I summon a spirit to prove my abilities? Perhaps your own dear departed will grace us with their presence? Hmm?”

    The banker shrinks back, muttering, “I have no desire to be haunted, thank you.”

    “That’s what I thought,” Agatha says with a smug smile and just for flourish she allows her magic to right the candles with a flick of her wrist.

    The room goes silent and stunned, questioning whether they can believe their own eyes. She lets the tension in the room build for a few moments before exhaling deeply and slumping forward in her chair. “Ahhh,” she groans theatrically, her eyelids fluttering. “They are here. I can feel them…” The young women across the table squeal, clutching each other tighter.

    Agatha begins swaying gently, hands drifting over the table as though guided by unseen forces. She deliberately knocks a stack of tarot cards to the floor, leaving behind only one card on the table: skull-faced DEATH riding a white horse. Made by an old acquaintance of hers back in England, this version has long flowing black hair and a green flag. Far closer to the true face of Death, of course.

    Nevertheless, the group gasps at the sight of it.

    “The veil grows thin,” she whispers. “The spirits… they… oh!” She jolts upright, her eyes wide and unfocused. “Lucy?”

    “Harold?!” the plump woman cries. “Harold, is that you?!”

    “Aye, Lucy. It is me…” Agatha makes a small sobbing noise. “I’m sorry, he’s very distant.”

    “No!” she wails. “You had him! You had him- tell me, what does he say?

    Does he forgive me?”

    Agatha draws out the pause, letting the room fill with the weight of expectation. She toys with the idea of confirming the wife’s guilt, but in the end, the pain in the woman’s eyes is too sharp and fresh.

    “Sell them all, my dear.” She smiles softly, her voice tender. “He says… the stamps don’t matter. What matters is that you find happiness.”

    The woman bursts into grateful tears, clutching at Moustache Man for support.

    The banker scowls. “Rubbish! I knew Harold since we were boys and he would never have said the stamps don’t matter, they were his life’s passion!”

    “Richard, really,” Moustache Man sighs. “Is this the time?”

    “This woman is lying to us! I demand a refund of my money!” Banker continues angrily.

    Agatha’s patience thins and then snaps.

    Her hand flashes out, catches his lapel and drags him forward until they are nearly nose to nose. Revela memoria, she hisses from her mind, reaching into Banker’s skull and scooping out what she seeks to know.

    “Harold kept your secret, Ricky,” she sneers viciously. “He never told a soul about that mistress of yours you keep downtown. Sophia and her little brat, Esme, isn’t it? He kept your confidences and now all he asks is that you care for his widow in her hour of need. You can do that, can’t you?”

    Richard the Banker’s eyes are so large they are in danger of falling out of his socket entirely. “I-I-”

    Agatha smiles coldly and releases him. “I thought so.”

    Lucy blinks around her tears, bewildered by the exchange. “Richard? What do you-?”

    “I must go,” he splutters out, emptying his pockets of every cash note he carries. “I have to- I am- I have somewhere to be.”

    As he exits, Agatha leans back, satisfied and mutters some nonsense about the spirit world closing its doors for the evening.

    When the clients leave, they shower her with praise, thanks, and, most importantly, money. Added to the Banker’s fee, it’s a tidy sum for a night’s work.

    She gathers the money into a neat stack, tucking it away in her coat.

    Just as she reaches for a half-empty glass of wine on the mantel, she catches a glimpse of her reflection in the cracked mirror. For a moment, she freezes, staring at her own face. Wrapped in her ridiculous turban and fringed shawls, the dark smudges under her eyes look a little too real, her cheeks a little too hollow.

    She shakes her head, abandoning the glass. “Too much wine and too little sleep.” she mumbles to herself as she wipes off the makeup.

    And something tells her she needs her wits for what awaits her at home.

    Home is a small but well-furnished apartment on the east side of the city, in a neighbourhood not far from the apothecary she once shared with Rio during the smallpox epidemic almost precisely two centuries ago. Agatha enters quietly, shutting the door with more care than usual.

    Rain patters against the window, the glass lit by the dim glow of a single gas lamp. Beside it, Rio has taken up her usual spot in a large wingback armchair, head resting in one hand as she stares out at the slick, dark streets. Her other hand loosely grips the armrest, fingers twitching as though playing a piano only she could hear. The lamp flickers like a candle guttering in a breeze, casting jagged shadows across her face.

    Agatha sighs at the sight that greets her, the same sight that has greeted her every night for months and months and months.

    She leans down, brushes a kiss to the top of Rio’s head, against her oily, unkempt hair. “Hello, sweetheart.”

    “Do you hear them?” Rio asks, not turning away from the window.

    Agatha pauses, her brow furrowing. “Hear what?”

    “The voices,” Her fingers now drumming softly against glass. “They’re screaming."

    "There are no voices, my love."

    "So many..." she breathes. "They...I can't hold them all. They just spill over my edges, Agatha..." She stops, closing her eyes and pressing her palm to her forehead.

    Agatha frowns, setting her bag down on the nearby table. “Rio, darling. The war is over, remember?”

    The Great War, the War to End All War, the Last War. Agatha has heard it called all sorts of things and each one fails to capture just how obliterative the conflict had proven. Millions dead, cities decimated. Rio is no stranger to violence in the slightest, but the sheer volume has proven overwhelming; the sacred balance she always upheld with such integrity and reverence has been thrown into disarray.

    Agatha has never seen her lover so unhinged. Rio is better than during the War, at least. But three years on and Rio’s disjointed ramblings continue to swing between despondence and fury with no warning. It is as though something inside her has fractured.

    “Not for them,” When Rio’s eyes open, there is no hint of life in them. Only voids filled with shadow. “Not for me.”

    She wants to be gentle. She wants to soften her edges, she wants to be a comfort.

    But her voice is sharp all the same. "You can't wallow like this. Not again." "You can't understand it. It's beyond you." Rio stares at her, her eyes dull. “Try,” Agatha half-snarls, half-begs. “Just try and I'll listen, I'll help-” "Help." Rio echoes the word with such disdain. "What help could you offer me?"

    Agatha refuses to show her flinch. "What help is this doing?" she demands instead. "Sitting here like a mad woman, curled in your own contempt, as though this is the first time men have slaughtered one another for you."

    "This is different." Rio says stubbornly.

    "How? You’ve managed before,” Agatha insists. “This isn’t the first war ever waged. It won’t even be the last.”

    "You can't feel it." she snaps, half to herself in a neurotic tone. "Of course you can't feel it. It's not just men they tore apart this time...There's an order, there's always been an order..."

    “Rio, please, you have to move on.” She knows she sounds impatient but she's at a loss.

    “There is no moving on!” Rio throws herself out of the chair, staggering to the far side of the living room. She has taken to wearing her long green sigil robe constantly without repairing it; the odour alone is foul, the smell of slow decay and rot.

    Agatha sighs and rubs her eyes. “What can I do about it? I can’t bring them back, I can’t set this to right-”

    “It’s all about you,” Rio spits caustically. “I forgot, the universe spins on your whim, doesn’t it? Or at least it used to.”

    “What is that supposed to mean?” Agatha demands.

    “Where were you tonight?” Rio says instead of answering. “Conning a few mortals out of money? It’s pathetic, Agatha. You’re a witch, not a carnie.” “It pays the rent, not that you’d know anything about it.” Agatha mutters tensely.

    But Rio isn’t even listening. “Or were you selling those potions again, huh?”

    “Those are a necessity.” Agatha’s temper sizzles with the words.

    “A necessity,” Rio’s expression darkens. “Right.”

    “I’m doing what I have to do to survive.” Agatha explodes. “You think I like living like this? Looking over my shoulder every second of every day, wondering who’s coming for me next? Because they will, you know. They always do.” She adds in a mutter.

    “You sound insane,” Rio taunts, prowling closer. There’s life in those eyes again but none of the softness she used to have when she looked at Agatha. “Destroying witches for what they might do. You know who that sounds like.”

    “Don’t you dare!” Agatha seethes at the mere mention of her mother, violet magic crackling at her fingertips now.

    “That damned book of yours has its hooks so deep in you and you don’t even know it.” Rio steps toward Agatha; her cloak absorbs the pale light of the lamp like a void. “That’s where the binding potions come from, isn’t it?”

    “You didn’t seem to have a problem with it when I was gift wrapping them for you,” Agatha snaps. “What? Upset I’m not leaving presents any more, darling?”

    “They attacked you, Agatha. They gave you no choice. But what you’re doing now? Binding witches who’ve done nothing to you? That’s not defence, it’s-”

    Agatha cuts her off viciously. “It’s survival!”

    Rio’s laugh is sharp and bitter. “It’s cowardice, Agatha. Plain and simple.” Agatha’s voice rises, trembling with anger. “Do you know how many have tried to kill me? How many I’ve had to kill to protect myself? Do you?!”

    “And who’s fault is that?” Rio snarls back, voice rising to match Agatha’s. “You could have walked away. You could have destroyed the Darkhold or locked it away where no one could find it. But no, you had to keep it. You had to keep chasing its promises, its power, no matter the cost.”

    Agatha’s breath hitches, and for a moment, she falters. “Don’t you dare lecture me about cost, Rio.”

    Rio paces back toward her armchair with a manic sort of energy, her shoulders hunched like a caged beast. “You’re letting it consume you. The Darkhold doesn’t care about you, Agatha. It’ll chew you up and spit you out like it has every other fool who thought they could master it.”

    Agatha flinches as though struck, her face pale with fury and pain. But when she stands, her heart is hard as stone. “You have no idea what I can master.”

    Rio won’t look at her, just resumes her vigil at the window with unnerving focus. “The Darkhold is corrupting you. It’s turning you into something you don’t even recognize.”

    Agatha stares at her, her chest heaving, her mind racing. She wants to fight back, to argue, but the words won’t come. Finally, she turns away, grabbing her coat. “If you think I’m such a monster, maybe you’re better off without me.”

    St Michael’s Hospital reeks of bleach, sickness, and desperation.

    Agatha wrinkles her nose as she steps through its heavy wooden doors, her heels clicking against the scuffed tile floor. The hallways bustle with the graveyard shift of nurses in stiff white uniforms and harried doctors who barely spare her a glance.

    She tucks a stray lock of hair beneath her burgundy cloche hat and adjusts her black leather gloves while she allows herself upstairs to the second floor offices.

    “Dr. Langston,” she murmured to herself, scanning the names stencilled on the frosted glass panes of the doors she passed. Ah. There it is.

    The lettering on the door reads Dr. Horace Langston, Obstetrics and Gynaecology in neat, gold letters. Beneath it, in slightly smaller font, is a flourish: Founding Member of the Greater Boston Obstetrics Association, Fellow of the Royal College of Physicians.

    Agatha rolls her eyes. Clearly someone was rather proud of himself.

    The office is as pompous as the man himself, she decides almost instantly. Dark mahogany shelves line one wall, crammed with leather-bound medical textbooks, their spines embossed with gold. Certificates and diplomas framed in thick wood crowd the opposite wall, each angled just so to ensure no visitor could miss their significance.

    Agatha meanders toward the diploma from Harvard Medical School, and leans in to inspect it. The ink is faded, but the signature of some long-dead dean has been lovingly preserved under the glass.

    “Harvard,” she sniffs under her breath. “Of course.”

    Her gaze drifts to a small photograph on the desk: a younger Langston, smiling smugly in front of a hospital much like this one, shaking hands with another man in a bowler hat. He probably framed that handshake too.

    Agatha settles into the leather chair behind his desk, kicking her feet up on the wooden surface.

    She summons a tiny orb of electric purple light, balances it the tip of her finger with a carefully constructed restraint that has taken years to master. She is still in the process of mastering the Darkhold’s second chapter, she knows. But she has conquered the madness, hasn’t she? It’s not paranoia if there truly are mysterious figures waiting to attack?

    Binding these women isn’t an act of treachery, it is survival. That’s all witchcraft is: surviving when everyone else would count you out.

    Her thought wander to the next chapter of the Darkhold, the final part she has chosen not to reveal even to Rio. Chaos & Rebirth. Agatha has never come across chaos magic herself. In fact, no one in living memory has encountered it. But she knows it is the spark that produces all life in the universe. If she can master it, conquer it, wield it…Perhaps she could bring life back to the one person she most desperately wishes to see again.

    Chaos and rebirth…it is the last chance she has to see her boy again, to hold Nicky.

    She cannot share the secret with Rio, not in the least because she isn’t sure how this might upset her precious balance. If the Darkhold holds the power to give her back her son, Rio certainly is not aware of it else she would’ve already lit the damn thing on fire.

    Agatha has to play her cards carefully here. The first step is to master what she has unlocked so far and if that means binding witches to prevent them from stealing the Darkhold from her before she has her chance, then that is what she’ll do.

    She toys with some paperwork on the good doctor’s desk, including a pamphlet for the obstetrics association he supposedly founded. Dr.

    Langston is a clever man, but not as clever as he thinks he is. When he first called on her, he let slip that there’s a midwife interfering with his business, a witch who’d been helping those turned away from Langston’s care—poor women, unwed women, women who asked too many questions.

    Agatha suspects Langston is not a man who handles competition with grace, least of all when that competition is a woman with opinions. It must gall him terribly to admit that the problem is beyond his control. He needs her—Agatha, the outsider, the witch—and that is probably what annoys him most of all.

    The door swings open with a sharp creak, cutting her musings short.

    Langston enters, his coat tails fluttering as he strides in, followed closely by a younger doctor carrying a clipboard. Agatha recognizes the type immediately: eager, fresh-faced, and so hungry for approval it is almost pitiful.

    She gives a little fluttering wave with a smile. “Well, hi there.”

    Langston stops short, face twisting into a look of irritation that he quickly smooths into an unconvincing smile. “Miss Harkness,” his voice is clipped. “You’re… early.”

    Agatha rises, smoothing her skirt. “You’re welcome.” she replies, letting the sarcasm hang in the air.

    The younger doctor blinks, clearly unsure of what to make of her. “Dr.

    Langston,” he enquires hesitantly, “Should I reschedule your meeting with Mrs. Caldwell? She’s waiting in—”

    “No need,” Langston interrupts, ushering the younger man back through the door. “Miss Harkness was just… dropping off a parcel for me. A personal errand.”

    Agatha raises an eyebrow but says nothing as the younger man glances between them, clearly unconvinced. “Of course, sir,” he says finally, ducking out of the room and pulling the door shut behind him.

    The moment they are alone, Langston’s polished demeanour cracks. “You were supposed to come tomorrow.” he hisses, rounding the desk to glare down at her. “I can’t have you traipsing into my office without warning.” Agatha peers up at him, her feet still on his desk. “Oh, forgive me, Doctor. I must have misplaced the etiquette handbook on how to secretly conspire with morally bankrupt men.”

    Langston’s nostrils flare. “Do you think this is a joke? If anyone finds out about this—”

    “What?” she interrupts, standing so swiftly he is forced to stagger back. “What, Horace? What happens if someone finds out you’ve been hiring a witch to solve your problems? Will Harvard revoke your precious diploma?

    Will your patients whisper that you’re not as brilliant as you claim to be?” She leans in, her voice dropping to a dangerous purr. “Or is it that midwife you’re really afraid of? That she might undo all your hard work of keeping Boston’s women dependent on you?”

    Langston flinches but quickly recovers, smoothing his coat with a sharp tug. “You have no idea what you’re talking about. This is about public safety. That woman is dangerous.”

    Agatha cackles loudly. “Spare me the sermon, Doctor. We both know you don’t give a damn about public safety.”

    He fumes. “This is unacceptable-”

    In an instant, her hands crackle with violet magic and all at once, every diploma in the room explodes into smithereens.

    With a yelp, Langston freezes but she can see his pulse racing in his throat. “You forget, doctor. I don’t work for you,” Agatha leans in ever closer, toying with her prey. “I tolerate you. Now, where’s my payment?”

    Langston’s breathing is shallow as he shakily opens a drawer in his desk. Inside, he withdraws a thick envelope, and tosses it onto the desk. “As agreed.”

    Agatha doesn’t move right away, allows the silence to stretch to nearly unbearable tension between them. She considers killing him in that moment, bringing his body home to Rio as a peace offering. I love you, she would say. I don’t want to fight. Look, I brought you a misogynist!

    The binding potion burns in her pocket. She could destroy it. She could break the dozen odd binding spells she’s already let loose into the city. Those witches who seek her out, who attack first, none of them have ever come close to stealing the Darkhold, not really.

    But it only takes one, a voice inside her whispers. And then you lose any chance of seeing Nicky again.

    She snatches the envelope and tosses the binding potion onto the table.

    “You’ll need to dilute in water and have her drink the lot.”

    His eyes track the tiny bottle hungrily. “I see. That’s all?”

    “That’s it.” Agatha takes great care to keep her tone flippant and careless, but it sharpens on her next words: “But as a reminder, the next time you speak to a woman like that, I’ll make sure your name ends up in a séance or two. Imagine what your patients will think when their dearly departed start whispering about you.”

    Langston pales slightly but didn’t reply. Agatha smirks, turning on her heel and heading for the door.

    “Oh, and Horace?” she calls over her shoulder. “I’ll be sending you a bill for the extra trouble.”

    She doesn’t wait for his response, simply lets the door slam shut behind her as she exits.

    Rio was right about that much. Power requires sacrifice. The Darkhold demanded a cost. But if it means seeing Nicky again, Agatha will pay whatever is required of her.

    Chapter End Notes History check: - World War I (also known as the Great War, the Last War and the War to end all Wars) was the largest military conflict the world had seen up to that time. It was one of the deadliest conflicts in history, with an estimated 40 million military and civilian casualties. This included over 8 million soldiers and sailors who died, and more than 20 million who were injured. This was due in no small part to new military technologies, such as trench warfare, artillery, machine guns, and chemical weapons, which resulted in unprecedented carnage.

    When we talk about the natural order of things, you can bet that WWI wiped the floor with that balance. Considering Rio's goal isn't necessarily carnage but rather balance and order, WWI would have fucked her up badly.

    - Mysticism and a fascination with the occult became extremely fashionable in the 1920s in North American and Western Europe, which gave rise to new trends like seances and spiritual readings. For example, Harry Houdini actually very famously did his best to discredit these psychics and mediums because he did not believe in magic in the slightest. Close friend of his Sir Arthur Conan Doyle (the man who wrote Sherlock Holmes) however was not only convinced magic existed but that Houdini was himself magical, even when the man tried to refute it.

    HOLLYWOOD HILLS, 1938 Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes HOLLYWOOD HILLS, CALIFORNIA Honestly, you bring down one little air balloon and suddenly you’re the villain.

    It’s hardly her fault that hydrogen was so combustible. The stupid thing was leaking well before Agatha got involved. And if Rio hadn’t been pressing on her every nerve that day- Whatver. To make a long story short, she figured it was time for a change. Maybe a little sunlight and frivolous mischief. Which makes California the perfect destination.

    Smirking at her reflection in the polished chrome of the car she has just stepped out of, Agatha adjusts her brooch, pinned at the centre of the striking V-neckline of her silken gown. The plum toned fabric is so perfectly cut on the bias that it hugs her every curve, shimmering with every step.

    “Keep the change,” she calls over her shoulder to the driver who blinks back, bewildered as to how he has found himself all the way in the Hollywood Hills.

    The mansion before her is a temple to indulgence. Cream stucco walls gleam under strings of Edison bulbs draped like constellations across the garden courtyard. Wrought iron gates swing open to reveal a driveway lined with polished Packards and Cadillacs, chauffeurs milling about in the cool night air. Laughter and jazz spill over the balcony railing, mingling with the scent of orange blossoms and cigarette smoke.

    The house belongs to someone famous—perhaps a director or a studio executive—but the name escapes her. No matter. The crowd gathered here is the who’s-who of Tinseltown: stars, producers, writers, and their respective hangers-on. Prime prey for a little meddling.

    Agatha sweeps through the party, a ripple of conversation following her through the garden as whispers dart from one sequined gown to the next.

    The men, dressed in impeccably tailored dinner jackets, cast appraising glances. The women, coiffed and gloved, clutch their champagne flutes with a mix of curiosity and envy.

    Agatha indulges in their envy, throwing a wink here and a flirtatious smile there. She’s only been in town a few weeks but long enough to learn that everyone here wears some kind of mask. Everyone is playing a role, a game. Agatha likes games, likes winning. Tonight she’s not interested in Agatha the witch or the fugitive or the coven slayer. She doesn’t want to be a childless mother or the great love of Death or the wielder of the Darkhold. Tonight, she is simply another mysterious beauty, a sparkling accessory to Hollywood’s collective vanity.

    She reaches the bar, where a boyishly handsome man holds court, a glass of bourbon in one hand and a cigar in the other. He inspects her from head to toe as she approaches, his grin broadening.

    “Mervyn LeRoy,” he says, offering a hand. “You must be new to town. I never forget a face, and I’d definitely remember yours.”

    Agatha’s smile is polished and sharp. “Agatha Harkness,” she replies smoothly. “And you must be the man everyone’s talking about.”

    A lie, but a convincing one. She has found that Hollywood’s players love to think they are the centre of every conversation, including the ones happening behind their backs.

    “Always,” LeRoy says with a wink. “Now tell me, are you here as a starlet or someone’s scandalous paramour?”

    “Wouldn’t you like to know?” she teases, taking a sip of champagne offered by the bartender.

    LeRoy laughs, clearly charmed. “You’re going to fit right in.” He leans closer, lowering his voice. “Let me introduce you to someone. Gale!”

    From the shadows of the garden, a woman emerges, her raven-black hair swept into glossy waves and a slinky silver gown clinging to her frame like a second skin. Her sharp features seem almost sculpted, her lips painted the colour of blood.

    Agatha tenses ever so slightly at her approach. She might look like a fairy- tale queen but Agatha knows a witch when she sees one.

    “Gale, this is Miss Agatha Harkness,” LeRoy says. “Agatha, Gale Sondergaard, one of MGM’s finest talents.”

    “Oh, Mervyn, don’t embarrass me,” Gale says, though her smile betrays her pleasure at the flattery. She turns her attention to Agatha, her gaze piercing. “Charmed, I’m sure.”

    “Gale’s starring in my next production,” LeRoy brags loudly. “A fairy tale adventure in technicolour, no less!”

    Agatha allows him to ramble on about the project (an adaptation of some children’s book involving witches and wizards and scarecrows or some such) but her eyes flit back again and again to the lovely Ms Sondergaard. Her beauty creates an aura of sorts around her; passers-by are helplessly drawn into her, magnetised.

    Glamours, Agatha suspects. Powerful ones, cast by a particularly dedicated witch. She’s faintly impressed by their clever design.

    The party swirls on. LeRoy’s sycophants drift toward the far end of the garden, where a live band has begun to play a sultry jazz number. Agatha finds herself chatting flirtatiously with actors and producers, writers and starlets, basking in their attention for a while at least.

    But the high never lasts. The role of Mysterious Beauty is a thin one, superficial like everyone else here. It starts to chafe and itch like an ill- fitting costume and Agatha withdraws to the bar where she can study the other partygoers as they walk the same circuits, laugh at the same jokes, whisper the same little snippets of gossip.

    It’s been a little over three months since she’d last held Rio. Longer still since she’d heard Rio’s laugh or seen her smile. The mortals called it ‘shellshock’ to refer to those soldiers who returned shaken and unhinged from their time at war. And then, just as she seemed to be recovering, whispers begin to rise from Europe once more: a new enemy rising out of the ashes, fuelled by a vicious, bloody hatred.

    War is coming again. Rio knows it, she can sense it. And all Agatha can do is watch her love unravel all over again, helpless to change it or fix it or prevent it. She hates being helpless. She hates Rio for making her helpless. Her champagne glass sparkles under the string lights as she twirls it absently, watching the bubbles rise.

    “A woman like you shouldn’t be drinking alone.” The voice is low, velvety, and tinged with amusement. Sondergaard stands beside her, a faint smirk playing on her crimson lips.

    “I suppose that depends on whether I’m drinking for company or for peace,” Agatha says, raising her glass in a languid toast.

    Gale laughs softly, the sound warm and throaty. She holds herself with the effortless poise of someone who knows precisely the effect she has on others.

    “Peace? At a party like this? Darling, you’ve come to the wrong place.”

    Agatha smiles faintly, though her eyes sharpen as she takes in the woman before her. She’s never quite managed to shake her suspicion of other witches, even after all these years. But beauty spells and glamours are hardly a threat.

    “You’re not like the others, are you?” Gale leans against the bar, her posture languid as a feline. But her eyes are keen and curious.

    “Neither are you,” Agatha counters.

    Gale tilts her head, studying Agatha like she is a particularly rare curio, as if she ought to be set in a museum under glass. “I’ve heard whispers. You have quite the reputation, Miss Harkness.”

    “You’ll have to be more specific. I’ve had several reputations over the years.” Agatha demurs with a shrug. It’s not unpleasurable, being the centre of a beautiful woman’s attention.

    “How intriguing,” Gale murmurs, sipping her martini. “Well, how about I guess?”

    Agatha’s brow twitches in amusement. “Go on then.”

    The actress purses her lips in thought. “I heard you were born amongst the Salemites.”

    Agatha inclines her head slightly. “True enough.”

    “That you rode with Washington himself?” her smile lengthens. “Or rather that you rode Washington himself?”

    Agatha lets out a chuckle. “He should have been so lucky.”

    “I see.” Gale’s eyes gleam. “What about that fire in Chicago?”

    She blinks back innocently. “I heard that was started by a cow.”

    Gale laughs brightly, the sound just as exquisite as she is. “You are a tonic, Miss Harkness. But you simply must tell me if it is true.”

    “If what is true?”

    Gale leans in, eyes fixed on her like an adder fixating on prey. “Why, the Road, of course.”

    The word hits Agatha like a crack of thunder. Her grip tightens on her glass, though she keeps her expression carefully neutral.

    “You think I’d share all my secrets with a stranger at a party,” she says, her tone light but edged with steel.

    “Stranger?” Gale leans in slightly, her voice dropping to a conspiratorial whisper. “Oh, darling, we’re far from strangers. We’re practically sisters, aren’t we? Hollywood is full of us—glamour spells, binding charms, deals made in shadows. It’s practically a coven unto itself.”

    Agatha smiles thinly. “Well, I’ve never been one for covens. I’m a one-

    witch show.”

    “Yes, the rumours seem to suggest so.” Gale tosses her hair casually. “I wonder: did you walk the Road alone?”

    “You seem very curious about it.” Agatha says around clenched teeth.

    “Keen to wander it yourself, are you?”

    Gale laughs, rich and unapologetic. “Me? Darling, look around. I have everything I could ever want and more. No, my interest is purely for my own curiosity.”

    “Well, you know what they say about curiosity killing the cat.” Agatha mutters.

    “Touché. But never underestimate the satisfaction that brought it back” She leans in, clinking her martini glass against Agatha’s champagne flute. “To curiosity, then.”

    “To… curiosity,” Agatha murmurs, lifting her glass. The crystal rim brushes her lips as she sips, the effervescent liquid sliding down her throat like silk. “So how have you enjoyed our little soiree?” Gale asks politely.

    It’s been a gas, she wants to say but other words begin to spill out, unbidden.

    “Honestly, it’s dreadful,” Agatha says, her tone light, almost conversational. “So much pretence, so much desperation. I mean, I’ve crashed my share of events, pretending I’m someone else—anyone else – but they’ve all picked the same character, having the same conversation over and over.”

    Gale arches a brow, a flicker of amusement brightening her sharp eyes. “Go on.”

    Agatha’s jaw tightens, but the words spill out as if dragged by some unseen force. “I thought it would feel good, pretending but everything catches up to you eventually. Not that I’m running from anything. I’m not. Rio’s the one who ran first, practically threw herself into chaos. The world’s spinning out of balance again, and she’s off brooding about it, like that’s all she is, like that’s all she can be. This stupid goddess-damned balance, the natural order she’s tearing herself to pieces over and what kills me is that it’ll never end! Just millions and millions of dead mortals, and Rio at the centre of it all, miserable and unhinged and-”

    Gale tilts her head, curiosity sharpening her gaze. “Rio. Is she your…?”

    “Nothing to you,” Agatha snaps, the heat rushing to her cheeks as she realizes how much she’s already said. Her fingers tighten around the champagne flute, her pulse quickening.

    “Oh, but she’s everything to you,” Gale counters smoothly in a voice like velvet. “Fascinating. Tell me, is that why you walked the Witches’ Road?

    To prove yourself worthy of her? Or was it something else entirely?”

    Agatha’s breath hitches. She wants to lash out, to reduce Gale’s martini glass to ash, but she forces herself to remain still; she bites her tongue until she tastes blood in her mouth.

    Gale leans in, pressing her advantage. “Some say the Road demanded more than you bargained for. That you sacrificed your son to claim your prize.” The mention of Nicky hits Agatha like a thunderclap. Her head snaps toward Gale, her eyes blazing. “Enough.”

    Even as the word leaves her lips, a realization dawns. Her tongue feels too loose, her thoughts too easily plucked. Her gaze shifts to the champagne flute in her hand, then back to Gale, whose smirk sharpens like the edge of a blade.

    “You slipped me something,” Agatha says, her voice low and dangerous.

    Gale doesn’t flinch. If anything, she looks amused. “Just a little truth serum. Nothing too potent, I assure you. Just enough to make the night interesting.” Agatha glowers at her. “Why?”

    “Whispers are my currency, darling,” Gale replies, swirling her martini with maddening ease. “And you’re a treasure trove. Hollywood might run on dollars and stars, but for us? Secrets and truths are worth far more.”

    Agatha stares, anger and humiliation coiling in her chest. Gale meets her glare without a flicker of remorse, her brazen confidence setting Agatha’s teeth on edge.

    “You think you can use my truths to barter?” Agatha asks, her tone clipped and ice-cold. “For what? A better part in some two-bit production?”

    Gale flashes her a sly smile. “Our circles overlap more than you realize, Agatha. And don’t look so put out—I didn’t force you to share anything untrue. Besides, what could the great Agatha Harkness possibly have to fear from a little gossip?”

    Agatha’s fingers itch to cast a hex, to strip the smugness from Gale’s face. Instead, she sets her glass down with a sharp clink and steps closer, her voice dropping to a venomous whisper.

    “You’re right about one thing,” she says. “You’ve no idea what it means to truly walk the Road.”

    For a moment, Gale’s smile wavers, a flicker of unease breaking through her polished facade. Then, just as quickly, it returns, brighter and sharper than before. “Maybe not. But I know how to walk a stage, darling. And here in Hollywood, that’s all that matters.”

    Agatha’s eyes narrow as Gale raises her martini in a mocking toast before slipping back into the crowd, leaving behind the faint trace of jasmine and smoke.

    Agatha turns on her heel, her jaw set, her heart hammering in her chest. If Gale thinks she can use tonight against her, she’s sorely mistaken. As her gaze lands on Mervyn LeRoy, laughing at the centre of a small, adoring crowd, an idea begins to take shape.

    A slow, cold smile spreads across her face. Let Gale learn what it truly means to cross Agatha Harkness.

    The chaos of the set ebbs into a low murmur, like the distant hum of an approaching storm. Agatha reclines in the director’s chair, one leg crossed over the other, a coffee cup balanced elegantly in her hand.

    The faint scent of greasepaint mingles with ozone as sparks crackle near a technician testing the fireball rig. Their new Witch, clad in the heavy green makeup and black gown of her new persona, paces in the distance, practicing her cackle.

    Agatha spent a few days with Margaret, coaching her on the finer points of the character: she’s monstrous, she’s despicable, she has only ever loved one thing and it has been destroyed and now she cares for nothing but her own survival and power.

    The session had been quite cathartic, to be honest.

    Mervyn LeRoy bustles toward her, his face a mixture of irritation and apprehension. He stops short, wringing his hands as he glances from Agatha to the towering Technicolor backdrop.

    “Are you sure about this?” he asks, leaning closer to avoid being overheard. “I mean, making her this witch? Scrapping Gale, the green, the voice—it’s a big swing, Harkness.”

    Agatha tilts her head lazily, swirling the coffee in her cup. “And when it succeeds, you’ll be a genius.”

    Mervyn shoots her a look that is half suspicion, half desperation.

    She thinks back to the conversation in his office. A day after the party, he’d invited her in, reluctantly intrigued by her bold suggestion to reimagine the Wicked Witch.

    She’d perched on the edge of his desk, legs crossed, her voice a low purr as she laid out her case.

    “She’s not a femme fatale, Mervyn,” Agatha had said, leaning forward just enough to draw his attention. “She’s the Wicked Witch. Let her be wicked. Let her terrify. No one will remember a glamorous sorceress draped in silk. But they’ll remember a monstrous hag who could haunt their nightmares.”

    When his hesitation lingered, she let a hint of magic lace her words, just enough to tip the scales. “Trust me,” she’d whispered, her fingers brushing the air in front of him as if sealing the deal. “This is how legends are made.”

    Now, watching him fidget, she smiles to herself.

    “Mervyn,” she says lightly, waving a hand as if dismissing his concerns, “if you’re going to second-guess every brilliant decision I make, you’ll never get anywhere in this town. Relax. This is going to work.”

    Before he can reply, an assistant approaches with a clipboard, asking about the next setup. Agatha waves them off, gesturing vaguely toward the craft services table.

    “Get another round of pastries while you’re at it,” she says, turning back to Mervyn with a smirk. “It’ll settle your nerves.”

    Mervyn mutters something unintelligible and hurries off, leaving Agatha to savour her victory.

    The moment is short-lived. She feels Rio’s presence before she sees her, the air around her chair cooling slightly, the faintest whiff of moss and wet earth teasing her senses. When she turns her head, there Rio is—leaning against the side of her chair in a simple sage green day suit, arms crossed, her expression grim.

    “You’re enjoying yourself,” Rio says, her tone even but pointed.

    “And why shouldn’t I?” Agatha replies, raising her coffee as if in toast. “I’ve earned it.”

    Rio arches a brow, her silence heavy with implication.

    Agatha sighs, setting her cup down and shifting slightly in her chair.

    “What? You came all this way just to scold me for having fun? Don’t tell me you’re still brooding over our little tiff.”

    “That’s what we’re calling blowing up half of New Jersey now? A tiff?”

    Agatha rolls her eyes. “You say that like it was deliberate.”

    “It wasn’t deliberate,” Rio says, her gaze unrelenting. “But it happened. And we both know why.”

    Agatha glares at her, the tension between them crackling. “What do you want from me, Rio? A confession? Fine. I let my temper get the better of me. Happy now?”

    For a moment, the noise of the set fades into the background. The director is shouting orders, Margaret is rehearsing her lines, but all Agatha can hear is the sound of her own heartbeat pounding in her ears.

    She glances away, her gaze falling to the coffee cup in her lap. “I don’t like this,” she mutters.

    “What?”

    “This,” Agatha says, gesturing vaguely between them. “You standing there, judging me. Like you don’t understand me.”

    Rio’s expression softens, but only slightly. “Then help me understand.”

    Agatha exhales sharply. “It’s not that simple.”

    “It’s never that simple with you,” Rio says, her voice low.

    Agatha hesitates, the words catching in her throat. She glances around the set, searching for a distraction, but there’s no escape. Finally, she looks back at Rio, her chest tightening as she forces herself to speak.

    “I worry about you.” she admits, the words tasting bitter on her tongue.

    Agatha has long shed her capacity for softness, her edges honed even further by the Darkhold, by the legend of the Road, by the loss of Nicky. But she tries. Goddess-three, how she tries to be soft here.

    As if sensing the precarious note in her voice, Rio goes very, very still. “I’m scared I’m going to lose you again,” Agatha continues. “And I’m scared of what I’ll become if I do.”

    Rio steps closer, her gaze softening. “Agatha…”

    “Let me get this out,” Agatha cuts her off sharper than she intends. “I’m not blind, Rio. I know the Darkhold has changed me. You think I don’t see the monster the rest of the world sees? Of course I do.” She can’t meet Rio’s eyes. “But I always thought you could see through it. That you saw me.”

    “I see you, mi vida.” Rio murmurs. “Always.”

    “Do you?” she challenges bitterly. “Because lately, the way you look at me…”

    “You know how hard it’s been,” Rio snaps back, her own frustration rising swift to the surface. “It’s impossible to explain how it feels, Agatha. It’s like I’m being torn in half. Like I’m drowning when I should be breathing-”

    Agatha finally looks up at her, incredulous. “That’s grief, Rio. Of course, I understand grief.”

    Rio looks away first, pondering her words. “It’s…it’s unbearable.”

    “Let me bear it with you.” Agatha pleads and she hates it, she hates begging like this. But there’s something in Rio’s face, something so terribly familiar. “You used to call me strong, remember? Let me be strong for you.”

    “We both know you’re not chasing all this power for me, Agatha.” Rio says quietly.

    “Maybe not,” Agatha agrees wryly. “But I’d use it for you. I’d be a monster for you.”

    Rio is silent for a long moment, her gaze searching Agatha’s face. Then, slowly, she kneels beside her chair, the posture supplicant, worshipful. Like a devotee taking communion, like an acolyte praying to a higher power.

    Rio presses a kiss to her hand, the gesture both reverent and grounding.

    When she looks up at her, there is something unspoken in her eyes, a confession lingering on her lips. “Agatha, I…I have to tell—”

    But before either of them can say another word, the director’s voice rings out across the set. “Quiet, everyone! We’re rolling!”

    The moment shatters like glass. Agatha’s hand falls back to her lap as Rio rises, the spell between them broken.

    Rio hesitates, then steps back, her expression unreadable. Agatha watches her retreating form, her chest tight with unspoken words. Come back. I’ll be better. We can be better. Come back. Stay.

    But then the soundstage buzzes to life as the call for action rings out.

    Margaret Hamilton is hoisted up on the rig, her broomstick wobbling slightly as the technicians adjust her wires. A hush falls over the crew as she steadies herself, green makeup shimmering under the studio lights. And then she begins to cackle.

    It’s a sharp, resonant laugh, rising and falling like a manic melody. The sound slices through the silence, electric and alive. It sends a ripple through the crew—some stifle grins, others exchange glances, and even Mervyn pauses mid-stride, his tension momentarily forgotten.

    Rio, halfway to the shadows at the edge of the set, freezes. Her head tilts slightly, her brow furrowing.

    Slowly, she turns back toward Agatha, her dark eyes narrowing in suspicion.

    “Why do I know that laugh?” she asks, her voice low enough not to disturb the take.

    Agatha leans back in her chair, her expression the picture of innocence, though her lips curl in a sly little smile.

    “Do you?” she asks, taking a lazy sip from her lukewarm coffee.

    Rio’s gaze sharpens. “Agatha…”

    Agatha shrugs, unbothered. “I might’ve coached her on it,” she says lightly, her fingers making a vague fluttering gesture.

    Rio blinks, caught off guard. “Coached her? Why?”

    Agatha’s smile softens, and for once, there’s no mischief in her eyes, only warmth. She looks up at Rio, her voice quieter now, almost tender.

    “I’ve always loved your laugh,” she admits. “And now it's recorded. For me to hear whenever I like.”

    Rio’s lips part slightly, but she doesn’t speak, caught in the unexpected intimacy of the moment.

    “Even,” Agatha continues, her voice dropping further, “When you don’t feel much like laughing yourself.”

    For a long moment, neither of them moves. The bustle of the set fades into the background, the cackle echoing faintly as the take continues.

    Rio doesn’t say a word. Instead, her hand moves almost imperceptibly, reaching out. Her fingers brush against Agatha’s, then fold gently around them.

    Agatha doesn’t pull away. For once, she simply holds on, her fingers curling slightly in return.

    The cackling on set crescendos to a glorious peak, and Agatha allows herself the faintest smile.

She is widely thought to be the inspiration for the Evil Queen in Disney's Snow White and the glamorous femme fatale archetype was definitely her brand as an actress. But Producer Mervyn LeRoy did indeed change the role dramatically just a week before filming began in October 1938, resulting in Sondergaard dropping out.

    She was not, to my knowledge, a witch.

    igniting hydrogen that had leaked from a gas cell and mixed with oxygen from the air. 37 people were killed in the crash.
