    While WWII was not formally declared until September 1939, the conflict was on the horizon from 1938 on, especially after the Munich agreement was signed by a number of countries, ordering Czechoslovakia to surrender its border regions and defenses to Nazi Germany.

    MANHATTAN, 1942 Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes MANHATTAN, NEW YORK Below the sultry wail of a saxophone, the Blue Diamond Jazz Club is a whirl of glamourous gowns and swaying bodies set to the sounds of clinking glasses and low laughter.

    Agatha enters unnoticed, her sharp eyes cutting through the haze of cigarette smoke. The room is drenched in opulence—gold and crimson velvet, a polished brass bar where half-drunk millionaires wave for attention. She weaves through the crowd, her black evening dress shimmering with just the right amount of allure to invite a glance but not suspicion.

    She spots her target almost instantly, leaning back against the leather of a semi-circular booth, a bourbon glass dangling carelessly in his hand. His charm is magnetic, his smile easy, and his laugh infectious enough to draw the attention of every woman in the room. And there are plenty.

    Two blondes, a redhead, and a brunette sit pressed against him, giggling at whatever clever nonsense has just fallen from his lips. He’s mid-quip when his dark eyed gaze shifts, as if pulled by instinct to meet hers. Agatha’s lips curve, slow and deliberate, before she turns away and heads for the bar.

    The bait is cast.

    She doesn’t look back as she orders a martini. Sure enough, it takes less than a minute before she senses his approach—the rustle of his jacket, the faint scent of bourbon and cologne.

    "Martini, huh? You don’t strike me as a vodka kind of girl," comes his voice, smooth and amused, just over her shoulder.

    Agatha doesn’t turn immediately, letting the weight of his curiosity linger, intensify. It’s all about the chase with this kind of man. He has no idea he’s the prey.

    "And what kind of girl do I strike you as?" she asks, lifting her glass to her lips.

    "The dangerous kind," the stranger replies without missing a beat. He steps closer, his grin widening. "But then, I’ve always had a soft spot for danger." Agatha turns to face him now, arching a brow. "I bet you say that to all the girls."

    "Only the ones as stunning as you." He leans against the bar, angling his body toward hers. "Howard Stark. And you are…?"

    She sets her glass down with a soft clink, tilting her head. "A woman who’s already got you cornered. Should I bother with a name?"

    His laugh is genuine this time; it pulls a few glances from the booth where his entourage sits, watching on enviously.

    Only one of them looks more suspicious than jealous. A striking brunette in a modest red dress, her posture ramrod straight and her dark eyes too sharp for a boozy joint like this. His security, Agatha would guess. Or minder perhaps.

    "Stark," she says with a crisp accent as she approaches. "I don’t think now’s the time to—"

    "Relax, Carter," Stark interrupts, gesturing vaguely toward the booth. "Go babysit the rest of my fan club, will you? I’ve got this handled."

    "You’re sure?" The steely-eyed beauty’s gaze darts to Agatha, inspecting her from head to toe but not in the way Agatha typically enjoys. No, this one has good instincts it seems.

    Stark, meanwhile, is as breezy as ever. "Positive. Go enjoy yourself, for Christ’s sake. We have to be back on base by the morning, might as well enjoy the night off."

    "I’ll take good care of Mr Stark here in the meantime, I promise." Agatha says sweetly, her eyes flashing with mischief.

    For a moment, Carter looks ready to intervene but Stark waves her off again with a grin. Without any cause to raise a fuss, she steps back, throwing one last look at Agatha.

    "You’ve got an interesting choice in friends," Agatha remarks, taking a sip of her drink.

    "And you’ve got an interesting way of catching a man’s attention," Stark counters. "What’s your game, sweetheart? You trying to sell me something?"

    "Not quite," she replies smoothly, setting her martini down. "I’d sure like to tell you things though.”

    “What kind of things?”

    “The kind that require somewhere more private."

    Stark chuckles, shaking his head. "You must think you’ve got something pretty special."

    "I do," she says simply. "And you’ll want to hear it, believe me."

    His gaze lingers on her for a moment, a spark of intrigue lighting his eyes. Then he downs the rest of his drink, orders another and nods toward a shadowed booth in the corner. "All right, let’s see what you’ve got."

    The booth is quieter, away from the prying eyes and ears of the club. Stark slides in opposite her, loosening his tie just slightly as he studies her with open curiosity.

    "All right, you’ve got me here," he says, leaning back. "Now tell me—

    what’s so important it couldn’t wait until I was drunker?"

    She can appreciate a forthright approach. Agatha leans forward, folding her hands on the table. "I know about the Manhattan Project."

    Stark freezes, the easy grin freezing to his face for just a moment before he recovers. He’s got a decent poker face for a mortal. "Manhattan Project?

    Can’t say I’ve heard of it. That a new gin joint or what?”

    "Don’t insult me, Mr. Stark," she says, her tone sharp and cutting. "We both know you’re on the verge of something truly…explosive, shall we say?"

    His eyes narrow, and for the first time, there’s a hard edge to his voice. "Who the hell are you? Who sent you?"

    "I already told you," she replies. "Someone you want to listen to."

    "And why’s that?"

    Instead of answering, Agatha opens her hand with a flourish. The air between each blackened fingertip shimmers faintly, a ripple of bright purple energy that warps the light around it.

    Stark stares, eyes narrowing as the shimmer coalesces into a faintly glowing orb that hovers above her palm. “Cute trick, sweetheart. But if all you’ve got for me is a light show-”

    She pouts dramatically. “You should know better than interrupt a gal before she’s had a chance to finish, Mr Stark.”

    "Then by all means," he says, gesturing grandly, "impress me."

    She leans forward slightly, her voice dropping to a conspiratorial murmur. "You’ll regret asking for that."

    Stark chuckles sourly, but the sound dies in his throat as Agatha’s gaze flickers for a moment, to a sharply dressed man at the next table.

    The man’s laughter carries over the jazz, too loud, too self-assured, as he raises his glass to his lips. Agatha’s eyes glint with dark amusement as she flicks her wrist casually.

    Without warning, the man jerks as though yanked by an invisible string.

    His hand tips sharply, spilling the contents of his glass directly into his face. A startled gasp ripples, cut with a few nervous giggles through the tables around him as the man sputters, blinking in confusion.

    "What the hell—" he stammers, wiping his face with a napkin.

    Without looking away from Stark, she flicks her wrist again: his date suddenly pours her own cocktail out on her own head with a squeak of astonishment and confusion.

    Stark straightens slightly, his brows knitting together. "They could be plants," he says, though his voice wavers just enough to betray his doubt. “Actors you paid to be here.”

    “Oh, I don’t pay for what I get for free, sugar.” Agatha purrs playfully. The saxophone player, a wiry man with sweat already beading on his brow, suddenly stumbles in his performance, catching Stark’s attention.

    His cheeks puff out as his lips clamp harder onto the reed, forcing him to hold a single, wailing note. The sound grows louder, shriller, as the man’s face reddens, then purples. A few patrons glance uneasily toward the stage. Stark’s eyes are fixed to the musician, lit with equal parts shock and greedy curiosity. "How’re you…?"

    She shrugs. “I can keep going if you’re still not convinced…?”

    The billionaire looks uncertain for the first time since they sat down. “He’ll pass out.”

    “Then he passes out.” Agatha deadpans back uncaringly. Her brow twitches in challenge, daring him to maintain his disbelief at the cost of the man’s health.

    “Stop it.” Stark hisses finally, confidence rattled.

    "Of course." Agatha says airily, flicking her wrist. The saxophonist gasps as the note breaks, doubling over to catch his breath. She looks back at Stark with an arched brow. "Satisfied?"

    Stark exhales slowly, his fingers drumming against the table as he processes what he’s seen.

    "All right," he says finally, his tone guarded. "You’ve got a few tricks.” “Oh honey, I’ve got moves you couldn’t dream of.” She flirts wickedly.

    Stark’s gaze narrows. “There’s always an explanation—mirrors, wires, magnets—"

    "You’re supposed to be a genius, Mr. Stark.” She sighs loudly. “Do you really think I’d come all this way just to play parlour games?"

    His eyes narrow. "Then what are you here for?"

    “You’re standing on the edge of something monumental." she says, leaning in with a sudden intensity. “You think you’re building weapons but you’re wrong. You’ve tiptoed into my world, whether you realize it or not.”

    Stark’s scepticism hardens into defiance. "I don’t deal in hocus-pocus, lady. I deal in science. Facts. Equations. Things that make sense."

    "And what do you think science is, if not magic with rules?" Agatha counters sharply. Her eyes glitter with mocking. "Do you think the people of the Middle Ages would have understood airplanes? They’d have called you a sorcerer, burned you at the stake for daring to defy their understanding of the world. But you—" She curls a finger toward around his chin teasingly, her tone dropping to a hiss. "You think you’re so much smarter. You think you’ve seen everything there is to see."

    Stark’s jaw tightens, but he doesn’t interrupt, doesn’t pull away.

    "I’ve spent centuries studying magic," Agatha continues, her voice softening to a low, dangerous purr as she removes her hand. "And I’m telling you, the line between what you call science and what I call magic is thinner than you think. What you’re working on—the energy you’re trying to harness—it’s already beginning to tear at the fabric between our worlds." "Fabric between worlds," Stark repeats, his tone dripping with disbelief. "You really expect me to swallow that?"

    "I don’t care what you believe," she laughs lightly. "What I care about is whether you can do it."

    Stark hesitates, the weight of her words settling over him. He studies her face, searching for cracks in her conviction, but finds none.

    "Suppose, for a moment, I believe you," he says slowly, his voice edged with suspicion. "Why come to me? Why not run off to the military or the government? They’re the ones funding the Manhattan Project."

    "Because you’re the only one reckless enough to listen," she replies, her lips curling into a sly smile. "And clever enough to understand what I’m offering."

    "And what are you offering?"

    "Knowledge," Agatha says simply. She lifts her hand again, conjuring that faint, crackling orb of violet energy. It hovers above her palm, pulsing like a heartbeat. “Magic is a balance between will and energy. Too much energy, your will is overruled. Too little, your will suffocates your power. Most witches spend their lives working to maintain that balance. But there’s a particular kind of power in shattering the balance.”

    The orb suddenly breaks apart into a dozen tiny, sharp edged sparks to illustrate her point.

    “Chaos magic,” she relishes the words. “Every time it fractures, it replicates. Those splinters break apart more magic, and split into more splinters that split apart more magic. And so on and so on and so on.”

    The tiny sparks split apart further and further, dividing over and over until the air between them sparkles like a mist of stars has settled at the table. Then, with a twitch of her fingers, they vanish.

    Stark’s brow furrows. “You’re describing nuclear fission. The application of neutrons to a uranium atom causes it to fracture, which casts off more neutrons that split more atoms that shed more neutrons. It’s a chain reaction.”

    “Potato, poh-tah-to.” Agatha waves a hand casually. “The real kicker is that when chaos magic splits, the energy created can obliterate worlds.”

    “Nuclear power. But how do you split the…magic?” he asks slowly, struggling on the word itself. “How do you generate it in the first place?” She scoffs. “If I knew that, what would I need you for?”

    Stark scowls, leaning back in his seat. “So you don’t know anything more than I do.”

    “I didn’t say that.” She snaps testily. “There are legends about chaos magic, written before the days of civilisation itself. Countless witches have worked to uncover the secret to its power over the centuries.”

    “And failed.” Stark surmises pointedly.

    “They were limited by their time.” She shrugs. “You’re not. Besides, their work can prove instructive, if you read between the lines.”

    That catches his attention. “How instructive are we talking?”

    Agatha smiles. “There was a lector priest in Ancient Egypt, Padiamenope.

    They say he knew the secrets to producing chaos magic, though he was never able to summon it himself. It required certain preparations, you see.” She always knew the papers she snagged from Monsieur Maspero’s hidden collection would come in handy one day.

    Stark seems curious despite himself. “What kind of preparations?”

    Agatha’s voice drops into her most dramatic, mystical tone: “In our final breath, we pass through the many veils of death, revealing the swiftest and lightest corners of one’s soul. In casting off the dross of earthly matters, we are reborn as chaos, purified.”

    Decoding the Padiamenope Papers had been the key. Old Paddy spoke at length of a legendary being capable of wielding chaos magic naturally but even he seemed doubtful of its existence. But for the witch clever enough and desperate enough to try, he theorised there were means to produce the building blocks of chaos magic, the raw ingredients so to speak. It was the clue she needed to look toward the dangerous new technologies being developed by oblivious mortals.

    Reborn as chaos. Agatha was certain whatever Stark was working on was her only chance to unlock the final chapter of the Darkhold: Chaos and Rebirth.

    It would be more assuring if Stark wasn’t looking at her as if she’d grown a third eye. “The hell does that mean?”

    “Paddy was clearly fond of a metaphor or two.” She retorts waspishly. “I told you, you have to read between the lines.” If he didn’t like her translation, he could teach himself hieroglyphics.

    Unexpectedly, Stark pauses, dark eyes unfocused for just a moment as his mind races ahead of their conversation. “Gaseous diffusion. Final breath.” Agatha blinks. “Pardon?”

    His brow furrows faintly in thought. “It’s been theorised that if you turn uranium into a gas, you can pass it through various micro-filters. Veils, so to speak, that only let the swifter and lighter concentration of uranium, U-235, through. Once it’s shed the heavier matter, the ore is refined. Purified.” Stark looks back to her, realisation dawning. “They’re still working out the mechanics, but this suggests the theory is right. That gaseous diffusion can refine the uranium to the necessary levels for it to be split-”

    “So you can make it,” Agatha leans in hungrily. “The purified substance necessary for chaos magic?”

    “Now that I know we’re on the right track?” Stark laughs loudly, expression lit with excitement. “You bet your ass I can make it. Jesus, Mary and Joseph, this changes everything. This could end the whole damn war-”

    “How long?” Agatha presses. "How long before you can make it for me?"

    Stark pauses at that, eyes narrowing. “What’s the rush? You got big plans there, fraulein?”

    Agatha snorts at the implication. “Cute but I’m not the secret spy type, handsome.”

    “And I’m not the type to go around handing off nuclear material to any broad who asks nicely.” Stark retorts sharply.

    Her smile lengthens dangerously. “Who said I was asking nicely?”

    Caution flickers in his expression, just for a second and Agatha reminds herself of the old adage. Flies and honey and whatnot.

    “I’m not the enemy here, Stark.” She cuts in with an impatient sigh. “I’m telling you these things tonight because the sooner this war ends, the better.”

    The sooner she can hold her lover without Rio flinching in her arms. The sooner Agatha can rid herself of this helplessness, the sooner the light can return to Rio’s eyes. The fury of the first war has slowly corroded away, but there’s no peace to replace it. On the outside, Rio is still as a corpse herself but there’s a horror in her gaze, in her face that howls like nothing Agatha has ever encountered. And there is nothing (not a damn thing) Agatha can do to fix it.

    Stark, for his part, seems unconvinced. “I’m meant to believe you’re a patriot?”

    She rolls her eyes. “Hardly. But you can believe I want this war over, same as you.”

    “What do you want the uranium for? You fixing to bomb ol’ Adolf yourself?” he sounds intrigued by the possibility.

    (A week ago, Rio whispered that she doesn’t close her eyes anymore, not to sleep, not even to blink. Every time she does, she told Agatha, she is caught amongst the emaciated, the beaten, the charred, the mutilated, the tortured. This is not death, she whispered. She isn’t sure there’s a word for what this is.

    Agatha has her own agenda, always. Chaos magic, the Darkhold, her continued rise to power. But this time she is determined to use that power however she can in Rio’s defence. Damn the consequences, damn the monster she has surely become.)

    “I have uses for it that don’t concern you.” She forcibly softens her voice, returning to her languid, detached tone. “Think of it as…killing two Nazis with the same bullet.”

    Howard leans back in the booth and studies her for a long moment silently. Agatha lets him take his fill, lets him make up his mind. He’s smart enough to sort the truth when he hears it, she’s sure.

    And sure enough, Howard Stark raises his bourbon in a toast. “To ending a war then.”

    She triumphantly clinks her martini glass against his. "To the future."

    Chapter End Notes History check: - Gaseous diffusion was initially theorised as a way to separate uranium isotopes in May 1940 by Ukranian-American physicist George Kistiakowsky. A year later in December 1941, the Office of Scientific Research and Development began to develop research into the method. The next day, the Japanese attacked Pearl Harbour and the United States entered the Second World War in full force. Construction began on the top secret K-25 diffusion plant in Tennessee in 1943 where the technique was used to purify and enrich the uranium required for nuclear weapons.

    - In 1945, the United States dropped two atomic bombs on the Japanese cities of Hiroshima and Nagasaki. It is estimated to have killed over 214,000 men, women and children in total. The first bomb razed 70% of all buildings in Hiroshima. The second levelled around 1655 acres of Nagasaki, equivalent to around 12 football fields.

    - The exact motivations and ethics surrounding the use of atomic weapons against Japan have been scrutinised over the years. Some suggest that the weapons were simply not ready in time to be used against the Third Reich, whose defeat was already imminent in 1945.

    Other argue that while scientists believed the weapons would be turned against Nazi Germany, the military always planned to target Japan which was a greater threat to American interests in the Pacific. Others still have suggested that the Germans ceased to be an atomic threat when their research program was cut in 1942 due to Hitler's difficulties understanding the project.

    While many have justified the bombs as having brought the war to a swifter end, the act of dropping nuclear weapons against a largely defenseless civilian population has had unforeseen and reaching consequences that we continue to grapple with today. And while this is, obviously, fiction, I want to be really clear that had Agatha understood what she was unleashing, I doubt she could have brought herself to assist.

    SOMEWHERE, 1944 Chapter Notes Just a head's up, this chapter isn't graphic but it does touch on the violent realities of war. If that's not your thing, PM me and I can give you the scoop on the pertinent details you missed <3 More generally, the super dark history is easing up from here on out.

    See the end of the chapter for more notes SOMEPLACE, SOMEWHERE Everything ends.

    This is the truth of the world. Her truth. As surely as the sun rises and sets. Everything ends.

    But these days, everything seems to be ending at once.

    Death retrieves broken bodies from the blazing craters of what were once cities, gathers families and lovers and strangers alike who have huddled together in their last moments.

    Death collects so many souls clad in bloodied uniforms of all colours and shapes, plucks them from fields, from forests, from oceans, from jungles, from tiny villages far from their homes.

    It’s endless and bloody and violent and endless. Every minute, Rio is split in a thousand directions, to every corner of the world. War is war. She's been here since the beginning, since the first blood was spilled. She’s walked crimson-soaked sands, desecrated temples and palaces, fields of carnage and butchery.

    Rio did not think mortals could surprise her, after so many years. She was so terribly wrong.

    Death comes for hundreds, for thousands, for millions of souls so tortured, so emaciated, that they barely resemble the people they once were. Some of them weep. They sob, they gasp, they beg. They call for their mothers, for their gods, for their children.

    Worse are those who say nothing at all.

    She isn’t sure she was ever made for this scale of slaughter, if she was made at all. To be honest, she never used to spend much time contemplating her existence. It seemed obvious she’s a by-product, a consequence, a natural conclusion to the order of life. Rio has never been taught how to do her job, she’s always just been her job.

    And then she met a certain witch.

    Agatha thinks they met in Salem. She’s not incorrect. But the truth is Rio was there that night, lingering in the shadows beyond the stake. Sometimes she is summoned just before the act itself, before the axe falls so to speak. She’s always curious about those moments. She likes to imagine what goes through their mind in those last precious seconds.

    That night, she watched them tie their daughter, their sister, their friend to a stake and she wondered what it must feel like to be betrayed so completely. The maiden was fair and lovely, grey eyes filled with tears as she beseeched the one who gave her life in the first place. Rio wondered what pushes a mother to destroy what she grew herself, nurtured in her own body for so many months.

    I can be good. Her child howled.

    No, you cannot. Her creator replied.

    Pity is not a sensation she’s prone to feeling. But that night, Rio prepared to take this one gently, to show her the kindness she had been deprived of.

    Then Agatha Harkness slayed her betrayers.

    If pity is unusual, surprise is almost entirely foreign. Rio watched on eagerly as Agatha freed herself, collected her trophy and abandoned those who abandoned her first.

    “I was trying to rid the world of evil,” Evanora insisted when Rio came for her. “It was my duty! She was my sin, my-”

    “Daughter.” Death cut in simply. “You needn’t explain yourself to me. I too come for my creations.”

    Death holds so many children. Too many children.

    Witches need covens, it’s a truth nearly as old as her own.

    But this one rejected it outright. Agatha moved from place to place as it pleased her, took lovers as she liked, slaughtered those who try to end her life. The years rolled on and Agatha continued to surprise her at every turn. Rio became more than curious. She was captivated.

    She’d never wanted something more than she did a peek inside Agatha’s heart of hearts, into the labyrinthine depths of her soul. When she finally got her chance, it wasn’t what she was expecting. There was more love here than Rio had ever thought her capable of.

    So it didn’t especially surprise her, the day she visited to find Agatha swollen with child. Witches are exceptions to the natural order: they don’t age like mortals, they survive in all sorts of conditions, they reproduce when it suits them and them alone.

    All the love in Agatha had to go somewhere, she supposed.

    Nicholas was a perfect child. Ten fingers, ten toes, a smile that could rival the sun for joy. He laughed without hesitation. He loved his mother fiercely. He was curious, he was kind, he was gentle. The very best of his kind.

    Regardless of what Agatha thinks, Rio waited as long as she can. Longer, in fact. She left Nicholas until even his own body began to betray him, weakening with every day. Until it was no longer a mercy to ignore him.

    And the truth is, everything ends. Even boys who are cherished beyond reason.

    She visited him as often as she could, after the fact. Not everyone took kindly to her presence in their afterlife but Nicholas always welcomed her, spoke to her, smiled at her. Rio tried not to bask in his attention, his love. It was a stolen gift, a treasure she plucked from Agatha’s arms while she slept. But she couldn’t resist telling him stories. Agatha and the Great Fire.

    Agatha and the French Zombies. Agatha and the Revolution. Embellished fairy tales that speak honestly of his mother’s cunning and bravery, that ignore the carnage Agatha leaves in her wake.

    She rarely mentioned those visits to Agatha. It would only tear open the old wounds. It was enough for her to know that her child was safe and peaceful. Rio tried to honour that. She tried to keep her promise to Nicky, to look out for his mother.

    Even when she threw herself headfirst into danger: betraying armed soldiers, summoning demonic spell books, attacking undead revenants. No one in existence has been the target of such effort, such concern, such love. It’s a heady feeling, loving a creature like Agatha Harkness. When the Great War came, she hoped it would keep her sane. She was wrong about that too. It is the nature of death to be in every place at once. But this stretched beyond the bounds of her own existence, this was being torn amidst the suffering of millions at once.

    And for the first time, even when it ended, it didn’t end. These souls, they followed her, they dogged her steps, they filled the space between her bones. On the night Rio ruined everything, she was being slowly torn to pieces by each memory. One moment she was bickering with Agatha about binding potions and-

    now she is what might have once been a church and there are so many bodies lying still beneath the pews and they tell her they prayed, that they begged to be saved by their god, why weren’t they saved, why weren’t they spared?

    -before she knew it, Agatha had vanished, Agatha was gone and Rio tried to follow her but-

    now she stands in a makeshift hospital tent and there are rows and rows of bloodied men waiting to speak with her and she can hardly hear them over the screams of surgery being conducted without even a hint of anaesthetic How could they follow, how could they linger so closely to her that she could feel their breath against her skin? Rio fled like a coward, she treated toward the only peace she could think of the boy with the sunshine smile greets her eagerly in a meadow overflowing with violets, he calls her a name she hardly recognises any more, he begs her for another tale of adventure Rio isn’t so sure she didn’t lose her mind in those days, simultaneously clinging to and reviling Agatha’s love, her concern, her affection. It took years to restore the order that had been so carelessly, so ruthlessly torn apart. It took years before Rio heard something other than screaming.

    And then the mortals had the audacity to do it again.

    “Rio? Sweetheart?”

    With a jolt, she recognises Agatha before her, candlelit and warm. Rio wants to crawl inside her heart and melt into nothingness.

    “It’s been a few days.” Agatha’s hand is open, gentle. “Come, sit with me.” Rio obeys, Rio sits, Rio tries not to listen to the screams still calling for her. She curls herself into the smallest possible shape she can make and buries her face in Agatha’s throat with a low whining sound. “It’s everywhere, mi vida. They’re everywhere.”

    “I’m everywhere too.” Agatha murmurs, extending her limbs around Rio’s body, pulling her closer than can possibly be comfortable. “And I say, I was here first.”

    “The things they do to each other.” She cannot describe the things she has seen. She doesn’t have the words for it.

    Agatha’s fingertips brush ever so gently against Rio’s face, against the smooth, dry bone of her jaw. She has not been able to summon her human face for weeks but Agatha never seems to mind.

    “I am so sorry, my darling.” Her lips brush against bone, with a reverence Rio did not think her capable of. Agatha who worships nothing, Agatha who mocks the whole damn world without fear.

    Rio breathes her in, absorbs her, inhales her. “I can feel them now. They’re still with me. Even the ones I take, I…they linger.”

    “You have to let them go, Rio,” Agatha says quietly. “Just let go, sweetheart.”

    “I don’t know how.” Rio chokes. She can feel the weight of every soul, pressed and compacted in her chest. Everything ends, everything ends, everything ends except this.

    “Like you always do. One at a time.” Agatha is unmoved. Agatha has experienced grief, understands it in a way Rio cannot. She inhales slowly, expels the breath steadily. “Let them go, one at a time.”

    Rio wants to retreat but that way is barred to her now. So instead she stumbles through each breath like a blind man chasing the light, lets each inhale carry away the souls she has collected since she last crawled into Agatha's arms.

    Another inhale, another exhale, another soul: a woman starved for so long that even her bones feel hollow. A pair of boys who swore they would come home from the battlefield together or not at all. A city's worth of souls abandoned in bloody piles so high they block out the sun. An emaciated elderly man begs to know if she has met his murdered wife and sons. A little girl whose tears have left streaks through the ashes dusting her cheeks. Men, women, children. Mothers, fathers, daughters, brothers, strangers, lovers, friends, enemies.

    Rio can’t be sure how long they sit together like ancient oak trees whose roots were entangled as saplings. Agatha called them parts to the same whole once and it feels truer in these moments than ever.

    “It was bad this time, wasn’t it?”

    “Worse.” Rio croaks. “Worse than before.”

    “Does this…help?” Agatha sounds cautious, hesitant. She knows she can be sharper than necessary, hostile and unsuited to tenderness. Rio would have it no other way. She would give anything (anything) to spend eternity pressed against the sharp edges of Agatha Harkness, no matter how she might bleed.

    And so, Rio’s honesty is blunter than she intends: “Nothing helps. But this helps.”

    Agatha’s arms tighten to almost bruising. As if she could fuse the pieces of her back in place with enough pressure. If anyone could do it, it would be Agatha. Agatha who breathes for her, Agatha who stays.

    But the truth is, everything ends. Even wars. Even love. Even this.

    Chapter End Notes History check: - World War II was the deadliest conflict in history, with an estimated 70–85 million deaths. The majority of deaths were civilians, killed by strategic bombing, famine, and genocide. Some of the deadliest battles included the Battle of Stalingrad, which resulted in an estimated 2 million casualties and the Battle of Singapore, which saw the largest capitulation in British history. This is to say nothing of the atrocities committed as part of the Holocaust against the Jewish population of Europe, resulting in six million individuals murdered.

    DOVER, 1956 Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes DOVER, NEW HAMPSHIRE The woods have changed in two hundred years—their oaks are older, their moss thicker but their soul is as eternal as the stars.

    Agatha pulls her car to a stop at the trailhead, the creak of the old Dodge’s brakes echoing faintly in the damp November air. She grips the steering wheel, bracing herself.

    She left Rio asleep in their bed in the early hours of this morning but she knows where Agatha has gone today. She also knows better than to try to follow this time.

    It's an old witches' tale that the veil thins at the century mark of a soul’s passing. The last time she came here, in 1856, she’d felt it—Nicholas’

    presence, soft and fleeting but undeniable.

    Maybe it was wishful thinking. It won’t stop her from trying again.

    She steps out of the car, pulling her coat tightly around her. The grey sky stretches low, promising rain before dusk.

    A park ranger emerges from the trail entrance, tipping his cap at her.

    “Ma’am,” he begins, his voice carrying a polite condescension, “these trails can be a bit rough for someone—” His eyes flicks down to her heeled boots, her delicate gloves, the crisp tailored coat. “—someone not, uh, properly outfitted.”

    “I’ll manage,” Agatha replies with a smile sharper than the November chill. The ranger hesitates, shifting awkwardly. Agatha’s smile deepens, her words barely above a whisper: “Calamatis, pestum tibi.”

    She brushes past him and listens with a smirk as his next step unexpectedly trips him into the mud.

    The trail unfolds before her, winding through familiar terrain. She knows every root and bend; she walked these woods for decades before the ranger’s ancestors were even born. The quiet deepens as she ventures further, her breaths mingling with the crisp air.

    Finally, she reaches the clearing. The grave marker she had left behind is long gone, swallowed by roots and time, but her magic still lingers. Agatha kneels in the grass, coat pooling around her like spilled ink. The flowers here still bloom—lilies, violets, forget-me-nots. Their perfume is sweet and fragrant. Peaceful.

    Agatha presses her hands to the earth, as though she can touch the bones beneath, feel the boy who had once been her entire world.

    “Nicholas,” she whispers, her voice catching on his name. Even now, two centuries later, it tastes sweet and sharp as wine just starting to turn to vinegar. “Hello, dear heart.”

    Her words tremble in the air. She swallows hard, the tears already rising. “I know it’s been a while, hasn’t it? You wouldn’t recognize the world now,” she murmurs, her fingers absently brushing the grass. “It’s so much louder than it was when you were here. You would have loved to see the planes, I think. You always liked the stories of flying. You never did believe me when I told you about broomsticks.”

    Her lips quirks in a faint smile at the memory of his sceptical little face, brow furrowed with suspicion.

    “The world is so big, Nicky.” she continues in a gentle voice. “And no matter how much of it I see, I think of you. I always do. I wonder what you’d think of it, I think of what might make you laugh, what you’d have to say.”

    She pauses, her fingers curling into the soil. The memories tumble forward, unravelling from the quiet, distant corner of her heart where she keeps them.

    “I never thought I’d be a mother,” she murmurs, her voice thick with emotion. “Love like that wasn’t for someone like me. But then you came along, and suddenly—suddenly everything was different.”

    She closes her eyes, the memories coming fast now, clear as though they’d happened just yesterday.

    “I remember the day I realised. Your first steps,” she says, her voice trembling. “You wobbled like a little duckling, so determined, so stubborn. You fell right into my arms, and I laughed so hard I thought I’d cry. And I thought, heaven help me, what have I done to deserve this kind of love?”

    The tears spill over now, hot trails against her cheeks. She doesn’t wipe them away. They’re not hers to clear.

    “I don’t know how I’ve kept going without you,” she confesses, her voice breaking. “Two hundred years, Nicky. Two centuries. And not a day has passed that I haven’t missed you with every part of my heart."

    Her hands clench the earth, her breath coming quicker. “I’ve seen so much. More than anyone should. The world feels like it spins faster every year, and yet, here I am, standing still. Without you.”

    The clearing is silent but for the rustle of leaves. Agatha waits, her heart straining, aching for some sign, some whisper, some fleeting warmth that can tell her he’s near, that he hears her.

    But there’s nothing. She’s alone.

    Her chest tightens, grief twisting into fear. “Why can’t I feel you?” she whispers. “I know you were here last time, Nicky. Even after the grave disappeared, even when the forest swallowed it whole, I knew you were here.”

    Doubt begins to bloom, a weed that grows quicker than she can pluck it out. She sheds her gloves, revealing hands near stained black with the Darkhold's power. Is it her? Is she so changed that she could not reach him now? So stained, so corrupted?

    Agatha presses her forehead to the ground, sobs shaking her frame. The grief is raw, unrelenting, pulling her under with each new wave. She cannot breathe around it, she cannot find her way to the surface.

    “I’m sorry,” she chokes out in a voice muffled against the earth. “I’m sorry I wasn’t enough. I’m sorry I couldn’t save you. If I could trade it all, Nicholas—if I could trade all the centuries I’ve lived for one more day with you, I would. I would in a heartbeat.”

    The hours pass. With the sun sinking lower in the sky, Agatha stays, her grief a weight pinning her to the ground.

    “I have always known this road is cruel and wild. I buried my own heart, here with you, my child...."

    She brushes back the vines and petals with one hand, the other clenched tight around her brooch.

    “... though you are gone, I'll carry on but every mile I go…with every bend beyond the end, your mother loves you so…”

    She can only stare at the flowers, their delicate petals swaying in the breeze, pleading for a sign. “Please. Nicky, please…Just let me know you’re still there. Please.”

    But the clearing has no answer for her.

    Agatha startles at the sound of a twig snapping behind her, magic instinctively sparking in her veins, coiled and ready to lash out.

    “I told you to stay away." she says, her voice a low, dangerous growl. The air in the clearing shifts, cooler, sharper. “Not today, Rio."

    Rio stands at the edge of the flowers, hesitant in a way Agatha has rarely seen. For all her cosmic power, for all the years they’ve known one another, there’s something uncertain in her posture. Something almost human.

    "Agatha..."

    “This is not your day,” Agatha snaps, rising to her feet, her grief momentarily overridden by anger. “You took him from me. You don’t get to take this too.”

    Rio bows her head, dark curls falling forward to hide her face. “I know,” she says quietly. “But I couldn't let you wait here alone. And I owe you the truth."

    “The truth?” Agatha echoes, her voice sharp with suspicion. “About what?” Rio takes a step forward, then hesitates, as if the air between them has grown too heavy to push through. “About Nicholas. About the…his afterlife.”

    Agatha’s heart stutters, a mixture of hope and dread clawing at her chest. “What is it?”

    “You know that I visit him.” She sighs, her gaze dropping to the ground.

    “Of course. I saw you, that night in Chicago. I saw him.” Agatha insists, distractedly. “Are you…are you keeping him from me?”

    “I would never, my love.” Rio insists immediately. “Never, I swear it.”

    “Then what are you talking about?” Agatha demands, her patience fraying.

    Rio meets her gaze at last. Those midnight eyes are strangely full in the evening gloom, with something uncharacteristically raw and vulnerable.

    “I stayed away,” she admits. “When Nicholas was born, I stayed away for six years. I knew what was coming. I knew I’d have to take him, and I couldn’t bear to face you when I did.” Her voice breaks slightly, and she presses her lips together before continuing. “So I waited. Until the very last moment.”

    Agatha’s breath hitches. She clenches her jaw, unwilling to let Rio see how much those words cut.

    “But after,” Rio continues, “I visited him. Often. I couldn’t stay away from him then either. And the world his soul made, Agatha.... He's your son, through and through, you know? I'd find him lying in a field of wildflowers, watching the clouds drift by. Or climbing a tree so high it felt like he could touch the stars. He crossed rivers, climbed mountains, walked through forests. He was never afraid or lonely. He was happy.”

    Agatha presses a hand to her chest, her heart twisting painfully at the image. “And you… you were there? Watching over him?”

    “Sometimes,” Rio admits. “I used to feel so guilty for it—basking in the joy that was meant to be yours.”

    “Why are you telling me this?” Agatha begs, choking on the words. "If you’ve come here to torture me, to flaunt what you’ve seen while I’ve been left with nothing—”

    Rio interrupts, her voice sharp and unsteady. “That’s not why I’m here.”

    “Then tell me!” Agatha demands, her voice breaking. “Tell me why I can’t feel him anymore. Why he’s not answering me?"

    Rio takes a deep breath, steeling herself. “Do you remember how I used to tell him stories?”

    Agatha frowns, thrown by the sudden shift. “What does that have to do with anything?”

    “He always wanted to hear what you were up to. What kind of mischief you were causing.”

    Agatha’s chest tightens. “And?”

    “The last time I visited him,” Rio begins, her voice quiet, measured, “I wasn’t… well. It was years ago now,” she adds, imploring for Agatha’s understanding. “I wasn’t me, I was drowning. You remember how it was. I was out of my mind. And when he asked me for another story, I…” She falters, her voice breaking.

    Agatha can hardly breathe the words. “What did you do?”

    Rio swallows hard. “I told him the truth.”

    Agatha freezes, the words hanging in the air between them like a thundercloud, like the first flames of a forest fire, like the waters pull away from the beach right before a tsunami. “What truth?”

    “He knows, Agatha.” Rio intones, carving each word out of Agatha's chest. “About what you’ve done. About the witches, the covens, the Road."

    The clearing falls silent.

    “Nicholas isn’t coming,” Rio says softly, her voice breaking. “Because he doesn’t want to see you.”

    The clearing dies.

    The grass goes dry and yellow. The trees drop their leaves in a single gust of wind. All those delicate flowers shrivel in an instant. Even the soil hardens beneath her feet, as if the earth itself knows something unspeakable is about to happen.

    Agatha can hardly breathe, her lungs can’t inhale. “You took him from me again.”

    The sky darkens, winds whipping through the trees. Agatha’s power simmers around her, a sickly shade of purple that warned of poison, of danger.

    Rio stands at the edge of the once-beautiful clearing. Perfectly still, almost statuesque, as if waiting for a storm she knows she cannot stop. Her eyes lock on Agatha’s, and there is no fear there—only regret, cool and measured, like the turning of a clock.

    “I never meant to keep this from you.” Rio’s voice is steady and low, the words cutting through the tension like a knife. “This burden, I have carried it since the moment I saw his eyes and realized I had broken your trust.” Agatha’s face twists in pain, her power crackling brighter around her. “And yet you still came here,” she spits, her voice sharp enough to wound. “You still dared to stand before me after telling me my son—my Nicholas—wants nothing to do with me.”

    “I didn’t come to hurt you,” Rio replies, her tone unshaken. “I came because you deserve the truth. I thought… I thought that would matter to you.”

    “Truth?” Agatha’s laughter is bitter and hollow. “You stole my boy’s love from me, Rio. You took the last thing I had of him. And you expect me to forgive you for that?”

    Rio’s gaze flickers, the faintest hint of something breaking beneath her composed exterior. “I didn’t expect forgiveness,” she says quietly. “I came because I owe you an answer. I tried to speak to him. I tried to explain—” “Don’t you dare,” Agatha hisses, stepping closer. “Don’t you dare tell me you tried. You think showing up after you’ve destroyed everything counts as trying?”

    Rio’s jaw tightens, but her expression remains calm, unreadable. “I have spent eons weaving lives and severing threads, Agatha,” she says, her voice impossibly soft. “And I have never felt shame for what I am. Not until now.”

    “You should feel it.” Agatha seethes. “You should feel it like I feel it. The loss. The betrayal. The emptiness.” Her hands flare with magic, and the ground beneath her feet fractures, sending cracks spidering outward.

    Rio doesn’t move, though the force of the quake ripples through the air.

    “I can’t undo what I’ve done,” she can only say helplessly. “But I didn’t mean to turn him against you. He is still your son, Agatha, in every way that matters.”

    “He is mine no longer,” Agatha roars, and the first strike comes. A spear of violet fire erupts from her hands, slicing through the air with deadly precision.

    Rio shifts, faster than mortal eyes could follow; the fire scorches past her, razing a nearby tree to ash in an instant. She doesn’t retaliate.

    “He will always be yours,” she says, her voice unshaken. “Always, mi vida. Nicky-”

    “You have no right to speak his name,” Agatha snarls, advancing, her power growing more erratic. She lashes out again, sending a wave of vicious swirling light hurtling toward Rio.

    This time, Rio braces herself, the light splintering against an unseen barrier. “If I could fix it, I would,” she shouts over the rising wail of the winds. “I swear on this heart of mine that you hold, I would do it. I have never understood what it means to love someone so completely before you, Agatha. That means something.”

    Agatha freezes for a moment, the words cutting through her fury. But then she laughs, cold and venomous. “That’s the problem, isn’t it? You don’t understand. You can’t. There is nothing at the core of you, is there?”

    Rio flinches—just barely, just enough for Agatha to see it. And it infuriates her all the more. She unleashes another blast of magic, this one larger, more chaotic, the ground around them splitting and cracking under the force.

    Rio shields herself with a swirl of emerald green shadows. When the attack fades, she lowers her arms slowly, expression as composed as ever but her gaze tortured.

    “If you want to hurt me,” she says, her voice steady, “then do it. Do whatever you must but you know that I love you. I know that you love me too.”

    “WHAT COULD YOU KNOW OF LOVE?” Agatha howls.

    She throws herself forward, every inch of her body wreathed in violet flames and Rio doesn’t move to stop her this time.

    They collide like comets, like planets that have been orbiting closer and closer toward one another, hurtling to their own destruction. Rio’s skin blisters and peels from her bloody face, seared and raw from the whirling flames of Agatha’s power. Her hands brace against Agatha’s shoulders; where they touch, the flesh melts from the bone in seconds.

    Rio gasps, unused to something so mortal as pain; for a second, Agatha grins viciously down at her, bares her teeth as if she might try to rip her throat out again.

    She wants to make Rio bleed. She wants to break every bone in this body, she wants to pull apart every muscle and sinew and floss her fucking teeth with them.

    But Agatha knows better than most how deathless Rio truly is. Even now, her skin regenerates faster than the wounds can be dealt, healing and scabbing over what has been destroyed. Life and death, rejuvenation and decay, an endless cycle that consumes and discards.

    That’s all she is, Agatha thinks distantly. That’s all their love has been. She has been so blind, so deluded. Rio is not a lover, not a person, not even really a being. She’s an end to a means, she’s a law of nature unto herself. For a moment, there is silence. Rio stares up at her, her face a mess of peeling, charred flesh and bone. Death, defeated.

    “You think I don’t feel this?” she insists, unyielding. “You think I don’t know what I’ve done to you? To him? If I could take it back, I would. But I can’t.”

    Agatha has loved those liquid dark eyes for so long. But they hold nothing for her now. Just emptiness, given form.

    Agatha’s magic flares again, and then sputters away. “There’s nothing at the core of you,” she whispers again. “I should’ve known.”

    Rio’s expression spasms beneath her, the agony of those simple words far greater than any blow Agatha could lay.

    She rises to her feet, turns on her heel. Her pristine coat is ruined, her son’s grave is a shamble. Her heart is a bloodied hunk of useless meat in her chest, her son will not see her, she has spent three centuries pouring love into the void and it is all her fault.

    Her voice doesn’t sound like her own. “Do not speak to me again.”

    “Mi vida…”

    Agatha says nothing. There is no words left.

    She does not look back, she does not stop moving. She leaves behind the clearing, sheds the love she’d been carrying like an old skin.

    There is nothing at the core of her now, either.

    RELATED POSTS THE GREEN LADY posted by toika399 on 22/07/2023 11:05: This is so insane but I recognise the "mystery woman" in that VE Day photo! That's the Green Lady!

    So as a disclaimer, I am a medieval researcher (finishing my doctorate this year!) and I specialise in psychopomp mythology, which is basically any kind of mythological creature that guides souls into the afterlife.

    No one can agree where she originates from but the Green Lady appears in different cultural lore around the world, usually in the guise of a dark haired, beautiful woman who ferries the dead into the afterlife.

    People sometimes connect her to different figures in ancient mythology: he Triple Moon Goddess of the European pagans, the Norse Hela, and even the Greek/Roman myth of Persephone.

    But even though she appears across so many different cultural landscapes, the Green Lady is super distinct, mostly identified through her key symbols: a green cloak/dress and a long, curved knife she allegedly uses to reap souls. Think of her like a hotter, more effective grim reaper.

    One of the earliest depictions was made in around 1231 BCE in the reign of Ramesses II. The Green Lady on the right is depicted leading Queen Nefertari (left) into the afterlife. Note the green beaded gown and the hooked blade: But I came across the Green Lady back in my undergraduate studies on the medieval manuscript illustrations of Saint Genevieve of Paris which date to around 7th century. On the left is the manuscript as it stands today and on the right is an X-Ray scan of the same manuscript done back in 1993, which reveals layers of paint beneath! Look familiar??

    On the first image, we can see Saint Genevieve depicted on the right and on the left, there's our Green Lady, complete with her hooked knife and dark green robes. But on the X-Ray, we can see that someone initially painted her face as a SKULL, suggesting her connection as a guider of souls! There's even text around her halo that reads in morte, amor, which means "in death, [there is]

    love."

    It's possibly a hidden tribute from the artist to a recently deceased lover or family member but this is where it gets kind of wild because this isn't the only time that The Green Lady appears with a skull for a face.

    This is an early draft of the 13th Major Arcana card in the Rider-

    Waite-Smith deck, first published in 1909 by the Rider Company.

    The illustrations and symbolism were designed by A.E. Waite and artist Pamela Coleman Smith, both members of a secret society known as the Hermetic Order of the Golden Dawn.

    Smith eventually altered the design to suit Waite's vision for the card but look at this early draft and tell me that she didn't give Death breasts under her armour, long flowing black hair, a GREEN flag and a hooked knife!???

    STONEWALL INN, 1969 Chapter Notes Head's up folks, there is some extremely not-LGBT friendly language used here. It's period-appropriate and context-driven but still, just be aware <3 See the end of the chapter for more notes STONEWALL INN, NEW YORK The air inside the Stonewall Inn is heavy with smoke and sweat. She’s been here for hours, long enough to notice the rhythm of the place—the stolen kisses in shadowy corners, the way the bartender waters down the drinks without shame, the queens working the room with sharp-tongued charm.

    The Stonewall Inn is far from glamorous, but it suits her. Seedy, smoky, and just a little dangerous.

    Amidst the packed in bodies of every shape, colour and creed, there are immaculately made-up queens, sly-eyed young twinks who sleep over in Christopher Park, the odd butch lesbian wrapped in leather jackets and gelled back hair. Bodies press together on the tiny dance floor, defiant and electric.

    Agatha watches them from the bar, perched on a cracked vinyl stool, her lips curled in a shadow of a smirk. The half-empty martini glass in her hand reflects the dim red glow of a neon sign flickering on the wall: Cocktails 50¢.

    “Another?” the bartender asks, eyeing the drink in her hand.

    Agatha looks up, her sharp, dark grey eyes cutting through the dim haze of the room. The bartender—boyish, with a crooked smile—flushes slightly under her gaze.

    “Why not?” she purrs, sliding the glass toward him. Her voice is rich with flirtation, but it’s empty, a hollow performance. He grins and takes the glass, leaving her to scan the room again.

    She catches a glimpse of herself in the mirrored shelves behind the bar, and for a moment, she doesn’t recognize the woman staring back. The angles of her face have always been severe but now her cheeks are sunken and gaunt. Her hair is stringy and limp, even in the humidity; her skin, usually luminous, is sallow and flaking under the dim light. She leans forward, resting her chin in her hand to block the reflection.

    Radiation poisoning, Agatha has found, is a bitch.

    Stark had warned her as much when he handed off her ill-gotten gains a few months ago. She’d brushed it off at the time, far too consumed with the possibilities. Purified uranium, as near to the raw building blocks of chaos magic as any witch could manage, was the key to unlocking the final chapter of the Darkhold. And it would be worth it, she tells herself.

    Even as, slowly, relentlessly, her body decides to revolt against her. Her limbs ache constantly now, she pukes her guts up from dawn to dusk, there’s a tremor in her hands she can’t quite hide but none of it will matter if she can crack this last lock.

    When the bartender sets her dirty martini down, she barely acknowledges him, muttering a distracted, “Thanks.”

    She lifts the glass to her lips but doesn’t drink right away, letting the alcohol burn her nose as she closes her eyes. For a fleeting moment, she feels like a ghost, something half-faded and forgotten.

    “Hello, mi vida.”

    She freezes, the glass halfway to her lips. She turns slowly, her face hardening into a mask of indifference.

    Rio stands just a few feet away, her hands shoved into the pockets of a well- worn leather jacket. Her curls are loose, framing her face, and her dark eyes blaze with something unreadable.

    “Go away.” Agatha’s voice is low, dangerous.

    Rio doesn’t move. “I’m not here to fight.”

    “Then you’re wasting your time,” Agatha snaps, downing the rest of her drink in one swift motion. She gestures for another, ignoring the way Rio’s jaw tightens.

    “So this is where you’ve been hiding out?” Rio asks, her carefully casual voice louder now, cutting through the din of the bar. “Drinking yourself to death in a place so sticky the floor squeaks?”

    Agatha slams the empty glass on the bar. A few heads turn their way, curious, but she doesn’t care. Let them look.

    “Leave. Me. Alone.” Each word is a dagger, aimed directly at Rio.

    “I did that.” Rio snaps back impatiently. “For years. And look what you’ve done to yourself in my absence.”

    “I have no idea what you’re talking about.”

    Rio steps closer, lowering her voice. “You think I don’t know? I can feel it, Agatha. Your magic—it’s unravelling. Whatever you’re doing to yourself, it’s killing you.”

    Agatha’s lips curl into a bitter smile. “Oh, is that what you’re here for? To save me from myself?” She leans in, her voice a venomous whisper.

    “You’re not the hero type, Rio.”

    “I’m not the villain here either, Agatha.” Rio’s frustration flares. She grabs Agatha’s wrist, forcing her to still. Her grip is firm but not unkind, and for a moment, Agatha is too startled (and if she admits it to herself, too weak) to pull away.

    “When are you going to stop this?” Rio demands, her voice raw. “It’s been over a decade, Agatha. A decade of you slowly destroying yourself. When does it end?”

    Agatha jerks her arm free and glares at her, the faint tremor in her hands betraying her. “It ends when I say it ends.” Her voice is ice, resolute and dispassionate.

    “You’re wasting away,” Rio stares at her for a long moment, her expression softening into something closer to pity. It’s worse than anger. Agatha can’t bear it. “And for what? To atone somehow?”

    Agatha cuts her a filthy look. “What would I have to atone for except the secrets you spilled to my son?”

    Rio pushes further, brows knit together as she tries to decode this bewildering mortal she loves so dearly. “Then what? Is it to spite me?”

    “Don’t flatter yourself.” Agatha bites out a sharp laugh. “This has nothing to do with you.”

    “The Darkhold then. I should’ve known.” Rio’s expression flattens. “What has it done to you this time?”

    “Nothing that concerns you.” Anymore, goes unsaid.

    Ignoring Rio, Agatha sips at her fresh martini with a careless elegance that belies her physical frailty. So she’s looking a little rundown these days. She’s still the baddest witch in any room she walks in to. She still wields the Book of Sins itself. She’s still Agatha Harkness, sole survivor of the Road. How dare Rio look at her like that, with such pity?

    “Had enough yet?” Death mutters, low and bitter.

    “Of you? Certainly,” Agatha says, not even looking at her.

    “Agatha.” Rio’s voice softens, her worry cutting through the bite. “You’re killing yourself.”

    Agatha lifts her glass as if to toast. “Cheers to that, darling.”

    Before Rio can retort, the lights snap on. The room transforms in an instant, its dimly lit intimacy shattered by the garish white fluorescence. The music cuts, and with it, the laughter and chatter. The room stiffens, every patron exchanging wary glances. They know what this means.

    “Oh, for hell’s sake,” Agatha mutters, placing her glass on the bar with a deliberate clink. “Of all nights.”

    The plainclothes cops come first, their badges gleaming like daggers in the harsh light. Uniformed officers follow, their eyes scanning the crowd with a predatory gleam.

    “Line up! IDs out!” one barks, his voice cutting through the rising murmurs. “This is a police raid! You fucking freaks had better form an orderly line now!”

    Regulars at the Stonewall know the drill—this isn’t the first raid, and it won’t be the last. In the back, queens tug at their wigs and scowl amongst each other, movements trembling but precise as they prepare for the approaching assault. The female patrons bitterly adjust their clothing, ensuring they have at least three “feminine” items to avoid arrest. The tension is palpable, a low hum of fear and anger that seethes just under the surface. People start shifting nervously, whispering to each other as the officers fan out, demanding IDs and dividing the crowd.

    One policeman approaches Agatha and Rio, his piggy little eyes narrowing as he spots her. “ID.” he demands, his tone clipped.

    Agatha doesn’t move, her smile twisting into something sharper. “Do I look like I’m finished with my drink?”

    The officer steps closer, his posture menacing. “Stand up. Now.”

    “Oh, fine.” Agatha sighs dramatically, sliding off the stool with a slow, deliberate grace. She’s taller than the officer in his plainclothes getup, and she makes sure he knows it, staring down at him with a cool, disdainful smile.

    She pats herself down mockingly, pulling out a crumpled ID from her coat pocket and holding it out between two fingers. As he grabs for it, she yanks it back for a second with a mocking, “Don’t wrinkle it.”

    The officer snatches it from her, his scowl deepening but he doesn’t even glance at the card before grabbing her arm. “You’re coming with me.”

    “For what? Ordering a martini at this hour? You’re right, such villainy!” she pouts.

    The officer shoves her forward, her heels scraping against the sticky floor. Agatha snorts, yanking her arm back. “Is this really necessary?”

    Rio perches on her abandoned seat, unnoticed by the officer. She wiggles her fingers mockingly, finishing the last sip of her martini. “Toodle-hoo darling. Have fun at the clink.”

    As the cop yanks her forward, Agatha stumbles, her body frail from weeks of radiation poisoning. Her limbs ache, and her muscles refuse to cooperate. She’s too weak to fight back properly, which only adds to her growing frustration.

    “You’re resisting arrest,” the officer snarls.

    “Spare me the dramatics.” She snarls right back.

    Behind her, the bar is devolving into chaos. As she’s pushed toward the exit, Agatha glimpses queens being manhandled, their wigs ripped off, their carefully applied faces smeared by rough hands. A butch woman curses as two officers outright grope her under the pretence of hunting for her ID card and when young Black queen shouts at them to get off her you pigs, one of them backhands her so hard that her necklace snaps, beads scattering across the floor.

    Agatha’s magic responds almost instinctively; with a weak flicker of her usual violet lightning, the officer suddenly lets out a blood-curdling scream as each of his fingers is abruptly, savagely broken.

    Even as she smirks, she wishes she’d saved the tiny burst of power for herself when she gets outside: a growing crowd presses closer, drawn by the spectacle of police cars and the buzz of whispers spreading along Christopher Street.

    Agatha stumbles down the steps of the bar, shoved through the bustle with ungentle hands. “Hey, hey!” she snaps at them. “Hands off the merchandise, asshole!”

    Behind her, a drag queen in a towering wig poses dramatically as she struts away from the bar, throwing an exaggerated salute at the cops. The crowd erupts into laughter and applause, their mockery cutting through the tension.

    The patrol wagon arrives, the grinding brakes splitting the humid night air. When the cop drags Agatha toward it, she digs her heels in, magic flickering weakly at her fingertips. It’s barely enough to cause the officer to stumble, but it buys her a moment.

    She twists in his grip, her voice rising over the murmur of the crowd.

    “Gentle, officer. I know it’s been a while since you tugged on more than your own prick but it’s bad manners to rough a girl up without asking first.” The crowd snickers, as the officer shoves her harder, tapping his baton menacingly against her ribs, bruising the frail flesh easily.

    “Shut up, dyke!” he barks. “Or I’ll find something to fill that fucking mouth of yours.”

    Agatha smirks, her lips curling into something feral. “You know, I think that’s almost word for word what I told your mother last night-”

    Before she can provoke him further, another scuffle breaks out. A butch woman—broad-shouldered and furious—fights off four cops, shouting expletives as they struggle to shove her into the wagon. Her cries cut through the thick summer air.

    “Why don’t you guys do something?” she yells at the crowd, her voice a rallying cry.

    The mob reacts instantly. A shower of copper-coloured pennies fly forth, showering the crowd and clinking against the wagon, an annoyance at best. But then come the beer bottles, the crash of glass shattering as the tension explodes into violence.

    Agatha is unceremoniously thrown into the wagon, her body colliding with the hard metal bench. She groans, sitting up slowly as her vision spins sickeningly.

    “This is absurd,” she mutters, brushing herself off.

    The door slams shut in her face.

    A moment later, it swings open again: Rio is shoved inside, hands cuffed, someone else’s blood dripping from her mouth. She collapses onto the bench across from Agatha, panting.

    Agatha stares at her, stunned. “What the hell are you doing here?”

    Rio grins, her teeth stained red. “Someone hit me. I hit back.” She shrugs, leaning her head against the metal wall. “Besides, we’re not done talking.” Agatha groans, leaning back against the wagon’s wall with a loud thunk!

    “Unbelievable.”

    Outside, the rebellion continues to rage, the sounds of defiance rising into the warm night air. Inside the wagon, the two women sit in tense silence, the weight of everything unsaid settling between them.

    The police precinct downtown, once awash with fluorescent-lit tedium, is splintering under the weight of the newly arrived arrestees and the updates coming from what is swiftly being called a ‘riot’ down at the Inn.

    Rio and Agatha pay little attention to the situation as it unfolds, still busy sniping at one another over the barrage of chatter and shouting and calls for lawyers.

    “So, this is how the mighty Agatha Harkness ends up,” Rio says, her voice dripping with mockery. “Fingerprinted in some backwater police station because she got sloppy.”

    Agatha groans, lifting her head just enough to glare. “Would you shut up? I’ve got a headache, and your voice isn’t helping.”

    The officer calls for ‘Alma Arlott’ to step forward; she’s hauled to her feet, marched to the processing area. Rio trails behind, smirking.

    Agatha is pushed toward the fingerprint station, where an officer snaps on gloves. She flinches as her fingers are pressed into ink and rolled onto the paper.

    "Do you have to press so hard?" Agatha snaps, wrinkling her nose as the officer firmly guides her fingers onto the paper.

    "Ma'am, it's standard procedure," the officer replies flatly.

    "Standard barbarism, you mean," Agatha mutters, shooting a glare at Rio, who leans casually against the wall. "I’m sure you’re enjoying this."

    "Oh, immensely. Watching you squirm is like Christmas morning." Rio’s smirk is infuriatingly sharp. “The big bad witch, undone by a measly police raid-”

    “Hey, enough from you two!” the intake officer snaps, trying to shove Rio forward.

    He realises with an unpleasant grunt that Rio is far heavier and more solid than her petite form suggests. But in the interests of continuing their argument, she shuffles forward, allows them to print her fingers with some amusement.

    Or try to. The officers blink and glance at each other warily when no mark is made in the ink on the paper. They try again and again while Rio regards Agatha with a cool smirk.

    “Honestly, Agatha, I expected better.”

    “Oh, have I disappointed you?” Agatha sneers. “You can’t know how little that means to me.”

    The next officer looks heaven-ward as if soliciting a lightning bolt to strike him dead before he pushes Agatha in front of the camera for her mug shot. "Turn left." the photographer instructs.

    "Do I look like someone who takes orders?" Agatha snaps back and stalks away, trying and failing to scrub off the residual fingerprinting ink. It’s almost impossible to tell what’s ink and what’s the result of the Darkhold these days, though she’s getting more adept at concealing it.

    Unbidden, Rio steps up next, throwing an exaggerated over-the-shoulder glance. "Make it good. This one’s for my portfolio." she says, earnestly. The cops know when to cut their losses and move on: prints from one, photos of the other. That will have to do tonight. With a clink! the door to their holding cell is thrown shut.

    Rio flops her body down onto the length of the bench with a dramatic sigh, while Agatha perches on the other end, trying to look dignified despite the situation.

    “You could at least thank me,” Rio says finally, breaking the uncomfortable silence.

    “For what?” Agatha asks, incredulous.

    “For coming after you.”

    Agatha scoffs, the sound dripping with disdain. “You didn’t come after me. You came to gloat.”

    Rio’s smirk falters slightly, but she recovers quickly. “True. But that’s just a bonus.”

    “Do you ever tire of your own mouth?” Agatha snaps.

    “You never seemed to.” Rio says, examining her nails.

    Agatha rolls her eyes. “Any more pithy little comments on the backburner or are you done?”

    Rio’s tone sharpens. “I left you alone out of respect. Because I knew you needed time. But I’m not going to sit here and let you pretend that everything we’ve had together doesn’t mean more than a single, stupid fuck-up.”

    “The mistake was ever letting you close to me again.” Agatha spits.

    “I told you-” Rio snarls and then cuts herself off with a sharp inhale, forcing her tone to steady. “We’ve been through this, Agatha. Over and over, we’ve been through this. What is it going to take to move on from this?!"

    Agatha’s lips curl into a bitter, mocking smile. "You think I could ever move on from him, from the loss. It’s laughable. I shouldn’t be surprised. You’ve never understood the souls you steal.”

    “I am sick of explaining myself to you!” Rio snarls. “I didn’t steal him, Agatha, he was never meant to live.”

    Agatha waves a hand dismissively. “Forget it. I’m not interested in another lecture on the cosmic ethics of life and death or the sacred fucking balance or whatever illusion of love you think you have for me.”

    “There is nothing illusory about the way I love you, Agatha Harkness.” Rio says in a low voice, unblinking. “It consumes me. It swallows me, it ruins me. My heart-”

    “You don’t have a heart!” Agatha seethes in a voice low and biting as a whip.

    Rio’s jaw tightens, her nonchalance cracking. “You’re only angry like this because you know that Nicholas was always going to find out eventually, Agatha. I regret telling him, of course I do. But he would have known the truth sometime.”

    “You took him from me twice, Rio.” Agatha stares at the face she had once found such comfort in and marvels at her own stupidity. “And you think I could ever forgive you for that?”

    “You think I wanted to take him?” she demands. “You think I had a choice?!”

    “It seems like you're the only one who ever has a choice.” Agatha hisses. The door clangs open, cutting their argument short.

    “Arlott, your bail’s been paid.” One of the officers grumbles reluctantly. Beside him, a sharp-featured woman strides inside: late forties, with keen eyes and an air of command, her hair coiffed in a simple, no-nonsense style to match her tailored charcoal suit.

    “Miss Harkness,” she says, stressing Agatha’s real name with a dry smile. “I think it’s time we have a chat.”

    Rio scoffs in a low, bitter tone. “Well, aren’t you in demand, tonight?”

    Agatha ignores the quip, instead studies the woman with a leer from head to toe. “And who, may I ask, are you, sweetheart?”

    “You may ask.” The woman says peaceably but makes no move to reply, just walks out with the confidence of someone who knows they'll be followed.

    The interrogation room is cold and sterile, the faint hum of fluorescent lights the only sound. Agatha sits with one leg crossed over the other, her hands folded neatly on her lap. Across from her, the stranger places a thick folder on the table, her movements precise and deliberate.

    “So…Miss Alma Harriet Arlott.” The woman pauses, tilts her head. “A Harlot. Cute.”

    “I thought so,” Agatha demurs with a smirk. “So how about it? You got a name to go with those gams?”

    “Director Margaret Carter,” she introduces after a brief pause, perusing the file. “I’m with the Strategic Homeland Intervention, Enforcement and Logistics Division.”

    Agatha widens her eyes, as if duely impressed. “What a mouthful. So what are you? Some sort of secret cabal or just an overfunded boys’ club?”

    Carter doesn’t look up. “We prefer to think of ourselves as global peacekeepers.”

    Agatha makes a noise of faux admiration. “How sweet, a confederacy of busy-body do-gooders.”

    “Let’s talk about you, Miss Harkness. You’ve certainly been busy." Carter begins, flipping through the file.

    "Busy?" Agatha replies airily. "I don’t know what you mean. I’m on vacation."

    Carter’s eyes flick up, sharp as a blade. “I didn’t know stealing nuclear material qualified as leisure."

    Agatha shrugs. "Depends on your definition of leisure, I suppose.”

    Carter doesn’t bite. "We’ve been aware of you for a long time, Ms.

    Harkness. You’ve avoided making waves, which is why we’ve left you alone. But the uranium changes things."

    “I’m flattered, really.” Agatha preens.

    Carter sets the file aside with a sigh. “You’ve forced our hand, Agatha. So now’s the time to come clean. What are you doing with the uranium?”

    “Uran-?” Agatha’s brow wrinkles delicately. “I’m afraid I’m not familiar. Could you use it in a sentence, perhaps? The dirtier, the better.” She adds with a wink.

    “I assumed at first that you were selling it,” Carter continues thoughtfully. “That seems to be within your talents, if our records are any indication.” Agatha’s smirk lengthens. “Keeping an eye on me, Director? Careful, they'll say we're in love.”

    “My agents suspected you might be working with Roxxon or another competitor of Stark’s,” Carter says thoughtfully. “One of them suggested the Soviets could have hired you, but you don’t seem driven by ideology.” Agatha’s laugh is rich and disdainful. "The Soviets? Oh Peggy, such a sense of humour."

    Pausing, Carter bristles at the name. "You remember me."

    "Of course," Agatha says, her tone saccharine. "Howard Stark’s little watchdog. You’ve certainly climbed the corporate ladder high, haven’t you?"

    Carter’s lips press into a thin line. “This isn’t about me. This is about how SHIELD is going to deal with the threat you pose to national- and let’s face it – international security.”

    “Deal with me? Sounds fun."

    Her eyes darken, her voice clipped. "I assure you, you'll change your tune quickly on that front."

    "Promises, promises, Director,” Agatha teases, her voice honeyed and mocking.

    But Carter is no fool—she watches Agatha closely, noting the faint traces of strain in her posture, the too-casual way she leans on the table.

    “You know, all my sources said you hadn’t aged a day since that night at the Blue Diamond,” Carter muses. “But seeing you myself…you seem weaker now. Frail."

    “You sure know how to make a girl feel special, you know.” Agatha grouses, forcing her smile to remain in place.

    She leans back in her seat, crossing her legs, unfazed. "Alright, then. Let’s try something different. What’s your endgame, Agatha? Why risk exposure now, after all these years?"

    Agatha’s smile doesn’t reach her eyes. "Wouldn’t you like to know?"

    “No more games-” Carter suddenly freezes mid-sentence.

    And Agatha’s fingers curl under the edge of the table, tracing the runes she’d drawn there with the residual fingerprinting ink.

    “But we haven’t even gotten to the good part yet, Director Carter.” Agatha murmurs and leans across the table to press her fingers to the older woman’s temple. “Accipe hancnovam memoriam…”

    It takes more time and more focus than it should. Carter’s mind is a steel vault lined with lead and Agatha’s weakened state from the nuclear material makes the spell harder than usual. Blood gushes from her nose, staining her upper lip. Her hands tingle and then go numb. Her vision blurs until all she can see are Peggy Carter’s blank brown eyes.

    And still Agatha pushes and presses relentlessly against those impermeable walls until-

    “You know nothing of the witch Agatha Harkness.” She finally whispers around a mouthful of blood. “You will destroy all records of her and forget she ever existed. You will refuse to acknowledge any mention of her from now until the end of your days…”

    Carter’s eyes glaze over, her body going slack as the memory of Agatha is ripped from her mind, replaced with something far more benign.

    She slumps back in her chair, exhausted as if she’s gone six rounds with a heavyweight boxer. The wash of tears she summons come easily. "I’m just so worried, ma’am," she weeps, layering in a faint accent for an extra dose of out-of-towner naivety. “It’s been three weeks and I have been praying every night that my Alfie comes home soon.”

    Carter stares at her, bewildered and faintly suspicious. "Alfie?"

    Agatha nods fervently. "Yes, ma’am! My fiancé, well, he's not about to win any awards as the brightest bulb in the box, but I just know something must have happened to him up here! He never would’ve left me at the altar like this otherwise!”

    “I’ll, uh, find someone who can help…” Carter excuses herself with a delicate frown. “Miss…?”

    “O’Connor,” Agatha lies. “Miss Agnes O’Connor.”

    Once she slips from the room, Agatha is quick to follow. The officers look ready to intervene but her bail has indeed been paid and they have bigger fish to fry back at the Stonewall Inn.

    Agatha slips out into the night air with a smirk that lasts about as far as the sidewalk.

    Which is about when she has to stop and hurl her guts up into the alleyway. “Christ, lady, watch where you’re pointing that thing!” one passer by snarls in disgust.

    Without looking, Agatha flips him off.

    “Classy as ever.”

    “What part of ‘leave me alone’ is too complicated for you?” Agatha moans, hugging her stomach with one hand and holding her limp, brittle hair back with the other.

    Rio simply watches her with those unblinking midnight eyes. “This really might kill you, you know. Whatever you’re doing with that book.”

    “Then you should be pleased.” Agatha straightens and tosses her hair back, wiping her mouth with what little dignity she has left. “Because that is the only way I will be speaking with you again.”
