    She walks away without a second glance. And tells herself that is the sound of her heart hardening to stone, not splintering like glass, inside her chest.

    Chapter End Notes History check: - The Stonewall rebellions were a series of violent confrontations between police and LGBTQ+ activists that took place in New York City's Greenwich Village on June 28, 1969.

    - It began in the early hours of June 28th, when the police raided the Stonewall Inn, a popular, Mafia-owned, gay bar that sold liquor without a license, for the second time in a week. As the only gay bar that allowed dancing in the city, it was an extremely popular venue, with around 200 people in attendance that night.

    - During such raids, patrons were ordered to show identity cards and were bodily checked (and groped) to ascertain their biological gender.

    Those dressed in feminine clothing who were discovered to be biologically male were arrested, as were women who wore less than 'three pieces of feminine clothing'.

    - That night, patrons and onlookers fought back, throwing debris and taunting the police. The police responded with undue violence. Similar encounters occurred on subsequent nights, followed by protest rallies.

    - There were earlier events that involved activism in support of the LGBTQ civil rights movement, often involving BIPOC individuals who bore the most aggressive brunt of police brutality and violent.

    However, the events on June 28th did mark a new turn in the modern LGBTQ civil rights movement.

    - Although the Stonewall riots are often referred to as "riots", Stonewall veterans have explicitly stated that they prefer the term Stonewall uprising or rebellion. The police initially used the term "riot" to justify their use of force.

    - The real central character at the heart of the rebellion was a transgender activist named Marsha P. Johnson (the P standing for 'Pay It No Mind') who arrived in the early hours of June 28th after the raid had already started and the Inn itself was already aflame. Johnson's role in the rebellion is well worth the google deep dive if you have a spare moment; her absence in this story was more to keep the focus on Agatha and Rio's bitter sniping as they are scooped up and tossed in jail in the early phase of the uprising.

    BROOKLYN, 1977 Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes BROOKLYN, NEW YORK It’s been so long since Agatha has left the apartment, she’s forgotten what fresh air feels like against her skin. The memory is inconsequential now. Not when she is so close.

    “Veni…eam quod ambulat in spatio inter mundos…”

    The flickering light of a single weak bulb fights against the gloom in the crumbling apartment. Peeling wallpaper droops in damp strips, the air thick with mildew and rot. Agatha kneels in the centre of the room, clutching a small silver disc to her chest, its faint green glow casting sickly shadows over her hollowed features.

    Around her, the cheap drywall glimmers with intricate glyphs unseen for millennia, scrawled in frantic chalk etchings that bleed power into the stagnant air.

    So close now, the words purr against the inside of her skull, vibrations that echo back and forth. That’s it. Keep going.

    She drinks in the praise like nectar, lips twisting in a jittery grin as she chants faster: “Veni, eam quae spirat in tenebris…”

    Good girl. It murmurs, rich and self-assured, brimming with amusement.

    Just a little more. Then she’ll be yours. It will all be yours.

    The uranium glows brighter, saturating the room in green light. Its energy seeps through her trembling fingers, burrowing into her bones, bending to her fractured will. The ancient glyphs hum in response, vibrating with raw, primal force.

    The spell begins to crest—so close now, she can taste it—

    A shadow falls across her.

    “Well, someone's been busy."

    The voice cuts through the room like cold steel. Agatha’s head jerks up to see Rio standing at the edge of the ritual circle, her silhouette impossibly dark against the unholy light. Her sweeping cloak seems to devour the glow, her expression unreadable.

    Agatha scrambles to her feet, unsteady but determined, uranium clattering to the floor at the centre of the circle.

    “I knew you’d come, my darling.” she murmurs. Her voice is rough and sweet, poison wrapped in silk.

    Rio ignores her. “Love what you’ve done to the place.” She muses, inspecting the ritual glyphs that flare with the same faint glow as the uranium. “Very junkie chic.”

    Agatha laughs too loudly, her movements jerky, twitching. “I know, it’s not up to my usual standards. I’ve been working, you know.”

    “Have you now?” Rio raises a brow, her expression a careful mask of indifference.

    “Of course!” Agatha steps closer, her bloodshot eyes wide and brimming with manic sincerity. “All these years apart—so hard, you can’t imagine—

    and, well… I—” Her voice cracks, her grin faltering. “I miss you, Rio.”

    Rio’s gaze darkens just a fraction, the only indication that she has heard the words.

    Agatha’s trembling hand reaches out, hovering near Rio’s throat. Her voice drops to a coaxing whisper. “Come on. Don’t you miss me? Not even a little…?”

    Rio snatches her wrist mid-air. The grip is merciless, bone grinding against bone. “What have you done to yourself now, Agatha?”

    It's a fair question. Years and years of experimentation with chaos magic has taken its toll. Much of her hair has fallen out, even a tooth or two. Her body feels shrunken, like a skin sack stretched thin over her frail bones but it's nothing she can't fix once she's finished.

    “I know, I know,” Agatha admits, her rasping laugh brittle as dry leaves. “I’ve seen better days. But it’s the inside that counts, right? Give me twenty minutes, and I’ll remind you just how much fun we used to—”

    Rio squeezes on the delicate bones in Agatha’s wrist, until they crack with a sickening pop!

    Agatha doesn’t even flinch.

    It’s all the confirmation Rio needs. “That book has its hooks in you now,” she observes in a cold, carefully neutral tone. "You're practically a meatsuit for it to play with these days, aren't you?"

    “You’ve got me all wrong, baby.” Agatha yanks her hand free, the shattered bones snapping back into place with an unnatural crunch. Her fever-bright eyes gleam with cult-like fervour. “I’ve never been more in control.”

    “Bullshit.” Rio’s voice cuts like a whip. “It’s eating you alive.”

    “You can’t understand.” Her twitching intensifies as she staggers backward. “No one can. No one’s come this far. And I’m so close to the end, my love. All I need is a little more power…just a little more and I can fix everything!”

    The air begins to hum, the glyphs surging to life, complex layers working in tandem to compel Rio’s power to the surface. Where Agatha can get to it.

    “You know you can’t absorb my power like you do the others. It’ll kill you.” Rio says, her voice laced with disgust and something deeper. “You know that.”

    Agatha’s hands clench and unclench compulsively. “Not this time. I’m stronger now, I can take it. I can, I know I can.”

    Make her give it to us, the voice insists.

    Rio inspects the glyphs again with disdain. “I suppose this is one of the Darkhold's nasty little hexes. Trying to force my hands, are we?"

    Agatha flattens her scowl, but she can’t conceal the way desperation sharpens her tone. “It’s only meant to give you a little push, my love. Just a nudge in the right direction, that’s all.”

    “The direction that ends with you dead, you mean.”

    Agatha ignores her flat tone. “Give it to me, my love. One good blast,” her lips curl in a playful, jittery smirk. “Come on, you can’t say you’ve never wanted to…”

    It’s so close now, Agatha can taste it.

    But Rio doesn’t move, doesn’t raise her hands or move to strike. “I was ancient when the first pages of that Book were written, Agatha.” She says instead, in a voice low with pity. “What power do you think it holds over me?”

    Around them, the glyphs suddenly flare, and in one blinding flash, they shatter.

    Take it from her! Agatha hears her own voice howl. Take it from her! TAKE IT!

    “You owe me!” Agatha screeches with a rage that is not wholly her own, flying at Rio with fingers curled into claws. “You owe me this-!”

    “I owe you nothing.” Rio shoves her off easily. Agatha crumples, skeletal frame landing with a brittle thud. “Look at you. You’re barely a witch anymore.”

    “I’m more than you could ever understand,” Agatha looks up at her with a manic, furious glower. The grey of her eyes is terrifically blue against the bloodshot veins. “Something beyond your precious fucking balance.”

    Rio regards her with a cold, empty smile. “You know, I’ve always loved that arrogance of yours.”

    “Oh sweetheart, let’s face it, you love everything of mine,” Agatha purrs back with a smile sharp as a razor. “I don’t need a spell to keep you at my beck and call, Rio. You know you'll come for me whenever I like.”

    Rio’s jaw tightens, revealing just a faint crack in her composure. Agatha leaps on it, gleeful in her own viciousness.

    “It was easier than even I thought, keeping you on the hook.” She boasts cruelly. “Just the tiniest hint of affection here and there and you crawled back every time, like a beaten dog.”

    “Enough.” The command is ice and steel.

    Agatha ignores it. “Just look at you. Three centuries later and you’re still here begging for scraps of my attention,” she marvels. “I mean, am I good or am I good-”

    “I said, enough.”

    Rio’s magic slams into her like a freight train, sending her sprawling against the ground with such force that the building itself seems to groan in pain. Bones snap, blood splatters. Agatha only laughs, the sound half howl, half cackle.

    “How does it feel, sweetheart?” she calls out, head lolling. “Being Agatha Harkness’ bitch?”

    Rio pounces on her, pinning her down with her full weight. “I know what you’re trying to do. I’m not one of your Road-seeking idiots, Agatha.”

    Still smirking, Agatha bucks her hips in a cruel facsimile of the golden days they’d once basking in each other’s affections.

    “Come on,” she taunts with another unhinged cackle. “Take a shot. You want to, don’t you? I can see it in your eyes.”

    “It. Would. Kill. You.” Rio repeats, her voice trembling with furious restraint. “You know that. Or you did before you reduced yourself to a rambling, brain addled puppet.”

    Agatha’s feverish smile only widens. “I guess it’s time for Plan B, then.” The deactivated glyphs dissolve in a flash, to reveal a fresh, hidden layer of runes, scratched in Agatha’s own blood.

    One hand flicks towards the walls, igniting the spell; the other reaches for Rio's belt.

    Agatha’s magic blooms with explosive power, colliding with Rio’s in a shockwave of raw energy unleashed upon the world with the speed and terror of an act of God. It decimates the apartment and keeps going, rippling outward in every direction. Lightning flashes across the sky as the magic greedily corrupts every source of power in its path: every lamp, every light, every appliance, every device, every home.

    New York City plunges into darkness in a matter of seconds.

    Rio’s eyes widen- and then she vanishes. As if she was never there to begin with.

    Agatha lies in the silence that follows, trembling with breathless laughter. The only light comes from the runes that still pulse faintly with power. In the distance, the sound of sirens begin to wail in a chorus that promises violent, unrestrained chaos.

    Agatha cannot bring herself to dwell on what new havoc she has unleashed. In her hands, she clutches a long, curved blade. And when the Darkhold begins to whisper again, she smiles.

    Chapter End Notes History check: - The 1977 blackout in New York City was a 25-hour power outage that occurred on July 13–14, when a series of lightning strikes from a storm in Westchester County hit a Con Ed substation on the Hudson River. The blackout caused widespread looting, vandalism, and fires, resulting in an estimated $300 million in damage. More than 3,700 people were arrested, making it the largest mass arrest in New York City's history.

    - The blackout was confined to New York City and its immediate surrounding areas. The looting was intensified due to the city's ongoing financial crisis and high unemployment rates, and the general atmosphere of terror incurred by the Son of Sam murders which were being investigated at this time. It was not until the next morning that power began being restored to those areas affected.

    PS. .....Agatha has gone full Darkhold at this stage guys, but then again, there had to be a reason Rio literally attacks her with a knife in the show, right? Buckle up, it's gonna get worse before it gets better.

    THE FILLMORE, 1986 Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes THE FILLMORE, SAN FRANCISCO The crowd outside The Fillmore stretches all the way down the block; the restless sea of teased hair, leather jackets, and bedazzled jeans thrums with a kind of vibrant chaos, a pulse that radiates right through the pavement. Of course, Agatha isn’t one for queues. She manoeuvres swiftly past the lines and inside the venue with none the wiser, nose wrinkling at the scent of cigarette smoke and sweat. Inside, the concert-goers shift from foot to foot, jostling each other with anticipation.

    She’s unused to the sheer volume of bodies, pressed together. The past decade has been spent in solitude, devoted to her work as she experiments relentlessly with the dagger she won from Death. She’s certain that the reaping knife is the key to unlocking the secrets of that third chapter, Chaos and Rebirth. There’s no time to lose. Even now, Agatha can almost feel Rio hunting for her from beyond the edges of her banishing spell. Nothing like a woman scorned or a cosmic entity enraged, it seems.

    But now, the crowd’s energy is an unrelenting, unbearable buzz against every inch of her skin. Unsettled, Agatha adjusts the illusion she has draped over herself, one inspired by a certain actress so many moons ago. The glamour works in reverse, deflecting attention from her rather than accumulating it. She moves amongst the crowds as just another mortal, plain-faced and slightly out of place.

    But beneath it, she sweeps across the venue with the practiced focus of a hunter fixed to her target. Somewhere in this chaos lies a thief named Lorna Wu.

    Her pretty, serene face is plastered across the venue poster-covered walls. Every glimpse of her sparks fresh outrage for Agatha who can recall the precise moment she overheard a snippet of a familiar tune over the radio. “-and now, the new hit single from Lorna Wu and the Coral Shore,” the radio host had enthused, clearly himself a fan. “You know the one! Here’s the Ballad of the Witches Road!”

    At which point, Agatha sliced her hand open on the goddamn dagger in pure shock.

    “I have learned the lesson,” comes a voice sweeter than birdsong. “Of all that’s foul and fair…Our love was forged in fire…Water, earth and air…”

    Agatha nearly set the damn device on fucking fire.

    Not only has this chittering fool stolen the Ballad, she’s changed the goddess damned words.

    Agatha is out for blood. She wants to rip the tongue out of Lorna Wu’s head, she wants to play cat’s cradle with her vocal cords.

    The outrage is easier to bear than the hurt so Agatha wields her fury like armour against the memories that linger at the edge of her mind. Memories of long grassy fields and wooded glens and a small hand in her own.

    The security guard at the entrance to the backstage corridor is broad-

    shouldered and bored, his arms crossed over a faded Metallica T-shirt. This is evidently not his kind of gig and his lack of passion is obviously affecting his work: Agatha watches as he checks lanyards with all the enthusiasm of someone counting sheep, waving in roadies and technicians with barely a glance.

    She approaches with a sweet smile, close enough for her voice to carry over the din. "Excuse me," she demurs with a flirtatious glance from below her lashes.

    The guard frowns, looking her up and down. "Backstage is for staff only." "Of course," Agatha’s hand brushes ever so lightly against his wrist. Her magic flickers like the hum of an old radio tuning into a station, subtle and unseen, wrapping around his mind with practiced ease. "You’ve already checked my credentials, haven’t you?"

    His frown softens, his brow smoothing as the enchantment takes hold.

    "Yeah," he mumbles, nodding slowly. "Yeah, you’re good."

    "And the lanyard?" Agatha prompts, glancing at the pass hanging from his neck. "You said I could borrow it, remember?"

    He blinks, his hand moving automatically to unclip the lanyard. "Oh. Yeah. Right."

    "Such a gentleman. Thank you," Agatha says with a smirk, plucking it from his fingers. She pats his arm as she steps past him, slipping into the backstage corridor with all the confidence of someone who undeniably belongs here.

    The hallway is dimly lit, narrow and cluttered with cables, crates, and the detritus of a touring band. The sound of the crowd is muffled here, but the energy remains, vibrating through the walls as the stagehands darts past her, their faces tense with focus.

    Agatha inhales deeply, the faint scent of beer and dust intermingled with smoke. She can hear snatches of conversation, the clatter of instruments being tuned, the rustle of fabric as performers prepare for the show.

    Unbidden, it reminds her of the sound stages she infiltrated in Hollywood, or the theatres she snuck around in New York, ages past she has not thought about in…well, a while.

    Her focus sharpens as she walks, her boots clicking softly against the concrete floor. Her fingers brush against the lanyard hanging from her neck and the glamour swiftly transforms: her unremarkable denim-clad concertgoer look morphs into power-suited woman on a mission. She really does enjoy a good shoulder pad, after all.

    She passes a pair of roadies hunched over an amp, their conversation punctuated by bursts of laughter and the unmistakable skunky smell of weed. Neither spare her more than a cursory glance—just another backstage insider in a lanyard.

    The stage wings draw her like a magnet, offering glimpses from the edge of the concert where the dim lights of the stage bleed into the audience, a heaving mass of bodies, their anticipation filling the room like a fever. The energy is electric, a strange alchemy of mortal excitement and the endless hunger for something larger than life.

    Agatha can’t restrain her smirk. Mortals and their incessant need to idolize. “Hey.”

    The voice catches her off guard, which is itself a surprise. Its owner is a little girl, no older than seven, dark hair pulled back in haphazard braids. She regards Agatha with an imperious, faintly suspicious look. “You’re not meant to be here.”

    Agatha raises an eyebrow with mild disdain. “How would you know?”

    The girl bristles, hands on her hips. “Because I know everyone here. And I don’t know you.”

    “Bully for you, kid.” Agatha retorts, brushing an invisible speck of dust from the sleeve of her plum-coloured blazer. “Now off you skip.”

    The girl narrow her eyes. “Skipping is for babies.”

    “And backstage at a concert is for adults.” Agatha snipes.

    She ignores the comment. “You don’t look like a roadie, and you’re way too old to be in the band.”

    Agatha’s lips tighten. “Well, aren’t you just adorable?”

    This, more than anything, seems to rub her the wrong way. “I’m getting security.” She warns with a scowl that would be terrifying on someone a few decades older.

    “Settle down, squirt.” Agatha rolls her eyes. “Or your face will freeze like that.”

    “My mom says that’s an old witches’ tale.” The girl snaps back, annoyed.

    Agatha is about to spell the girl away but the phrase catches her off guard. “An old witches’ tale, hmm? And just whose spawn are you?”

    The girl thrusts her chin forward indignantly. “I’m Alice Wu-Gulliver and Lorna Wu is my mom and she’s gonna get you thrown out of here on your ass.”

    Agatha smirks, inspecting little Alice curiously. “You’re quite the little watchdog, aren’t you?”

    Alice shrugs, unbothered by the condescension. “Someone’s gotta keep an eye on things. People think kids don’t notice stuff, but we do.”

    Agatha regards her for a moment, initial irritation giving way to a grudging respect. Still, she isn’t here to spar with a seven-year-old.

    “Well, since you’re so observant,” Agatha says smoothly, reaching into her pocket, “how about I make it worth your while?” She summons a candy bar, holding it up like a peace offering. “Take me to your mother, and this is yours.”

    Alice’s eyes flicker to the candy bar, but instead of reaching for it, she crosses her arms and cocks a brow. “What do I look like? A rookie? I want cash.”

    Agatha blinks once, then laughs despite herself—an actual laugh, low and genuine. “Cash, is it?”

    Alice nodded, completely unfazed. “You’re not the first person who wanted to meet her, you know. So yeah. Fifty bucks, and I’ll take you to her.”

    Agatha raises her brows, respect turned to admiration. “You’ve got quite the hustle for someone so young.”

    Alice shrugs again, her expression as shrewd as a seasoned negotiator’s.

    “Mom says talent doesn’t pay the bills.”

    Agatha’s lips curl into a smirk. “Your mother is a wise woman.” She reaches into her pocket, producing a crisp fifty-dollar bill and holding it just out of the child’s reach. “Do we have a deal?”

    Alice’s eyes sparkle with triumph. “Deal.”

    Agatha will give credit where credit is due: she’s adept at navigating the labyrinthine passages around backstage, unwavering in her confidence. She leads them into a narrow, cluttered room behind the stage, layered with rugs and scented with the faint, calming perfume of incense, a far cry from the raw energy of the crowd.

    A petite woman with long dark hair looks up as the door opens, relief flooding her pretty features when she sees Alice. Lorna Wu, in the flesh. “There you are!” Her voice trembles slightly with concern that seems out of proportion with a wayward child like Alice. “Alice, you know you’re not meant to run off like that!”

    “Lor, chill out,” one of her bandmate soothes. “You know everyone looks out for Ali around here.”

    Alice, unbothered by her mother’s concern, flashes a toothy grin. “I found a fan for you, Mom.” She jerks her thumb at Agatha with a knowing look, as though this were the most natural thing in the world.

    Lorna barely looks over at Agatha at first, busily inspecting Alice for injury. But the other bandmates are curious enough about this well-dressed woman in their midst.

    With a thought, the lanyard around her neck deftly transforms into a press badge.

    Agatha offers a hand to the guitarist. “Anna Harlin. I’m with Rolling Stones. We’re covering the show tonight.”

    The band look utterly delighted by the prospect, falling over each other to introduce themselves.

    But Lorna looks as unconvinced as her daughter.

    Agatha meets the look of suspicion with a practiced smile of bland, mortal confidence. “We’re working on a piece about ‘The Ballad’, actually. I was hoping for a few words with the auteur herself, if you have a moment.”

    Lorna’s eyes flicker, but she says nothing as she studies Agatha intently. Her bass player nudges her sharply. “Lor would love to chat, wouldn’t you?”

    “Sure.” She murmurs reluctantly, brushing a hand over Alice’s braids.

    “Why don’t you go check on Sam’s tuning skills, sweetheart?”

    Alice gives a dramatic sigh. “Well, someone has to check his work.”

    The guitarist leads the girl away while Agatha pushes her advantage. “So tell me, Ms Wu. What inspired such a…original hit?”

    Lorna’s gaze shifts as they take a seat on a nearby brown suede lounge. “It’s not quite an original,” she says slowly. Her instincts are telling her there is threat nearby but Agatha is well adept at concealing her magic when she wants to. “It’s a folk song, actually.”

    “Really?” Agatha purrs, smothering her tiny flash of rage at the admission. “Do tell.”

    “My mother passed it down to me,” Lorna recounts. “And her mother to her. I felt it could do with a twist, to bring it to a modern audience.”

    Agatha doesn’t miss the subtle avoidance in Lorna’s answer, the way she skirts around the specifics. She presses on it like a fresh bruise. “You know, I did overhear a rumour about that. Do you often look for inspiration in the work of other artists?”

    Lorna bristles, her jaw tightening. “I didn’t steal it, if that’s what you’re implying,” she snaps, her voice edged with a slow but steady temper. “The Ballad is part of my heritage. Part of who I am.”

    As a witch, goes unsaid between the two of them.

    Undaunted, Agatha tilts her head, eyes narrowed slightly in accusation. “So, what made you think of rewriting this ‘heritage’ of yours? Sounds like the point of the tradition is continuity, isn’t it?”

    “That’s exactly why I wrote it.” Lorna leans forward, her voice hardening. “I wanted it to mean something to my daughter when I pass it on to her.

    And now it will.”

    The words hit Agatha harder than she expected. There’s something in the way Lorna says it—something raw, something fiercely protective.

    “Every time she hears it, she’ll understand she’s loved.” Lorna continues, passionately. “And there is nothing more important to me than that.”

    “…I see.” Agatha mutters, biting back the memories each word invokes: days spent wandering the hillsides, nights spent around a fire, a boy who hums the familiar tune by her side, always, always by her side-

    She pushes them away. “How touching.”

    Lorna’s gaze cannot help but soften when she looks across to Alice, who is chiding the guitarist on his pitch. “I do whatever it takes for her,” she says, her tone resolute. “She’s my world.”

    There’s something in her chest, a flicker of something Agatha hasn’t felt in years. It’s a mix of envy and something deeper—something she’s not entirely ready to face.

    “Do you have children?” Lorna enquires, as if sensing the shift in Agatha. She stiffens, her breath catching slightly and despite her intention to lie, the words slip off her tongue: “Yes. A son, actually.”

    Lorna watches her closely, a faint smile tugging at her lips. “I can tell. Moms have a way of recognising other moms.”

    Just like witches have a way of sensing other witches. But for once, Agatha swallows whatever waspish remark her instincts would have her spit.

    Just in time. A stage producer’s voice breaks through the tension, sharp and insistent. “Lorna, we’re ready for you on stage. Let’s go.”

    Lorna nods, but her eyes linger on Agatha for just a moment longer, still ever so faintly suspicious. “It’s been nice talking to you, Miss Harlin.” she says politely. “But I’ve got a show to put on.”

    Agatha forces a stiff smile in response and watches as Lorna presses three kisses to Alice’s cheeks before she heads out. Another old witches’ tale, that three kisses will protect your child from evil.

    Agatha knows it well. She used to kiss Nicky just the same.

    Even thinking his name leaves her breathless. Like a floodgate, she can suddenly picture his face so clearly, can imagine his laughter and his voice so easily.

    On stage, that telltale melody begins to roll out amidst the screams of an utterly fixated crowd.

    Down, down, down the Road…

    Agatha had come here for retribution but what is Lorna Wu if not Agatha herself three centuries ago, a mother singing for her child?

    Lorna Wu performs the Ballad to crowds that scream her name, she shares it as part of the legacy she plans to offer her daughter. Agatha uses it to drain the power from witches too stupid and sharp-tempered to know better. She tries to leave quickly but the Ballad chases her down the stage corridors and passages.

    “If I can’t reach you,” Lorna calls after her in a voice sweet as honey. “Let my song teach you all you need to keep our love alive…”

    Every word punctures a corner of her heart she’d been ignoring for a long time, the piece that would always belong to Nicky. Had she forgotten the end to all this? Had she lost sight of where this road of hers was headed? Even as she bursts through the stage door into the night air, her lungs heaving with a mix of ache and panic, she can feel it in the distance: the Darkhold beckons, offering her power beyond every dream. The promise of losing herself in its complexities is seductive but Agatha cannot bring herself to return to it, not yet.

    Leaning against the filthy brick wall of the alley behind the theatre, she instead presses her hands to her belly, tries to remember how it felt to grow new life within her. Those memories pour from the wounds Lorna’s ballad has made inside her.

    Nicholas, Nicholas, Nicholas. It’s all been for him, of course it’s all been for him but when had she lost sight of him? When had she surrendered to the Darkhold’s grasp so completely that he had slipped from her focus? How could she have allowed such a thing to happen?

    The answer hits her like a hammer to the head. Rio is the only creature to remember Nicholas like she does. Rio is the only one who shares in the memory of him, who can understand the absence of him.

    Below her jacket, the reaping knife is an ancient, heavy weight against her. Agatha slowly withdraws it, studies her reflection in its silver blade. The cruel truth is, she doesn’t even remember taking it from Rio, not really. All she can recall is the blazing heat of her rage. But how much of that was her, Agatha finds herself wondering. She has long styled herself as the Wielder of the Darkhold.

    Now, she isn’t so sure she isn’t the one being wielded.

    Chapter End Notes I will die on this hill. Alice Wu-Gulliver deserved a better fate.

    History check: - The Fillmore Theatre is a historic music venue in San Francisco, and was the focal point for the psychedelic music scene during the 1960s and 1970s, helping to launch the careers of the biggest names of the era such as The Grateful Dead, Jimi Hendrix, Led Zeppelin, and The Doors. Fleetwood Mac performed at the Fillmore West in San Francisco on January 17, 1969.

    - And now, because no one cares: it's hard to accurately date Lorna Wu and the Coral Shore from the show. During Alice's trial, the show drew from classic seventies performers such as Cher, Diana Ross, Mick Jagger, Liza Minelli and Janice Joplin.

    - However many of the performers that inspired the show cultivated their aesthetic in the seventies and maintained it throughout the eighties, which makes it feasible that the Coral Shore might have also held onto their seventies-esquesignature style over the years. Alice's actress, Ali Ahn, was also born in 1981, which further informed the DOB I picked for Alice in this fic, which makes her 44 years old at the start of the show.

    SAN FRANCISCO, 1989 Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes SAN FRANCISCO, CALIFORNIA The earth itself has swallowed up the city. Agatha kneels amid the wreckage of what was once her home. Dust floats in beams of dim light filtering through fractured walls, settling over her like a shroud.

    Around her lies a wasteland.

    Entire blocks have been reduced to smouldering rubble; the horizon is jagged with collapsed buildings. The sun dips low over the city’s choking dust clouds, painting the destruction in fiery hues of orange and gold.

    Somewhere beyond this shell of a neighbourhood, sirens wail through the chaos. The muffled sobs of survivors echo through the streets, underscored by the intermittent crash of more structures succumbing to gravity’s pull. The book lies open in her lap, its pages thrumming with a dark energy that vibrates through her very bones. The air around her is charged with the lingering remnants of power she barely comprehends. But there is no elation, no triumph in this moment.

    Agatha’s breath rattles in her chest, shallow and uneven, as she gazes at the dark, arcane symbols inscribed on the brittle parchment. Each one burns in her mind, each syllable a bitter echo of the price she’s paid. She has wrestled the secrets free from the unyielding tome that had kept them hidden for centuries. It was supposed to be a moment of victory, the culmination of decades—no, lifetimes—of toil and sacrifice.

    But Agatha’s bloodshot eyes can only study the jagged skyline, the skeletal remains silhouetted against a sky painted in hues of ash and fire. She had known there would be a cost. She had whispered to herself a hundred lies, telling herself it was necessary. That she would do whatever it took. That she could live with the consequences.

    She had wielded the raw power of the reaping dagger for less than a second and as the uttered that final incantation, the Darkhold's last and most secret chapter opened to her. And so did the earth itself, splitting with a guttural roar to consume everything in its path in a quake that devastated thousands. As always, she sits in the wreckage of her own making.

    The tremor in her hands worsens. She places them flat against the rubble beneath her, trying to steady herself, but the sensation of splintered wood and shards of glass pressing into her palms only grounds her further in this horrifying reality. Her body aches, every muscle screaming from the toll of the ritual, from channelling forces she was never meant to wield.

    A cough wracks her chest; she spits blood onto the debris. The taste of copper lingers on her tongue, bitter and metallic.

    She should get up. She should move. But the weight of her exhaustion is insurmountable. Her knees buckle. She collapses further into the ruins before she can take more than a step.

    The air is heavy with silence now, broken only by the faint sound of something distant and familiar: a low hum, like static crackling before a storm. Her heart stutters.

    The faint scent of ozone cuts through the dust. And then Death steps amongst the ruins.

    Her entrance is unceremonious—no grand burst of power, just a slow, deliberate stride through the jagged debris. Her long green cloak trails over the rubble, her presence sharper than the broken glass scattered around her feet. Rio surveys the wreckage, her lips curling into a cold, mirthless smile. “Honey,” her sing-song voice drips with venom. “I’m home.”

    Agatha can barely stand to look at her. Her fingers twitch, but she offers no response.

    Rio clicks her tongue, feigning disappointment as she inspects the ruins, kicking over broken pieces of wood and plaster as she goes. “What? No witty retort? No clever deflection? That’s unlike you, sweetheart.”

    Agatha blinks slowly, her voice hoarse when she finally speaks. “Take it.” She gestures weakly toward the familiar curved blade lying on the rubble- strewn floor. “It doesn’t matter anymore.”

    “How magnanimous of you.” Rio sneers as she summons the dagger to her palm, wiping away a smear of soot with her thumb.

    “It doesn’t matter anymore.” Agatha can only repeat. Every ounce of her energy has been depleted, burned out. She’s a husk of a witch, empty as all those she’s drained dry over the years.

    Rio stops near the Darkhold, kicks at it with her boot as if it is some filthy, haggard thing. “Well look at that,” she muses in a voice dripping with mockery. “You finally did it.”

    The final chapter lies open, ancient secrets spilled at long last.

    Agatha finally moves, her hand twitching toward the book. “Don’t-” she says softly, her voice hoarse and weak.

    “Don’t?” Rio arches a brow, straightening and letting the word hang in the air. Her cool detachment begins to crack. “That’s all you have to say to me after everything? Don’t?”

    With a flick of her wrist, a jagged bolt of violently green spectral energy erupts from her palm.

    It arcs toward Agatha, striking the ground inches from her feet with a deafening crack. The force of the blast sends debris flying, a plume of dust engulfing the room.

    Agatha flinches but doesn’t defend herself further. Her hands will not cooperate, her muscles have turned to stone.

    When the dust clears, Rio’s expression has shifted. The icy calm begins to melt, giving way to the smouldering fury beneath.

    “You know, I imagined this moment differently,” she says, pacing again like a caged beast. “I thought you’d fight. That you’d try to talk your way out of it. But this? This defeated, pathetic act? It’s a little beneath you, don’t you think?

    Rio hand flicks forth again and another blast of magic follows: this one strikes at the heart of her, sends Agatha’s body flying backward, landing hard amongst a pile of unforgiving sharp-edged rubble.

    Ruthless as ever, Rio releases another wave of magic, this one ripping through the air like a storm of knives. Splinters of wood and shards of glass spray everywhere and then freeze, halted in the air just inches from Agatha’s skin, waiting breathlessly to slice her apart.

    But Agatha can raise no hand in her own defence. “Do what you came here to do.”

    Rio freezes in place, that beautiful face flickering with her deathly skeletal form as if she can scarcely control her rage. Her magic sparks faintly at her fingertips, electric green and ready to strike.

    “Do what I came here to do?” she repeats, her voice low and trembling with restrained anger. “Oh, no. That’s far too easy. Tell me the truth Agatha. Tell me what mess you’ve made of yourself now.”

    “I thought…” Her words falter.

    “I said,” Rio grabs her by the hair, claws digging into her scalp until she bleeds. She forces Agatha to meet her eyes. “You will explain yourself.”

    And Agatha hates being helpless. She hates losing the advantage. She hates being weak. But mostly she hates herself in this moment, starting up at Death with nothing but her own failure to offer.

    “I thought I could bring him back.”

    Rio stiffens. She releases her instantly, takes a step back as though repelled by the weight of Agatha’s confession. “You…you what?”

    “I thought I could bring him back.” Agatha repeats weakly. “The last chapter, Chaos and Rebirth, I…I hoped it could bring him back to me.”

    She thought admitting that would be the worst part. But no. The worst part is the way Rio looks at her, as if she doesn’t even recognise her.

    Her midnight eyes are endless and empty. “You’ve been chasing this…

    this,” Rio gestures wildly at the Darkhold, “To resurrect Nicholas? Are you…You are unbelievable.”

    The shards of glass hovering menacingly around Agatha’s body tremble with the words.

    “All this time, I thought you wanted power. That you wanted to…to rule.”

    Rio’s fury is a cold, biting thing, her voice trembling with barely restrained rage. “Not this. Not when you know what happens to the souls dragged back from the afterlife. You arrogant, selfish—” She stops herself, exhaling shakily.

    Agatha’s voice is barely above a whisper. “It doesn’t matter. I can’t do it.” Rio’s brow twitches. “Explain. Now.”

    “The Darkhold… it’s not for me.” A lone, slightly unhinged giggle escapes her. “The power it describes, the chaos magic—it isn’t mine to wield. It belongs to the Scarlet Witch.”

    The final chapter glorifies this unholy creature at length, explaining in detail the circumstances and scope of her power. It revels shamelessly in the destruction she will unleash: The Scarlet Witch is not born; she is forged. She has no coven or need for incantation. It is her destiny to destroy the world.

    For a moment, the room feels impossibly still, save for the distant sounds of the city’s chaos.

    And then Rio lets loose an ear-splitting cackle.

    “All of this,” she marvels. “All of this has been about the Witch?”

    Agatha feels something in her gut curdle and die. “You know about-?”

    “She’s expected to end everything, Agatha.” Rio seethes. “Of course, I know about her.”

    A sob builds in Agatha’s chest. “You…you knew…”

    “Centuries of destroying everything and everyone around you,” Rio marvels. “All for nothing. Well, I have to hand it to you, sweetheart.” Her voice drops any hint of mirth. “No one does ruination like Agatha Harkness.”

    Agatha can only accept the wounds torn open with each word. Her voice cracks as she speaks. “I had to try,” she whispers. “I couldn’t just… let him stay gone.”

    And finally, finally, Rio’s fury boils over. The hovering shards of wood and plaster and glass explode into dust around them.

    Agatha shields her face but doesn’t rise, doesn’t fight back. What point is there now? She’s ready to meet whatever fate awaits her.

    “You would defy the natural order of life and death itself. You would rip apart the balance of the universe just to ease your pain. And now you expect pity?” Rio steps closer, crouching so that their faces are inches apart. Her voice drops to a deadly whisper, cold and cutting. “No, this is your reward, my love.”

    The words strike Agatha with brutal precision, but she doesn’t respond. She doesn’t argue. She doesn’t even cry. There is nothing left in her this time, truly.

    Rio stands abruptly, straightening to her full height. The dagger glints in her hand, its power thrumming in the air between them. For a moment, it seems as though she might strike. But then, with a growl of frustration, she steps back, lowering the weapon.

    “I should kill you,” she mutters. “I should end this. But that would be too kind.”

    Abandoning Agatha to the fust, Rio stalks away without a backward glance, the glow of her magic fading as she vanishes into the void.

    “All hail Agatha Harkness,” she calls out over her shoulder, in a voice flippant with mockery. “Queen of the fucking ashes at last.”

    Chapter End Notes History check: - The Loma Prieta earthquake struck the Bay area on October 17, 1989, causing widespread destruction. Registering a 6.9 on the moment magnitude scale, the epicentre was thought to be in the Nisene Marks State Park, near Loma Prieta Peak in the Santa Cruz Mountains.

    It lasted around 15 seconds and affected the majority of the Bay Area including San Francisco, Oakland, Alameda, Santa Clara, Santa Cruz, and Monterey. 63 people died as a result while a further 3,757 were injured and an estimated $6.8 billion in direct damage was incurred.

    NOGALES, 1996 Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes NOGALES, ARIZONA The sun hangs low and heavy in the desert sky, bleaching the edges of everything it touches.

    Agatha moves through the dusty outskirts of Nogales, her wide-brimmed hat tipped low, shielding her eyes but doing little to hide her. Her steps are measured, deliberate, her presence unmistakable. The air ripples with heat, thick with the mingling scents of charred meat from street vendors, dry sagebrush, and the faint, acrid bite of suspicion.

    They watch her. They always do. She’s lived here for years now, far from every place she once called home but it doesn’t seem to matter. The old abuelitas on shaded porches still clutch their rosaries tighter as she passes, whispered prayers swallowed by the droning of cicadas. Children peek from behind their mothers’ skirts, their wide eyes curious and frightened all at once. Even the shopkeepers behind cracked windows and faded awnings offer only strained smiles, their wary gazes tracking her every move.

    Fear is easier to navigate than familiarity, she’s learned.

    The bell above the convenience store door chimes faintly as she pushes it open. The fluorescent lights inside hum like restless insects, bathing the cramped aisles in harsh, unnatural light. The air is cooler but stale, tinged with the faint metallic tang of refrigeration.

    Agatha moves briskly, plucking items from the shelves with practiced efficiency: rice, coffee, a loaf of bread already stiffening at the edges. Essentials.

    The thought of such a banal weekly routine had felt impossible in the beginning. After that fateful day, she moved restlessly around California, wallowing in the culmination of her lost dreams, her failures. Having banished the Darkhold to whatever interdimensional fold she could summon, it was the first time in decades she was truly, utterly alone.

    Arizona was hot, dusty, remote and overwhelmingly dull, all the things Agatha did not care for, but it was quiet. There were no memories here that could haunt her. The locals were superstitious sorts who watched the strange, lonely woman from a healthy distance.

    Agatha is halfway through reading the ingredients on a bag of flour—

    mostly to kill time until the bakery aisle cleared of gossiping mothers—

    when she hears the faint rustle of robes and the sound of boots deliberately stopping just short of her cart.

    She looks up, raising a sardonic brow at the two figures standing before her. Well, most of the locals.

    The older priest, his brown face creased by years of frowning, regards her as though she were a stain on his Sunday vestments. “Miss Harkness.

    Shopping, I see?”

    Agatha tilts her head, feigning surprise. “Well, Father, I was planning my weekly sacrifice to His Dark majesty, but the butcher is fresh out of chicken heads. You know how supply chains are these days.”

    The younger man standing beside him barely stifles a laugh, earning a sharp glare from his elder.

    He clears his throat, stepping forward with a disarming smile that Agatha immediately distrusts.

    “Father Elias,” he introduces himself earnestly. “I’ve just recently joined the parish. I was hoping to introduce myself and extend an invitation—”

    “To the church bake sale? Already a step above the last exorcism invite.” Agatha interrupts, smirking.

    Father Elias chuckles nervously. “Actually, we’re hosting a community dinner this Friday. We’d love for you to join us.”

    Agatha leans against her cart, fixing him with a look that could wither the cheap checkout roses right off their stems. “Oh? Did someone dared you to invite the local bruja ?”

    Father Elias looks taken aback, even as he tries to recover. “No, not at all. Everyone’s welcome-!”

    The older priest cuts in with a voice like the snap of a ruler. “Miss Agatha’s involvement in community affairs has historically been…

    counterproductive.”

    “Flattery will get you everywhere, padre. ” She winks.

    The priest stiffens, but before he can respond, a sudden beep from the nearby register cuts through the tension.

    Agatha straightens and smiles, nodding to the honey in her hand. “I’d better pay for this. The Lord might work in mysterious ways but a girl’s still gotta eat, right? Have a blessed evening, fathers.”

    The cashier at the counter is young, pretty and barely seems to realise Agatha is standing there, too busy mooning over the young Father Elias’

    boyish charms.

    “Lusting after a man of the cloth, huh, kid?” Agatha interjects, amused. “I like your style.”

    The girl flusters, caught off guard. “I wasn’t…I mean, I don’t…”

    Agatha smirks, snagging a nearby Hershey’s Kiss from the counter display. The infatuation hex is child’s play: once cast, she tosses it to the girl with a wink. “One little bite and you’ll have the good padre gagging for more.”

    “Really?” the cashier’s gaze locks on the foil wrapped hungrily. “You mean it?”

    Agatha pouts. “Would I lie to you?”

    But before the girl can reply, a throat clears behind her. Agatha glances back to find a grizzled old cowboy impatiently waiting, his expression carved from stone, his disdain barely masked.

    Agatha straightens, her smirk returning. She pulls a crumpled bill from her pocket and slides it across the counter. “Keep the change,” she says lightly. “And good luck with Father Hottie.”

    Mortals are so terribly predictable.

    She exits the store, preparing to cut through the alley behind the car park on her way back to her ramshackle little bungalow cottage. Her fingers tingle from casting the hex but not so much as she might have expected. She’d expended so much of her energy that day, opening the last chapter of the Darkhold. Burned out every ounce of power she’d accrued over the years and then some.

    It is slow going, restoring her reserves. It takes more patience than Agatha usually has for these kinds of things but for once, there’s no rush. Her goals have been defeated. Her plans, dashed. All she can do, all any of them can do, is survive.

    And on that cheery little note.

    She’s out of sight of the convenience store when it happens. A blinding pain errupts behind her eyes, accompanied by the sharp, sickening crack of something hard against her skull.

    The world tilts violently, then plunges into darkness.

    She wakes to the smell of damp earth.

    “You’re awake, Miss Harkness. That’s good.” The voice is calm, warm even, with a lilting confidence that immediately sets her teeth on edge. “My friend here was a little overzealous in subduing you. You have such a reputation, you see.”

    “Oh, Father Hottie,” Agatha moans, wincing through her throbbing head.

    “How could you?”

    “Just Elias, please.” the young priest replies patiently. He’s traded his priestly robes for a simple button-down and rolled-up sleeves, but the glint of a silver crucifix still hangs at his throat.

    Agatha squints through the gloom, her vision swimming. Two more figures hover at his shoulder, young and scrappy, looking to Elias nervously for instruction. He stands a little apart from the others, his boyish features and kind eyes now edged with something darker.

    Elias smiles faintly, his expression disarmingly serene. “It took us a while to find you. After that mess in San Francisco, even I lost your trail for a while.”

    “I’ve had practice,” Agatha mutters, wincing as she shifts her wrists against the magical ropes. “What is this? Late-night Bible study?”

    “You know why we brought you here,” Elias says, his voice calm but firm.

    His comrades shift uneasily behind him, nudging one another.

    “Do I?” Agatha arches a brow, letting her smirk widen. “Because from where I’m sitting, it looks like I’m about to get a free baptism from your little youth group.”

    One of the others—a wiry girl with tightly braided hair—glares at her. “You think you’re funny?”

    “I think you’re predictable,” Agatha retorts, looking past her to Elias. “So what’s the deal, padre? Priest gig not paying enough so you decided to dirty dance with the devil instead?”

    Elias chuckles softly. “I’m not here to debate theology with you, Agatha. I’m here for the Road.”

    Agatha lets her head fall back against the tombstone behind her, laughing. “That old thing? You really fell for that? What are they teaching in seminary school these days?”

    Elias’s smile doesn’t falter, but his comrades glance uneasily at one another. The pale boy with a mop of dark curls whispers something to the girl beside him, who shakes her head nervously.

    “I know you walked it, Agatha.” Elias insists. “All that power you let loose in San Francisco had to come from somewhere.”

    “Typical,” she sniffs. “I bet if I was a man, you’d be commending my work ethic.”

    “You walked the Road, you gained your prize.” Elias continues, his patience thinning. “I’ve been studying your histories, Agatha. I know all about that son of yours, I know about the Book of Sins.”

    The words cut deeper than Agatha lets show, but she masks it with a sharp laugh. “Someone’s been reading my diary, huh? Naughty boy.”

    Elias sighs, rubbing his forehead. “I wanted to do this the right way. Ease you in, bring you into the coven.”

    The girl’s eyes widen. “You wanted to what?”

    “She kills her covens, dude.” The floppy haired boy looks equally stunned. "Yeah, dude." Agatha mimics, mockingly.

    Elias ignores them all. “But if you want to be difficult-”

    “Oh, I insist on it.”

    “-then we can do that too.”

    Agatha stares at him for a moment, then bursts out laughing. “You really think you’ve got this under control, don’t you?”

    Elias’s lips press into a thin line, but his expression remains calm—too calm. It’s a mask Agatha recognizes all too well.

    “Father Hottie,” she coos, her tone dripping with condescension. “You’ve read my histories, have you? And here I thought priests were supposed to focus on the Good Book.”

    “You’re low on power, Agatha. I can sense it.” He insists. “You don’t want to do it like this. Show us how to open the Road and we’ll go down it together. You can be the witch of legend again.”

    Agatha’s smile sharpens, though the words sting. “Well, don’t you have me all figured out, playing babysitter to these...what are they, interns?

    Adorable. Tell me, do you hand out punch cards? Ten rituals, and you get a free latte?”

    The girl bristles, stepping forward, but Elias raises a hand to stop her. His patience is clearly wearing thin, a flicker of something dark crossing his boyish features.

    “I’m offering you a chance here,” he says, his voice low and measured. “An opportunity to reclaim what you’ve lost.”

    “What I’ve lost?” Agatha scoffs, tilting her head with mock curiosity. “And what, exactly, do you think I’ve lost? My patience? My car keys? My irrepressible good looks?”

    “You’ve lost your way,” Elias counters, stepping closer. The silver crucifix around his neck catches the dim moonlight, gleaming as he looms over her. “The Road could change that. For you, for us. You could lead us.”

    “Lead you?” Agatha lets out a bark of laughter. “I don’t lead witches like you. I eat them for breakfast.”

    “Careful,” he warns, his voice dropping. His companions glance at him nervously, the tension in the air thickening like a storm about to break. “Or what?” Agatha grins wickedly, her teeth flashing. “You’ll bore me to death with another sermon? Stick to playing dress-up, Father Hottie, you’re not the fearless leader type.”

    Elias’s expression hardens, the flicker of darkness igniting into a flame. “You don’t know anything about me.”

    “I know enough.” Agatha’s grin widens, and her voice takes on a cruel edge. “I know that you’re weak. And no amount of borrowed power will ever change that.”

    The words hit their mark. With a sharp, angry motion, Elias raises his hand, magic crackling to life around his fingers.

    “You want power?” he snarls. “Let me show you.”

    The blast of energy surges toward her, but Agatha doesn’t flinch. The moment it strikes, she gasps, her body jolting as the force of it courses through her. And then, as the pain fades, she feels it: the sweet, intoxicating rush of power filling the hollows within her, mending her wounds, reigniting the embers of her depleted magic.

    “Thanks,” she breathes, lips curving into a smile that sends a chill down Elias’s spine. “I was feeling a little under the weather.”

    Elias staggers back, his eyes wide as he realizes what’s happening. “No—” But it’s too late. Agatha laughs gleefully as the energy flow reverses, no longer expelling but draining inch by glorious inch until-

    The body drops amidst the graveyard grasses, wizened to a husk. All his boyish good looks ruined in an instant.

    Elias’s comrades scatter, their screams echoing into the night. She considers going after them but it seems like a chore. Instead, she nudges Father Hottie’s newly made corpse with her toe.

    “Should’ve stuck to the Good Book,” she mutters snidely.

    “I’m not sure mercy suits you, Agatha.” The voice slices through the stillness like a blade dragged across stone—dry, sharp, and painfully familiar.

    Agatha stiffens, her head snapping toward the source.

    There she is.

    Perched atop a half-sunken tombstone, Rio sits like a queen surveying her domain. Her skeletal face is unexpectedly adorned with vivid hues and intricate swirls, patterned like a calavera de azúcar sprung to life.

    Glimmering eerily in the moonlight, the hollow voids of her eyes hold an unearthly promise.

    Agatha’s breath hitches, though she will never admit it. Rio has always been a figure of inevitability, but here, in this painted form, she seems… more. Ethereal, otherworldly. She can’t look away.

    “Mercy has its uses,” Agatha shrugs, her voice as cool as the air around them. She masks the unease curling in her chest like smoke. “Besides, not every fool deserves to die.”

    “Spoken like a fool.” Rio retorts flatly.

    “What are you doing here, Rio? You look…” Agatha waves a hand vaguely toward her face. “Festive.”

    Rio tilts her head, the faintest twitch of her painted jaw resembling a smile. “It happens sometimes. When I walk among those who worship me. This is how they see me— lively, celebrated. Beautiful.”

    Well, Agatha can't argue with that. Come to think of it, November has come on quick this year; the border town has been littered with brightly coloured Dia de los Muertos paraphernalia for some time.

    She raises her brows thoughtfully. “A goddess, then. Do you enjoy it, playing their idol?"

    Agatha is still not certain this won't devolve into the kind of bloodsport that seems to be par for course for them these days. She hasn't seen Rio in years.

    Rio’s gaze unexpectedly flickers, as though drawn to some unseen force above her. “It’s reflexive. I don’t control it.” She pauses, then adds, almost absently, “Do you enjoy it?”

    “Enjoy what?”

    “Playing their villain.” Rio eyes the body of the recently deceased witch. “It seems to suit you.”

    Agatha snorts softly. “Does it matter?”

    “Everything about you matters,” Rio says sharply, her voice like a lash.

    “Even the things you pretend don’t.”

    The words hang between them, laden with meaning neither can name.

    Agatha crosses her arms, forcing herself to hold Rio’s gaze. “I doubt you came all this way to critique my moral compass.”

    For a moment, Rio is silent, as if deciding whether to answer.

    “Were you always so certain?”

    Agatha frowns, caught off guard. “Certain of what?”

    Rio’s hollow eyes bore into her. “When you schemed, fought, lied—did you truly believe you were right?”

    Agatha lets out a bitter laugh. “That was never the point, was it?"

    "Indulge me." It's not a request.

    The exhaustion in her sigh is obvious. "Survival doesn’t give a damn about right or wrong." She says eventually. "It’s about staying ahead of the wolves at your heels, whether they wear crowns or carry pitchforks.”

    "Survival above all else then." Rio surmises, half to herself.

    Agatha hesitates, then adds, almost reluctantly, “Survival and… maybe a little peace.”

    Rio chuckles humourlessly. “You are the last thing that comes to mind when I think of peace.”

    “Not my peace.”

    Rio’s painted jaw shifts as if grinding her skeletal teeth. “You mean to tell me it was all for my sake? I’d be touched if it weren't such an obvious lie.” “Maybe not entirely,” Agatha snaps, composure slipping. “But don’t pretend I didn’t care. I may have been selfish, but I wasn’t heartless.”

    “No,” Rio agrees, her voice low and dangerous. “Not heartless. Just ruthless. Everything you did, you did for yourself.”

    “And what if I did?” Agatha shoots back, her voice rising. “Do you want an apology? You think I owe you some grand confession?"

    "I think we both know what you owe me."

    Agatha sneers. "You’re no saint, Rio. You loved the game as much as I did. Don’t act otherwise.”

    Rio moves faster than light itself; her bony hand shoots forward, seizing Agatha in an icy grip that somehow burns her anyway. The inches between their bodies crackles with electricity, the weight of Rio’s presence suffocating.

    "Let's not get it twisted, Harkness." Rio tightens her grip on Agatha's throat, and it sets every nerve in her body ablaze. "You're only alive at all because I allow it."

    "I'm alive," Agatha snarls back. "Because there's not a damn thing in this world or the next that makes you feel the way I do."

    Her pulse thunders in her ears, but she forces herself not to flinch. She refuses to show weakness in front of this creature, never ever again.

    Rio’s hand falls away; her fingers ghost over Agatha’s skin for a lingering moment. “You’re arrogant,” she murmurs, almost to herself. “Arrogant and selfish and reckless.”

    “And yet,” Agatha rasps, her voice regaining strength, “Here you are.”

    Rio steps back abruptly, as if burned. Her gaze shifts back to the night sky above them, her skeletal face unreadable. “He reminds me of you.” she says softly, almost detached.

    “He?” Agatha frowns. “He who?”

    “He worships, he venerates. And yet…” Rio’s voice trails off.

    Ah. Agatha forces a bitter smile. “How charming. A new admirer to grovel at your feet.”

    Rio's painted jaw curles in a faint, mocking smile. “Is that bitterness I hear?”

    “Of course not,” Agatha retorts sharply.

    Rio hasn’t looked back at her yet. Agatha hates how much she wants her to.

    “I’ve been worshipped before, you know.” She says instead. “By saints, queens, savants. Candlelit Genevieve, Ennigaldi and her treasures. Ever-

    thinking Al-Kindi and oh, Tomoe. Holy hell, now that was a woman."

    Agatha scoffs at the litany. “As marvellous as it is having girl talk about your many fabulous exes, is there a point to this little saga?”

    “Renegades,” Rio murmurs vaguely as if she hadn’t spoken. “In their own ways. In all things but their love for me. Me, they revered.”

    Rio finally looks toward her, as if she’s studying the delicate symmetry of Agatha’s bones for answers. “But this one reminds me of you. His worship…it’s defiant. He prays with one hand and plots with the other. I wonder if he’ll destroy himself, too.”

    Agatha bristles but forces her tone to stay even. “In case you didn’t notice, I survived.”

    “For now.” Rio replies ominously.

    Agatha’s temper flares. “Do you want to know what I think?”

    Rio glances at her, unamused. “I’m breathless to hear it.”

    “I think you like your worshippers defiant,” Agatha prowls closer, her voice lowering to a seductive purr. “You like insolence.”

    She moves into the space between Rio’s legs like she belongs there, until their bodies are less than inches apart and that crackling energy is back with a vengeance.

    “I think you spend your whole existence upholding your precious sacred balance because if you didn’t, you’d tear it to shreds yourself.” Agatha whispers, gleefully. “And all you want is someone else to do it for you.” Rio has not moved an inch, but she doesn’t need to. Her eyes are fixed to Agatha’s, her breathing has ceased. Agatha knows all her tells. Agatha knows her .

    “And that’s you, is it? My holy destroyer? My monster?” Rio murmurs back, half-scornful, half-hopeful.

    But the words hit hard, harder than Agatha had expected. For once, she has no reply.

    They stare at one another under the moonlight and wonder how they could understand a stranger so perfectly.

    And then Rio stiffens, tilting her head as if listening to something far away. “I’m needed elsewhere,” she murmurs. “This has been…enlightening.”

    “Rio—” Agatha reaches out, but Rio is already dissolving into shadow, her voice lingering on the wind.

    “Until next time, mi vida .”

    Chapter End Notes History check: - We've already heard about Saint Genevieve of Paris but let's discuss a few of Rio's other great admirers. Ennigaldi-Nanna was a Babylonian princess and high priestess of the moon deity , in the ancient Mesopotamian city of Ur, around 530BCE. She is also known as history's first museum curator, having collected and displayed antiquities which she arranged and labelled. In my version, Rio found Ennigaldi's love of histories charming and spent long hours offering her knowledge where she could.

    -Al-Kindi, (c. 801–873 CE) was an Islamic philosopher, mathematician, scientist. Known as the father of cryptanalysis, al-

    Kindi was the first to use frequency analysis to decrypt cipher texts.

    He also introduced Hindu numerals to the Islamic world, which were later developed into the Arabic numerals we use today. He also wrote about the harmony of numbers, medicine, and the nature of the soul and prophetic knowledge. I'm imagining this was a real intellectual connection for Rio and she was deeply intrigued by the workings of al-

    Kindi's ever-inventive mind.

    - Tomoe Gozen is a legendary female samurai from Japan who fought in the Genpei War (1180-1185), known for her exceptional bravery, incredible beauty and her combat skills in archery and swordcraft.

    'Tomoe Gozen' is in fact a title of sorts, Gozen meaning 'young lady'

    and Tomoe referring to the comma-like design printed on her armour.

    There is doubt as to whether she existed as she doesn't appear in any primary accounts of the Genpei war and only appears in the epic "The tale of the Heike" where she is described as: "Tomoe was especially beautiful, with white skin, long hair, and charming features. She was also a remarkably strong archer, and as a swordswoman she was a warrior worth a thousand, ready to confront a demon or a god, mounted or on foot. She handled unbroken horses with superb skill; she rode unscathed down perilous descents."
