    MADISON AVENUE, 2015 Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes MADISON AVENUE, MANHATTAN The office hums like a kicked beehive.

    The sounds of controlled chaos normally thrill Agatha—people working under her thumb, deadlines looming like storm clouds, the occasional barked command punctuating the quiet frenzy.

    She’s never thought of herself as the corporate type but she’s found a nice gig at Crowley Crisis Management. The firm’s Manhattan branch had been all but wiped out in the widespread damage inflicted back during the alien fucking invasion of 2012.

    Yeah, that was a fun new law of the universe to discover.

    When the firm reopened last year, they wanted a fresh slate. New offices, new employees, new ass in the corporate seat. Agatha slipped into the role easily enough and it turns out that hanging on to it involves gossip, backstabbing and the kind of duplicitous bullshit she was made for. Besides, the wardrobe ain’t bad and CEOs don’t do much besides put the fear of God into underlings anyway.

    But today, the office has an edge of distraction, a palpable tension that she can feel the moment she sets foot off the elevator. The sound of murmuring voices, the way clusters of employees lean together like conspirators. Her lips curve into a faint smirk. She’ll let them simmer in their negligence just long enough to relish the moment of catching them out.

    “Miss Harley,” her assistant chirps, falling into step beside her as she strides through the sleek, open-plan workspace. “I, uh—there’s—”

    “Not now, Charlie,” Agatha cuts him off smoothly, already honing in on the disturbance.

    She adjusts her tailored designer blazer as she goes, the sharp staccato of her heels cutting through the office noise. She pauses just before the bullpen, angling her head slightly to eavesdrop.

    “Look, all I’m saying is, you can’t trust people who can toss cars like Frisbees,” comes a voice—a junior associate named Rick, if she remembered correctly. “Today it’s Sokovia, tomorrow it’s Manhattan.

    Again. What happens when they decide they don’t like us anymore?”

    “Oh, please,” snaps someone from the design department. “If it weren’t for them, half the planet would’ve been wiped out by aliens. Or did you sleep through 2012?”

    “And who invited the aliens in the first place? Pretty sure it was one of theirs,” Rick shoots back. “You don’t see regular people dragging apocalyptic wars to Earth.”

    “You’re being dramatic,” another woman counters. “Super-powered people are just that—people. Some good, some bad. That doesn’t mean we should throw the good ones under the bus.”

    “Good ones? Like the guy with the metal suit who literally built a robot army that’s currently trying to kill everyone in Sokovia?”

    Before the argument can spiral further, Agatha rounds the corner, her hands clasped lightly in front of her.

    “Rick,” she purrs, her voice smooth as silk but with the unmistakable snap of authority. “Please, do continue. I’d love to hear more about your insights on the ethics of global security.”

    Rick’s face flushes a deep crimson. “Miss Harley, I, uh—”

    “Don’t stammer, Rick,” she says, her eyes narrowing just slightly. “You’re already wasting time—don’t waste words, too.”

    “Sorry,” he mumbles, shrinking back into the group. The others have gone quiet, their gazes darting between Agatha and the screen like children caught sneaking a peek at a horror movie.

    Agatha casts her gaze to the screens, prepared to find some kind of dull news report but her annoyance swiftly sharpens into curiosity.

    The city seems to be flying.

    Yes, flying.

    The images are shaky and grainy, mostly hand-held phone footage but unmistakable: a city—an entire city—suspended in the air, crumbling edges silhouetted against a smoke-choked sky as it lifts higher and higher into the clouds, like a reverse meteor. The camera quickly zooms in on a familiar figure in red and gold armour streaking through the chaos, energy blasts cutting through the swarm of metallic drones surrounding him.

    Typically, Agatha likes Howard’s kid. He’s got a certain recklessness she appreciates, and she’s deeply enjoyed his antics as Iron Man over the years. But right now, there’s a goddess damned city flying through the air and it seems like it’s being lifted by the same propulsion mechanisms he uses for himself.

    Agatha is no stranger to plans going awry but this is…well. This is certainly something.

    “It’s Sokovia,” someone murmurs, as though Agatha can’t see that for herself.

    “What’s left of it,” Rick mumbles darkly. “Thanks to them.”

    Agatha’s eyebrow arches, but before she can respond, a familiar voice chimes in behind her.

    “You’re late to the party,” Meryl Beckley says, her tone as dry as ever. She leans against a nearby desk, her arms crossed and her ever-present green juice in hand. Her ever-busy CFO and the main competitor for the CEO job. “I don’t attend parties hosted by incompetence,” Agatha replies breezily, though her attention remained fixed on the footage.

    More familiar figures appear on the screen—a soldier in blue, his shield cutting through a line of robots before boomeranging back into his hand. A woman with a blaze of red hair who moves with the devil’s own speed.

    “Though I’m impressed by the staff’s commitment to amateur political analysis.” She adds absently. There’s some kind of light flashing back and forth amongst the different camera angles, crimson and deadly and strangely familiar.

    Is there a witch on that flying death trap?

    Meanwhile, Meryl smirks. “Come on, Agatha. It’s only the end of days.

    Besides, a flying city? Killer robots? This is a PR nightmare waiting to happen.”

    “PR is what we do,” Agatha retorts. “Not rubbernecking. If you’re so concerned, why don’t you whip up a contingency plan for Sokovian refugees while the rest of us get back to earning our keep?”

    Meryl’s smirk widens. “I already did. Sent it to you last night, though I’m guessing it got lost under your wine glass.”

    Her lips twitch, despite herself. Meryl is ambitious, ruthless and ready to tear Agatha apart for what she wants. Agatha is quite a fan of the little cut- throat.

    She turns back to the staff, who still hover by the screens. “Back to work, all of you. Unless Sokovia starts paying our invoices, they’re not our problem.”

    Reluctantly, the group begin to disperse, though a few stole glances at the screen as they return to their desks.

    Agatha rolls out the muscles in her neck sharply, only to feel Meryl step up beside her.

    “You’re distracted,” Meryl says, her voice low enough that no one else could hear.

    “Hardly.”

    “You didn’t tear into them nearly as much as you usually do.”

    “Consider it an early Christmas gift,” Agatha brushes past her, unbothered as ever.

    Meryl falls into step beside her, her expression unreadable. “You know, I’ve been busting my ass trying to keep this company afloat while you sit up there playing Queen of the Castle.”

    “Correction,” Agatha said, glancing at her with a faint smile. “I sit up there because I am Queen of the Castle. And I trust my capable CFO to handle the heavy lifting.”

    “Capable CFO who doesn’t even get a thank-you.”

    “You get a green juice budget,” Agatha quipped as she opened the door to her office. “Consider that your thank-you.”

    Meryl steps forward unexpectedly, pressing closer to Agatha than she has ever let herself before. “I’d rather a thank-you fuck.”

    Agatha can’t contain her delight. “I like a gal who gets straight to the point.”

    Meryl rolls her pretty brown eyes. “Well?” she demands sharply.

    Agatha considers the offer for a moment, enjoying the way Meryl’s attention is dragged back to her lips as she purses them. “Spike my eggnog at the Christmas Party,” she finally says with a wink. “And we’ll see who gets lucky.”

    With that, she steps inside, ready to collapse into her chair and enjoy her peaceful, luxurious office-

    Which is currently the scene of a massacre.

    The armchair in the corner—upholstered in pale cream leather imported from Italy—is overturned, one of its legs bent at an unnatural angle. A shattered lamp lies in a pool of glass on the floor with its shade dangling limply like a decapitated head. Papers that had been meticulously stacked on her desk are now scattered, confetti swirling in the air as if caught in a storm. And at the centre of it all is Rio.

    But not as Agatha has ever seen her.

    She is crouched atop her desk, lithe limbs clad in some sort of shimmering green armour cobbled together from leaves and shadows. A jagged half-

    broken diadem of gold clings to her matted hair, tangled with knots and twigs as though she'd recently fled through a hedge maze. Her feet are bare, filthy, and leaving smudged tracks across the once-pristine surface of the desk.

    She is scratching furiously at the polished wood, gouging deep grooves into it with her nails; they form patterns Agatha can’t quite recognise, shapes she has not seen in a long, long time.

    Agatha blinks, her brain trying to process the surreal tableau. "What in the hell—"

    Rio's head snaps up at the sound of her voice, her wild eyes locking onto Agatha with the intensity of a predator spotting prey.

    "He's coming!" she hisses, her voice a jagged mix of panic and rage. “The Titan is coming. It’s all going to burn…it’s all going to burn…”

    She leaps off the desk with a grace that should have been impossible for someone so dishevelled, landing inches from Agatha with a crash that sends a vase of orchids tumbling to the floor.

    Agatha takes an instinctive step back, slamming the door shut behind her just as Meryl's voice echoes from the hall. "Agatha? What was that crash? Are you alright?"

    "Fine!" Agatha barks, planting her back against the door as Rio darts past her, muttering incoherently. "Just... redecorating!"

    "Redecorating?" Meryl’s tone is sceptical, then pauses, meaningfully.

    "Should I come in and…help?"

    "No!” Agatha shouts, her voice an octave higher than usual. She clears her throat, trying to regain her composure. "I mean, no. Not that kind of redecorating! I’ve got it under control."

    Rio, meanwhile, claws at her own skin, throwing herself over and over against the bookshelves. “Tried to catch me,” she snarls, half to herself. “He tried, he tried…oh, it’s an old spell, he’s clever but I wouldn’t do it…not for him, not for him…”

    Agatha winces. “Lower your voice!” she glances nervously at the door. She can hear Meryl's footsteps lingering just outside.

    "Agatha, you sound... stressed. Should I call someone?" Meryl presses warily.

    "Call someone? Don’t be ridiculous!" Agatha forces a laugh, herding Rio away from the bookshelf as discreetly as possible. "Everything is perfectly fine. Just a little, uh, Feng Shui crisis."

    Meryl hesitates. "I’m opening the door."

    Agatha barely has time to lunge for the lock, twisting it just as Meryl jiggled the handle.

    “Don’t!” Agatha calls out, pressing her weight against the door as Rio suddenly tears a cushion in half, her magic crackling frenetically around her hands as if it cannot follow its mistress’ lunacy either. “I’m... not decent!” A beat of silence. “Not decent?” Meryl sounds intrigued now.

    “Yes!” Agatha shouts, her cheeks flushing against her will. “I spilled coffee on myself. Had to—uh—take off my blazer.”

    On cue, Rio paces back and forth through the debris, pausing to paw through the broken vase of orchids so she can chew on their delicate petals. Agatha wheels around, eyes wide. “Stop that!” she hisses under her breath. Rio pauses mid-chew, tilting her head like a confused animal. Then she resumes gnawing with twice the enthusiasm. “Half of everything!” she spits around the half-chewed petals. “Half of everything and he calls it mercy-” “Agatha?” Meryl’s voice comes again.

    “Still fine!” Agatha yelps, spinning back toward the door. “Just... give me five minutes!”

    But Rio crumples suddenly, clutching at her head as thick blackened blood begins to drip from her ears.

    “The magic is too loud,” she seethes nonsensically. “The stone, it screams at me, Agatha, even from the void…it’s all too loud. He’s coming, Agatha. The Titan is coming, and I can’t— I can’t—”

    Her voice cracks, and for the first time, Agatha catches a glimpse of something beneath the madness. Fear. Real, bone-deep terror. It’s enough to make her pause, her annoyance softening just slightly.

    “Rio,” she says carefully, crouching down a safe distance away. “I need you to calm down. Can you do that for me? Just... take a deep breath.”

    Rio looks at her, her wild eyes narrowing. “Breathe?” she repeats, her voice dripping with disbelief. “Breathe? Do you know what’s coming? Do you understand—”

    She breaks off, clawing at her armour as if trying to rip it off. The jagged crown tumbles from her head and clatters to the floor at Agatha’s feet. She peers at it curiously, and suddenly recognises the symbols delicately etched into the gold as the ones Rio scratched into her desk. She thinks she recognises one or two, something to do with binding spells but it’s not a script she’s familiar with.

    Mor alarmingly, the metal is twisted and snapped, edges matted with locks of dark hair and blood…as if Rio pried it loose from her skull with every ounce of strength she had.

    Something is wrong here. Something big. Agatha pinches the bridge of her nose.

    “Okay,” she mutters to herself. “Plan B.”

    She moves swiftly, grabbing Rio’s shoulders and shaking her. Hard. “Snap out of it!”

    Rio blinks, frantic movements pausing for a moment.

    “Focus,” Agatha commands, her voice sharp. “Tell me what’s going on.

    Who is the Titan? And why are you destroying my office?”

    For a moment, Rio just stares at her, her expression caught somewhere between desperation and confusion. Then, without warning, she flings herself forward, wrapping her arms around Agatha in a bone-crushing hug.

    “I tried.” Rio whispered, her voice trembling. “I tried, but he’s…he’s guarded, protected. I can’t…He’ll destroy everything, Agatha. Everything.” Agatha stiffens, utterly stunned by the sudden embrace. For a moment, she doesn’t move. She can’t…she can’t remember the last time she held Rio like this.

    So, slowly, Agatha raises her arms and returns the hug. If only to stop Rio from breaking anything else, she tells herself.

    “It’s going to be fine,” she murmurs, though she isn’t entirely sure she believes it.

    Rio clings to her like a life raft, like she used to those long nights when the war in her mind couldn’t be silenced. “You don’t understand,” she whispers. “The balance... it’ll…He’s going to burn it all, and I... I can’t stop him.” Agatha pulls back just enough to look her in the eye. “There’s always a way,” she says, her tone firm. “Always.”

    Rio presses her face into Agatha’s shoulder and keens.

    Binding spells etched into crowns, a magical stone screaming from the void, a Titan coming to burn the world. Agatha can’t put the pieces together. Her mind whirls and whirls, looking for answers.

    But her body is painfully aware of Rio’s weight in her arms, of her breath on Agatha’s skin. She can’t think around it.

    “Agatha, are you-”

    With a groan, Agatha gently extricates herself so she can open the door just a crack. “Meryl, desperation isn’t an attractive quality.” She snarls, patience thoroughly run through.

    The mortal narrows her eyes but stalks off without another utterance.

    It’s all for nothing: when Agatha turns around, Rio is gone.

    She wants to slam her head into fucking wall.

    “Meryl!” she barks suddenly, swinging the door wide. “Cancel my morning meetings. I’m headed out.”

    Her CFO looks like she’d rather chew glass than take orders from Agatha.

    “All of them?”

    “Yes,” Agatha waves an imperious hand behind her as she sweeps back toward the elevator. “And get maintenance up here. My office is a disaster.”

    Chapter End Notes Clarity surrounding Thanos and Rio is coming in the next chapter (the Snap chapter, I might also add). Three more to go before the end <3 EVERYWHERE, 2018 Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes EVERYWHERE, ALL AT ONCE He fucking did it.

    She can feel the moment it happens. One moment Rio is gathering souls from the grassy battlefields of Wakanda and the next, the world is howling. Half of every life everywhere all at once, gone. Not dead, just…gone.

    It’s like tearing the heart out of the universe. Every corner of existence screams in a pitch only Rio can hear. He fucking did it.

    She isn’t certain whether the human world feels the blow dealt to it yet. The air itself seems to hold its breath, waiting for the moment to settle into the aftermath. She cannot claim them.

    They are not dead. They are not living. They are simply gone.

    Rio feels her power surge, her heartbeat pulsing like a thousand stars. But there’s nothing to do. There are no souls to guide. Except.

    Death watches a suburban neighbourhood someplace: the households are empty, the cars abandoned in the middle of streets, their engines still running.

    Families left in half-finished conversations.

    Chaos unfurls across the world, but in that instant, a reckless, visceral choice floods her senses.

    It’s made before she has time to consciously choose.

    The universe is screaming at the attack to the sacred balance that has guided her every thought since existence first emerged from the primordial swamp and Rio abandons it without hesitation.

    In a single thought, she suddenly stands before the witch herself.

    The vestiges of the mortal realm flicker around them —people who she doesn’t care about, buildings she doesn’t recognise, objects that have no meaning—caught in the collapsing balance of existence. The only reality is Agatha Harkness’ blinding, devastating soul as Rio pulls them into the space between worlds.

    It is not void, nor is it light. The edges of existence blur into fluid lines, an infinite stretch of night sky curling and undulating like a living thing. There is no up, no down. The stars seem to breathe, distant as forgotten memories of something once seen in a dream.

    “What—what is this?” Agatha’s voice is barely audible in the nothingness. The edges of her form flicker. Her body seems to be partially there and partially lost in the vastness. The rules of mortality don’t seem to apply here. For a moment, they are weightless, a dream caught in the fold of another world.

    “Hello, mi vida.” Rio says helplessly.

    Agatha looks back, bewildered, suspicious but not afraid. Never afraid.

    “Where are we? What did you—?”

    Rio is still, her eyes focused on Agatha, but her mind filled with the scenes of a fractured world, spinning out of control. She can see it all, every moment unfolding in real-time, but she does not let it show. She isn’t sure what to say.

    Death watches the elderly man wandering the London streets, searching for his wife, for his best friend, for the partner he has known for more than half his life. He calls her name over and over and over-

    “It’s not safe out there.” Rio says, and the words feel foreign, as if they belong to someone else. “Not right now. You’ll be gone—if I leave you out there.”

    “Gone?” Agatha’s voice cracks disbelief. “You’re saying I’m... dead? Did you…is that what you’re trying to tell me?”

    “Agatha-”

    “Because if this is how it all ends,” Agatha snarls, “I’m gonna haunt your ass for a fucking eternity!”

    Rio chokes out a laugh. “Not dead. Just... not here. Or there. Not part of what’s happening. You’re safe here.”

    The last time they were so close, Rio had just escaped from a literal death trap.

    Thanos intrigued her from the start. She’s indulged in saints and savants and warriors but she thought he was something new. A crusader who revered her balance, who venerated her work. So different from the constant heartache, the ever-shifting flow of power with a certain witch.

    Rio told herself it was for the better. That this was how she was meant to be treated, even when she began to notice the gleam in Thanos’ eye. Prayers with one hand, plots with the other, she’d told Agatha.

    She had no idea. Not really, not truly, not until she deigned to visit him one day to find his latest gift, a crown fit for a ruler as exquisite as she. She’s never thought herself vain but, in the moment she slipped the diadem across her brow, Rio realised her pride had led her astray.

    “This is for the best, my queen,” his promises were murmured, soft and sweet. “You are not made for the sorrows of existence. You’ll find rest here, I promise.”

    Rio can take any form she requires, living or dead or whatever lies between. But as the crown began to tighten, sinking through her flesh, penetrating down to the bone, she silently swore to herself that the smooth onyx stone in his grasp will not be one of them.

    A death stone, he called it. A safe place for her to rest peacefully. A vessel that could contain the power of Death herself. A weapon he could wield for his own righteous agenda.

    She fought tooth and nail. She ripped apart more of his soldiers, more of his precious lieutenants, than any enemy he had ever faced, ripped that golden death trap out of her skull and ignored the blood and bone that came with it. She should’ve stayed, she should have reaped that bastard’s soul right then and there.

    But no one had ever called her brave.

    No, Rio dissolved into the shadows themselves with only a single thought: Agatha, Agatha, Agatha.

    The witch takes a step back, but the rift doesn’t shift with her—there is no place to go, no walls to press against. “What’s happening out there?”

    Rio hesitates, her own mind swirling with thoughts she cannot express. She can’t explain what she is doing, not really—not when it feels like the universe is collapsing. She cannot tell Agatha the full truth; she doesn’t even know it herself. All she knows is that everything ends. Everything but Agatha.

    “It’s…if you’re here, you’ll be safe.” Rio says cryptically. “I’ll put you back, just…just wait here with me. For a second.”

    Agatha watches her carefully, her gaze piercing through Rio like an old wound. “So…this is the bachelor pad huh? I bet you bring all your hot dates here.”

    Rio’s smile is faint, weary. “Only you.” It’s only ever her.

    Some believe that a terrible plague has wiped out their loved ones. Some think they’ve been abandoned by their gods, by the universe itself. Are they wrong? Has she abandoned them?

    “I’ll return you,” Rio says simply, her eyes averted. “When it’s over. You won’t remember any of this.”

    A flash of regret, mingled with curiosity. “I won’t, will I?”

    “There is no time here.” Rio’s voice drops to a murmur. “Which means there can be no recollection.”

    Agatha steps closer to Rio, her gaze softening. “You’re doing this for me,” she says, as if it’s a new concept. As if she hasn’t been paying attention for the past three centuries.

    “Of course.” Rio says helplessly. Her hand hovers for a moment over Agatha’s, but she cannot bring herself to make contact. “I’d do anything for you.”

    The rift pulses with a gentle current, pushing them further into its depths. Reckless, arrogant, selfish. All the things she has laid at Agatha’s feet in the past. She understands now. She understands how good it feels, how she stands here now with her reckless, arrogant, selfish choice and regrets none of it.

    “Tell me.” Agatha breaks the silence between them, defiant as ever. “Tell me why.”

    “You know why.”

    “Tell me anyway.”

    “I’ve said it every way I know how.”

    Agatha considers this and tilts her head, thoughtfully. “What can I do then? “What do you mean?”

    “I won’t remember this. So what do you want from me, right now?” The witch steps closer, fearlessly reaching for Rio’s hand that still hovers near her own. “What can I do, for you, right now?”

    “Survive.” Rio says simply.

    There’s a woman in the middle of a once-crowded Tokyo intersection and she cannot stop laughing, not even when her laughter turns to screams, turns to sobs, turns to silence.

    But Agatha is not satisfied. “More than that. Come on, there’s gotta be something. What do you want from me? Confessions?” Her lips twitch in a smirk that sparkles right up to her eyes. “Serenades?”

    “It wouldn’t mean anything,” Rio snaps instantly. “If you only say it out of gratitude.”

    “Not gratitude,” Agatha waves a dismissive hand at the very idea. “I’ll tell you the truth, I swear it. What do you want to hear? That I love you? That I’m sorry? That I wish I had treated you kinder?”

    Rio has wanted all of these words, desperately, at one stage or another.

    “You’re only saying these things because we’re standing in oblivion.”

    “This is the only time I can say them.” Agatha shrugs, unbothered. Her witch is surprising her even now. “There are no consequences to saying them here, for me at least. But you’ll have them. Come on, darling. I swore to tell you the truth, so tell me the same: what do you want, Rio?”

    Rio does not need to breathe but even she did, she would not have been able to in that moment. Agatha has stolen even that.

    “Tell me you love me.”

    Agatha’s eyes soften, grey as a gentle spring rain. “I love you.”

    “Tell me you want me.”

    “I want you.” She says earnestly. She pulls Rio’s hand to her chest and there are no heartbeats here but Rio can swear she feels it anyway against her palm. “I have never wanted anyone like I want you.”

    “Tell me,” Rio breathes. “That you forgive me.”

    Agatha doesn’t reply right away. Wordlessly, she slips Rio’s hand to her face, cradles it against her cheek as if it is something precious, something divine. Something holy.

    “Even when I’m not yours,” Agatha murmurs back. “I’m yours. Always.”

    It’s not the same. It’s not the ones she wants but they’re more than what she ever thought she might have again.

    Rio closes her eyes briefly, letting the weight of Agatha’s words settle into her bones.

    “You’re sure I won’t remember?” Agatha presses a soft kiss into Rio’s palm. “Not even a little?”

    “No.” Rio whispers, her voice distant. “But I will.”

    Everything ends. It’s the truth of the universe. Everything ends. Rio cradles Agatha’s face in her hands and tries not to listen the universe screaming out with one voice.

    Everything ends. Everything but her.

    on an unrelated note, there was a version of this chapter where Agatha was Blipped and i gotta say Rio was deeply unhinged about it also, not me remembering how agatha calls rio brave in the titanic chapter im not crying ur crying WESTVIEW, 2023 Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes WESTVIEW, NEW JERSEY The hex encircling this New Jersey suburban nightmare is unlike anything Agatha has ever seen.

    Like something out of a Stephen King novel, the dome of scarlet energy flexes and ripples, taller than she can fathom. She’s never seen a hex cast on this scale. It’s unheard of. She’d have thought it impossible if she weren’t standing right in front of it.

    When she first sensed the explosion of raw, untamed magical energy, Agatha speculated that someone was attempting to access the Vault of the Darkhold. She had fully prepared to hear about some small city going up in flames on the news, all for the ambitions of a mystery witch.

    But this is much, much more interesting.

    “Well, well,” Agatha murmurs, grazing her fingers over the edge of the hex. The sensation is both enticing and disconcerting. Whoever cast this is worth meeting. The precision, the sheer scale—it’s like nothing she’s ever encountered.

    She knocks gently on the hexed dome, testing its boundaries. The energy resists her touch, crackling with a warning.

    Agatha’s eyes glint with intrigue. “Oh, come on,” she murmurs to herself. “Let me play.”

    It’s been some time since she unspooled the power of the Darkhold, but it proves the only way to weave tendrils of her own magic into the fabric of the hex. It’s stubborn but not impenetrable. Nothing ever is.

    With a deliberate flick of her wrist, she tears a small opening in the wall and steps through. The moment she does, reality warps around her.

    “What,” she breathes, utterly bewildered, “the fuck?”

    The world has shifted to grayscale. Actual grayscale, like a vaudeville movie.

    She stands on a perfectly manicured lawn in front of a white picket fence. Somewhere in the distance, a jaunty saxophone melody plays—a tune so aggressively cheerful it borders on sinister.

    Agatha takes a step forward and immediately realizes her boots have been transformed into neat kitten heels to match her carefully pressed gingham dress.

    She scowls, tugging at the stiff fabric with a muttered, “Charming.”

    The neighbourhood is full of people, homes, trees, and pets. But everything feels...off. Plastic. Birds chirp on cue, as if directed by an unseen hand. A dog wags its tail like a metronome. A milkman waves from across the street, his grin unsettlingly wide.

    Agatha doesn’t wave back.

    Her sharp gaze flits around, taking in the surroundings. Each house is cookie-cutter perfection, their mailboxes labelled in cursive: The Smiths, The Harts, The Christies. It’s a grotesque parody of domestic bliss, straight out of a 1960s sitcom. Goddess, she used to hate the one with the witch who did housework.

    And yet, the magic here pulses beneath the surface, barely contained.

    Whoever built this world is not just powerful—they’re unstable.

    “Good morning!” a chipper voice calls out behind her.

    She spins, coming face-to-face with a smiling mailman. His uniform is pristine, his posture impeccable, and his expression utterly vacant.

    “Well, good morning...Stanley,” Agatha replies, forcing a pleasant smile as she reads his nametag.

    He tips his hat, practically glowing with wholesome energy. “Beautiful day for mail, isn’t it?” he chirps.

    “Simply delightful,” Agatha chirps back, her voice dripping with sarcasm. “Tell me, Stanley, is every day this...quaint?”

    Stanley tilts his head, confused, but his grin doesn’t falter. “Oh, every day in Westview is just perfect, ma’am. Can I help you with something?”

    Agatha arches a brow, her expression unreadable. “I certainly hope so,” she replies smoothly. “You wouldn’t happen to know who owns this lovely little...bubble, would you?”

    The man tilts his head, confused. “Bubble?”

    “The little...situation you’ve got going on here.” Agatha gestures vaguely to the world around them. “The magic, the puppets, the...oh, I don’t know, aggressive monochrome aesthetic?”

    He laughs brightly. “I’m afraid I don’t know what you mean. Now, if you’ll excuse me, I’ve got letters to deliver!” He tips his hat again and marches off, whistling a merry tune.

    Agatha watches him for a moment, her curiosity piqued. With a flick of her fingers, she summons him back using a precise thread of dark magic.

    The effect is instantaneous. Stanley freezes mid-step, his cheerful whistle dying in his throat. A shudder ripples through his body as the enchantment binding him loosens.

    His grin fades, replaced by wide, terrified eyes.

    “W-where am I?” he stammers, clutching his mailbag like a lifeline.

    “What...what’s happening?”

    Agatha steps closer, her expression cool but intrigued. “And who might you be, hon?”

    The man blinks, struggling to form words. “Dorian,” he finally whispers.

    “My name is Dorian. I—I don’t know how I got here. One moment I was...I was at work, and the next—” His voice breaks, and he grips his head. “Oh God, it’s like...it’s like I’ve been trapped inside someone else. I can’t stop myself! Please, please, you have to help me—”

    Agatha’s eyes narrow as she listens, her mind churning through the possibilities. Whoever cast this hex hasn’t just conjured a world—they’ve populated it, ensnaring these people into roles, pulling their puppet strings with ease.

    The level of control, the sheer audacity—it’s impressive. And dangerous.

    Dorian’s pleading quickly grows tiresome. “Help me,” he repeats, his voice hoarse with terror. “Please, you’ve got to—”

    Agatha silences him with a wave of her hand, the thread snapping instantly. His body stiffens, and the vacant smile returns to his face as the hex reasserts control. “Beautiful day for mail, isn’t it?” he says, resuming his cheerful tone as if nothing had happened.

    “Fascinating,” Agatha mutters, watching as he walks away. She taps her chin, her thoughts racing. This witch, whoever she is, has created something truly extraordinary. But why? And how?

    Adjusting her gingham dress with a smirk, she sweeps her gaze over the picture-perfect street. Time to meet the lady of the house.

    Wanda Maximoff is so damn weird.

    Her house, for starters, has a laugh track and Wanda doesn’t find that odd. The undercurrent of magic in the air is overwhelming, tangling itself around her senses. It has pulled her to this house, guiding her like a thread leading straight to the spider at the centre of the web.

    The front yard is immaculate, with perfectly trimmed hedges and a rose garden bursting with blooms in shades of red and white. A white swing swayed gently on the porch, as if moved by an invisible breeze.

    When she knocked on the door, Agatha wasn’t sure what to expect. But the chirpy little homemaker who looks ready to offer her a glass of lemonade wasn’t it.

    Not to worry. Agatha has always been quick on her feet when it comes to a nifty lie.

    “Forgive me for not stopping by sooner to welcome you to the block,” She punctuates the introduction with an exaggerated laugh, the kind that’s just a touch too loud. “My mother-in-law was in town, so I wasn’t.”

    Wanda looks rather delighted to have Agnes as her first visitor. The modest but cozy two-story is straight out of a 60s sitcom set, complete with floral wallpaper, the patterned curtains, the spotless counters. It’s a shrine to domesticity and “good old fashioned American values”.

    Agatha wants to puke.

    “So, what’s your name? Where are you from? And most importantly, how’s your bridge game, hon?” she adds with a cheeky smile.

    The audience, whoever they are, loves it.

    “I’m Wanda,” the homemaker introduces cheerily.

    “Wanda. Charmed.” Agatha rolls the name off her tongue. “Golly, you settled in fast! Did you use a moving company?”

    “I sure did, those boxes don’t move themselves.” Wanda says hastily, covering for…what? Her use of magic? As if the giant sitcom fantasy hex wasn’t giving that away?

    Time drags on. They discuss Wanda’s anniversary dinner, tips to seducing her husband. The longer she spends, the more Agatha can sense the gentle current of the hex, filling her mouth with just the right thing to say, just the right quip to laugh at. It’s insidious. It’s genius.

    When Wanda steps out of the kitchen to answer the phone, Agatha takes the opportunity to inspect the room more closely.

    She conjures just a faint pulse of her own magic, and watches as it ripples through the air. The response is immediate—a subtle but unmistakable shift, as if the very walls are alive and aware of her presence. Fascinating. She pushes just a little harder, scraping the surface-

    Nothing prepares her for the grief lurking beneath it.

    Agatha’s breath catches in her throat, unready for the sharp spike of pain delivered right to her chest: Wanda’s pain, it seems. She’s heard of channelling your feelings into your work but goddess three, this isn’t feeling, this is anguish.

    She abandons the search, lets the hex close around her tiny intrusion. The heartache vanishes once more, tucked away like a passive aggressive housewife sweeping dust beneath the rug.

    When Wanda returns a moment later, Agatha can’t help but look at her with fresh eyes. It seems like Little Miss Homemaker made this fantasy world to escape her own misery and frankly, Agatha can sympathise.

    “That was Vision,” Wanda says, smiling as though this is a perfectly normal name for a husband. “He’s running a little late at work, but he’ll be home soon.”

    “Oh, isn’t that always the way?” Agatha says with a laugh. “Men and their work.” She glances at the clock on the wall, then feigns a look of surprise. “Goodness, is that the time? I should get back. I swear, if I don’t cook, Ralph just wastes away! But don’t you worry, Wanda—I just know tonight is going to be perfect.”

    “Thank you so much, Agnes,” Wanda says, her gratitude genuine. “I couldn’t have done it without you.”

    Agatha waves off the thanks and heads back to her own house—or rather, the house she’s claimed for herself. It’s a tidy little place, though she’s already made a few modifications.

    Most notably, the basement, where the former homeowner Ralph is sitting on the cold, stone floor, bound and gagged.

    “Sorry, Ralphie,” Agatha sighs as she descends the stairs. “But I need some peace and quiet while I work, and you’re just too...chatty.”

    Agatha descends the creaky basement stairs with a bowl of soup in her hands, the kind of bland, lukewarm fare she imagines is the least objectionable thing to serve a hostage.

    She’s feeling magnanimous. Since taking over Ralph's house this morning, she's learned quite a bit and Wanda's oblivious attitude to her own creation seems to promise more to come.

    As she steps into the dim, musty space, Ralph glares at her from his corner, his arms still bound and his face set in a mixture of defiance and desperation.

    “Oh, look at that face,” Agatha coos mockingly as she sets the bowl on a rickety table just out of his reach. “Like a little terrier who’s just realized the door to the backyard is locked. Don’t worry, Ralphie, soup’s ready.

    Tomato, if you were wondering."

    Ralph groans, the gag muffling his protests.

    Agatha rolls her eyes and flicks her fingers, releasing the gag.

    “You’re making a big mistake, lady,” Ralph says, his voice rough but steady. “You think you can waltz in here, take over this town with your magic voodoo crap, and no one’s going to notice? The Avengers are already onto you. They’ll be here any minute to take you down.”

    Agatha pauses mid-step, turning back to him with a slow, amused smile.

    “The Avengers, you say? My goodness, Ralphie, you’ve really been paying attention, haven’t you?” She crouches slightly, tilting her head as if speaking to a child. “Tell me, do you happen to have Thor’s phone number? Or maybe you could put in a good word with the big green guy?”

    “I’m serious!” Ralph snaps. “They’re already here! You’re seconds away from getting your ass kicked, lady! The minutethey find out what you’ve done here—”

    “What I’ve done?” Agatha interrupts, her voice silky and laced with false sweetness. “Sweetheart, you’ve got it all wrong. This little wonderland isn’t my doing. I’m just a…concerned bystander, trying to get to the bottom of things.”

    She pauses suddenly, his words sinking in. “What do you mean they’re already here?”

    “The Vision!” he sneers, bravado flickering faintly. “And the Maximoff girl, oh man, they’re on to you!”

    “The Witch.” Agatha hums, pieces fitting into place finally. “I knew I knew that face.”

    The brooding redhead who’s been flitting around with Captain America and his merry band of superpowered boy scouts for the past few years. She’s been keeping an eye on her, mildly curious as to what drives a witch to adopt a pack of over muscled do-gooders as a coven.

    “She’s gonna tear you a new one!” Ralph insists.

    “Hush now, Ralphie,” Agatha rolls her eyes. “She’s not here to rescue you.” He falters, confidence slipping. “What are you talking about?”

    Agatha stops pacing and turns to him with a sly smile. “You mean you don’t recognise your little sitcom hostess upstairs?” She watches the dawning realization flicker across Ralph’s face.

    Ralph’s jaw drops slightly. “No way. They wouldn’t—she couldn’t—”

    “Oh, but she has,” Agatha says, her tone almost gleeful. “She’s the one who turned this charming little town into her personal 60s TV fantasy. And the best part? I don’t think she even knows how she did it.”

    Ralph stares at her, his bravado completely crumbled. “You’re lying. You’re trying to mess with my head.”

    “Why would I bother lying to you, darling?” Agatha says with a laugh.

    “You’re locked in my basement.”

    She moves back toward the stairs, flicking her fingers to silence Ralph again. He struggles against his bindings, but it’s no use. Agatha pauses at the top of the steps, looking down at him with a smirk.

    “Sweet dreams, Ralphie,” she says, her voice dripping with mock cheerfulness. “Don’t wait up for your heroes. They’re not coming.”

    She'll let him out eventually, once she's finished her work. Upstairs, Agatha takes a seat at the makeshift workspace she’s set up at Ralph’s dining table and summons forth the Darkhold for the first time in decades. It crackles eagerly at her command, parchment pages fluttering with temptation.

    But Agatha knows better now. “What are you up to, Wanda?” she murmurs, her fingers tracing the ancient text. “No runes, no spells, no rituals. Why this fantasy, sweetheart?”

    And more importantly, how?

    Chapter End Notes We're nearly full circle now guys! One last chapter coming to you soon.

    THE MORTAL PLANE, 2026 Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes THE MORTAL PLANE The alley is filthy with the stench of urine and despair, cloaked in the kind of darkness that swallows even the bravest beams of streetlight.

    Rio crouches over the body of a young man, his wide, unblinking eyes staring at a patch of graffiti on the brick wall beside him. Blood pools beneath his jacket, its sluggish trickle already darkening, already cooling. “Didn’t even see it coming, huh?” Rio mutters, drawing her reaping dagger from the folds of her coat.

    The man’s spirit stares at his lifeless body, bewildered.

    “What the—who are you?” His voice wavers, the confusion in his words barely masking the panic clawing at his edges. “Wait, wait, is that—am I —”

    “Dead? Yes,” Rio snaps, not bothering to look at him. “Knife to the ribs, punctured lung. Bled out fast. You didn’t even make it to the hospital.”

    She twirls the dagger absently, the blade catching the dim glow of a flickering neon sign above.

    The man gawks at her, translucent hands clutching at the front of his hoodie as if trying to stabilize something that isn’t there anymore. “But I… I didn’t… I just wanted my wallet back. That guy—”

    “Yeah, he didn’t care,” Rio interrupts. “Mugging, bad luck, life’s unfair, yada yada. Let’s get on with it, shall we?”

    She stabs the dagger into the air, the blade slicing through reality itself with a sound like fabric tearing under strain. A jagged portal yawns open, pale light spilling out in tendrils that reach greedily for the man’s soul.

    The man stares inside the void, his expression somewhere between awe and terror. “What is that?”

    “Your ride,” Rio says curtly, gesturing toward the portal with a flick of her wrist. “Hop in.”

    “But you’re not even going to—” he squawks, his voice rising, “—explain what’s happening?! This is kind of a big deal, lady!”

    Rio groans, dragging a hand down her face. “You’re dead. That’s your afterlife. What more do you need, a PowerPoint?”

    A new voice cuts through the gloom, smooth and saccharine with mockery.

    “She’s not usually this grumpy, you know.”

    Rio’s head snaps up, her eyes narrowing as they landed on Agatha, lounging atop a dented dumpster. The ghost sits cross-legged, translucent but glowing with a mysterious vivid light, munching on a bucket of- is that spectral popcorn?!

    Agatha waves a kernel in the air like a conductor’s baton. “Normally, it’s all ‘it’s time to move on, life is fleeting, death is eternal,’ blah blah. This is just lazy.” She pops the kernel into her mouth and chewed dramatically.

    Rio’s scowl deepens. “What the hell are you doing here?”

    Agatha leans back, resting on her elbows, utterly unbothered. “Oh, don’t mind me. Just spectating. Get it? Spectre, spectating.” She waves flirtatiously to the young man, who is staring at her now with wide eyes. “Hi, sweetie.”

    The man blinks. “Wait, I can be a ghost? That’s an option?”

    Rio’s tone sharpens. “Don’t encourage him.”

    Agatha feigns innocence, her lips quirking upward. “What? I’m just presenting alternatives. Ghosthood has its perks, kid. No taxes, no aging, no existential dread—well, okay, some existential dread, but that’s manageable. Plus, you can haunt whoever you want. The guy who stabbed you? Prime haunting material.”

    The man’s expression brightens, his shoulders straightening. “That actually sounds kinda cool.”

    Rio throws up her hands, exasperated. “No. No, it’s not cool. It’s pathetic, and it’s messy. Trust me, you don’t want to spend eternity stuck in the mortal plane, skulking around alleys and old houses. Just go to the afterlife.”

    Agatha snorts. “Ignore her. She’s just bitter because she can’t do anything about me.”

    Rio refuses to look at her but her skeletal teeth grind audibly. “I will find a way.”

    “Sure you will, darling.” Agatha tosses a handful of popcorn at her, snickering at the way the kernels bounce harmlessly off Rio’s coat.

    The young man takes a cautious step toward the portal, then hesitates, glancing between Rio and Agatha. “So, uh… what’s it like? The afterlife?” Rio pinches the bridge of her nose. “It’s fine. Peaceful. Quiet.” She finally shoots a pointed look at Agatha. “Unlike some people.”

    Agatha rolls her eyes, sighing dramatically. “Don’t let her scare you. She’s a big ol’ softie, really. Adorable.”

    Rio fumes. “I’ll give you adorable-” She turns back to the man, the skin melting from her face in an instant to reveal the aged menacing skull beneath, her eyes burning with the fire of a midnight sun: “Get in the portal. Now.”

    The man pales—an impressive feat for someone already dead—and stumbles away toward the void. “Okay, okay! Jesus, just put that thing away!”

    The portal seals shut behind him with a quiet whoosh.

    Rio stands there for a moment, her shoulders tight, her breath sharp. Agatha hops down from the dumpster, her heels clicking soundlessly on the pavement.

    “Do you always have to be such a killjoy?”

    Rio doesn’t turn. “Do you always have to ruin everything?”

    Agatha tilts her head, considering. “Yes.”

    Silence stretches between them, broken only by the distant hum of traffic. It’s been six weeks since the night Agatha left the Road, since she opened herself to Rio’s evergreen shadows and surrendered herself to the power of Death herself. Five and a half weeks since Agatha chose the cowardice of the spectral plane and began her campaign on the last vestiges of Rio’s patience.

    Finally, Rio speaks, her voice quieter now, almost resigned. “Why are you still here, Agatha?”

    Toying with a long strand of silver hair, Agatha shrugs with a faint smirk but with a flicker of something softer beneath it. “Someone’s gotta keep you on your toes.”

    Rio closes her eyes and wonders how she will ever bear the weight of this guilt, this grief, with this spectre of Agatha’s memory hovering at her side. “You should’ve gone.”

    Agatha’s form flickers in the streetlights. “You’d miss me too much.”

    The hospice room smells of synthetic lavender and cleaning chemicals but the bedside is filled with framed photos, cards, flowers: the kind of tchotchkes that accumulate naturally where life had been long and full.

    Rio stands at the foot of the bed, her dark silhouette stark against the soft golden glow of the bedside lamp. The elderly woman lies still, her hands folded neatly over her chest, her eyes open but unseeing. Her spirit, however, is not so serene.

    “This can’t be right” the soul gestures to Rio with a shaky hand. “Aren’t you supposed to be…well, where is our Lord? Why wouldn’t he send an angel? What’s with all the black?”

    Rio, gripping her reaping dagger with the enthusiasm of someone filing taxes, sighs. “You can file a complaint at the help desk but I’m all you get.” The woman squints. “Are you sure? You don’t look very…” She trails off, searching for the word.

    “Holy?” Rio supplies flatly.

    “Well, yes. I was expecting someone a bit more…”

    “Bearded? Wearing a toga?”

    “Yes!”

    “Yeah, I get that a lot.” Rio makes a half-hearted twirl of her dagger, opening a familiar tear in the universe. The portal shimmers dark at first, faint threads of light leaking through the edges like the seams of a poorly stitched wound.

    The elderly woman tilts her head, unimpressed. “That doesn’t look like Heaven.”

    “It’s Heaven-adjacent,” Rio says waspishly.

    The woman takes a step back, her arms crossing stubbornly. “I think I’ll wait for someone else. Maybe there’s been a mix-up. These things happen, you know.”

    Before Rio can respond, there she is. Again.

    “Good for you, Mrs Cheng. It’s good to have standards about these things, you know.”

    Agatha perches on the arm of a faded recliner in the corner of the room.

    “Oh, for the love of—” Rio hisses through gritted teeth.

    “Don’t mind me,” Agatha says, flashing a grin. “I’m just here to observe. Carry on.”

    The woman blinks back, hands searching absently for her glasses on the chain around her neck. “Who are you?”

    “Quality control,” Agatha replies breezily. “We’re doing a spot check.” She leans forward, waving her clipboard conspiratorially. “We’ve had some complaints.”

    Rio shot her a murderous glare. “I have one complaint and she’s a giant pain in my-”

    “Anyway, don’t let the gruff exterior fool you.” Agatha says brightly. “She’s actually one of our more popular employees. Personally, I think it’s because she has a fantastic ass.”

    The elderly woman gasps, clutching her pearls—or at least the ghostly memory of them. “I beg your pardon!”

    Rio’s head snaps around to Agatha, her eyes blazing. “Agatha. Shut. Up.”

    “What?” Agatha asks innocently, shrugging. “I’m just saying what we’re all thinking. Death’s got cake.”

    The soul looks between them, utterly scandalised and mildly intrigued.

    “Well, I… I didn’t think there’d be so many lesbians involved.”

    Agatha waggles her eyebrows. “Death is full of surprises, isn’t it?”

    Rio pinches the bridge of her nose, muttering something inaudible but likely profane. “Ignore her,” she says through clenched teeth. “Let’s get you to the afterlife, shall we?”

    The woman points accusingly at the void. “That does not look like the pearly gates. Where are the angels? The choirs? The… the harps?”

    Rio’s patience, already worn thin, finally snaps. “Fine. You want harps? I’ll give you harps.”

    With an irritated click of her fingers, the portal suddenly blazes with brilliant white light. The sound of a thousand heavenly choirs fills the room, harmonising so perfectly it makes the air hum.

    Mrs Cheng’s expression softens, her shoulders relaxing. “Oh… oh, that’s more like it.” She steps toward the portal, the celestial glow bathing her in a warm, ethereal light. “Thank you, I…I feel much better about this now.”

    She steps through, the portal closing behind her with a soft whoosh, leaving the room dim and quiet once more.

    Agatha’s grin is wide and obnoxious as she scribbles notes on her clipboard. “Bravo. Really. The harps were a nice touch. I’ll give it… oh, I don’t know, six out of ten. Points for flair.”

    Rio turns to her, her hands clenching into fists at her sides. “How much longer are you planning to hang around, Agatha?”

    Agatha shrugs, sliding off the recliner and sauntering toward Rio. “As long as the kid needs me. He’s a slow learner. We’re tackling the ancient art of untying yourself from a pyre this week. Classic Witch Skills 101.”

    “Your kid needs you,” Rio snaps back sharply. “Nicholas is waiting for you.”

    Agatha’s smirk falters, her eyes darting away. “I’m not ready,” she finally says uncomfortably. “Not yet.”

    Rio simply studies her for a long moment, her mask of composure perfect before anyone who was not Agatha Harkness. “Then don’t bother me until you are.”

    “I’m just saying, have you considered rocking a pitchfork and horns?”

    “I’m not the Devil, Agatha. And even if I was, how would that put a newly dead soul at ease?”

    “Who said it was for the dead? I deserve a little eye-candy every now and then.”

    “…so how do I, like, not be here anymore? Because this is feeling a little too toxic situationship for me.”

    “Take the tear in the fabric of time and space to your right, and make a left at the pearly gates, you can’t miss ‘em.”

    “Cool, well, um, great chat, I guess? Hope you guys can work it out.”

    “There’s nothing to work out!”

    “I’m not going through that- that thing! There’s been a mistake!”

    “I could open another one if you want. Something more…subterranean.”

    “Ooh, very ominous, I like it.”

    “I don’t deserve this! I did nothing wrong, I am pillar of the community.” “There are no secrets in death. You know exactly what you did.”

    “Those girls came to me, they wanted it and if they say anything else, they’re lying- AH!”

    “Agatha, for crying out loud.”

    “What? He’s much more portable as a human pretzel. Look, you can just-”

    “Did you just toss a soul through the void?”

    “Nothing but net, baby.”

    “So…how is he doing?”

    “How is who doing?”

    “Who do you think?”

    “I don’t know. How’s your pet abomination going?”

    “He’s fighting with his boyfriend right now. Something about keeping secrets, I don’t know, it’s very Degrassi High.”

    “Oh my god, that reference is older than I am.”

    “Hey, Miss Text-and-Drives, zip it. The adults are talking. So?”

    “So?”

    “Rio.”

    “Why don’t you go find out for yourself, Agatha?”

    Agatha Harkness’ grave blooms with deathless flowers.

    Exotic specimens, unheard of species, buds that should not possibly grow in the suburban conditions of Westview. They do anyway. And they never, ever wilt.

    Rio makes sure of it. The house is boarded up, condemned, since she was last here. The weather has warmed considerably, promising a bright summer season to come. Not that Rio would notice. She hasn’t felt warm since Agatha kissed her.

    She moves quietly around the garden, inspecting their last battlefield for any hint of magical interference. It’s not every day a witch like Agatha Harkness perishes. The earth here holds the memories of that night.

    “Well, aren’t we a Gloomy Gus?”

    “Is there nothing sacred you won’t intrude on?” Rio says wearily, feeling unaccountably drained for the first time in her long, long, long existence. “Hey, it’s my grave.” Agatha bristles, tossing back her long silver hair. “Though I like what you’ve done with it.”

    “Gee,” Rio’s voice is withering. “Thanks.”

    “Oh, come on,” Agatha ribs teasingly. “You’d rather wallow here on your own without me?”

    “Without an anthropomorphic fog here to remind me of the worst night of my life?” Rio shoots back in a deadpan. “You’re right, what am I thinking?” Agatha rolls her eyes. “I’ll have you now, disembodied is very in right now.”

    “I mean, it’s not enough I have to carry the knowledge that you chose some…aberration,” Rio fumes. “Over all those years we had together.”

    “Teen is not an aberration.” Agatha interjects in a rare show of temper. “You got your soul that night, the balance can’t have him too.”

    Rio carries on as if she had not spoken: “What a delight it is to have you here instead, making a mockery of her soul, of her memory!”

    The wind has picked up with every word, swirling dangerously in accordance with her temper.

    But Agatha isn’t one to back down from Rio’s wrath. “You can’t have expected me to go quietly. You know me, Rio.”

    “I never expected you to go!” Rio snarls back.

    The ghost gapes at her. “What happened to ‘everything ends, Agatha’?!

    Isn’t that the motto of your precious sacred order?!”

    “I would’ve broken it for you!” Rio’s voice is rough as a howling wind, as the roar of the ocean waves themselves. “I have broken it! Over and over and over. And you just left me-”

    “Left you?” Agatha cackles out of frustration, not amusement. “I’ve done nothing but cling to your side and all you’ve done is bitch about it!”

    “You’re not her.” Rio finally says, her fury abandoning her just as quickly as it had come. “You’re not my Agatha, you’re…an afterthought. A copy of a copy of a copy. But because you’re here, she can’t be there, she can’t be with Nicky, she can’t forgive me.”

    “I am not,” Agatha begins slowly, dangerously. “Nor have I ever been, an afterthought.”

    “That’s all ghosts are.” Rio insists.

    That only seems to enrage her more. “Could an afterthought touch you?”

    she accuses, reaching for Rio with hands that barely hold her form and yet somehow (somehow) Rio can feel their touch against her skin, cooler than she remembers but firm, willing.

    “Could an afterthought hold you? Kiss you?” Agatha’s ghost presses her lips to Rio’s palm, holding her gaze with open defiance. “I’m not some common poltergeist, Rio. I’m Agatha fucking Harkness and I decide when I leave your side. Not you, not the balance, me.”

    Rio can feel the shrivelled lump of charcoal she calls her heart aching with quiet, cautious desire. She wants to tear it out with her nails, she wants to scream at Agatha’s ghost (at Agatha herself?) not to give her this false hope. “You’re not the same.” Rio murmurs, brow furrowed ever so slightly. “I know you and this…you don’t feel the same.”

    “I’m not the same.” Agatha agrees. “But just because I’m new doesn’t mean I’m not yours.”

    Rio’s hands tighten on Agatha’s and it’s not the same, it’s not the touch she’s etched into the deepest parts of herself but it’s not not Agatha.

    “I told you, sweetheart,” Agatha continues with a smile, her arrogance blinding as a sun. “I warned you I’d haunt your ass for an eternity. You can’t blame me for following through.”

    Rio goes very, very still. “You remember.”

    It shouldn’t be possible. It can’t be possible. Souls cannot recollect the void, not the way Rio can.

    And yet.

    “There are no secrets in death.” Agatha presses her lips to Rio’s cheek ever so gently. “I will face Nicholas when I’m ready. I will train Teen into a witch worthy of my teachings. I will shadow your steps whenever I long to hear you say my name with that delightfully frustrated tone-”

    “Agatha.”

    “That’s the one.” Her smile widens gleefully. “I will unlock every secret this form has to offer me and I will abuse every single one. And throughout ever moment of this afterlife, however long it lasts, I will be yours. Even when I’m not yours.”

    There are ramifications to consider, consequences for what Agatha’s unique spectral existence means for Rio, for the universe, for the natural order of everything. This could upset everything. This could spell disaster. Agatha is one of the most powerful witches to have walked the Earth and she’s training an abomination born from chaos magic, for goddess’ sakes. There’s no precedent for what kind of powers she might still possess in this form, what havoc she could wreak. This calls for careful examination, this calls for swift and decisive action-

    Rio doesn’t care. Rio can’t care. Rio embraces the woman she loves, pulls her until every inch of their bodies press firm against one another.

    Death meets Agatha Harkness in a kiss that eclipses every thought apart from this one: Everything ends but them.

    Chapter End Notes And that's all, folks!
